11.1 Non-pluripolar products

15 Non-pluripolar products

In chapter 9 we multiplied the currents $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1,\ldots ,\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ p$ of locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions by the Bedford–Taylor recursion $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge T=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u_1T)$. The potentials that arise in global geometry are often unbounded: by corollary 14.1.9, a closed positive $(1,1)$-current on a compact Kähler manifold is $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $ with $\theta $ a smooth form and $\varphi $ quasi-plurisubharmonic, and $\varphi =-\infty $ wherever the current has a positive Lelong number, for instance along a hypersurface carrying a current of integration. Indeed, at a point where $\varphi $ is finite, the spherical means of a local potential are bounded below by its value at that point, so the Lelong number vanishes there by (12.5). For such potentials the recursion breaks down, since it multiplies the measure coefficients of a current by an unbounded function. In this chapter we construct a product for arbitrary plurisubharmonic potentials which keeps positivity, closedness, multilinearity and locality, at the price of ignoring everything that happens on pluripolar sets.

The construction truncates the potentials and discards their poles. We replace each $u_ j$ by $\max \{ u_ j,-k\} $, take the Bedford–Taylor product, and keep only its restriction to the set where all $u_ j>-k$, on which the truncated potentials coincide with the original ones. Since bounded products are local in the plurifine topology (theorem 10.4.3), the restrictions obtained for different $k$ are compatible and increase with $k$. Their limit is the non-pluripolar product $\langle \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ p\rangle $, which by construction gives no mass to pluripolar sets. Two phenomena without counterpart in the bounded theory appear. The limit may have infinite mass on compact sets (example 15.1.6). And the mass carried by the poles is lost: in degree one, $\langle \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\rangle $ is the restriction of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ to $\{ u>-\infty \} $ (proposition 15.3.6), so the current of integration at a point of $\mathbb P^1$ has zero non-pluripolar product, and even a potential with zero Lelong numbers and trivial multiplier ideals can have zero non-pluripolar mass (example 15.3.8). When the local masses are finite, the product is a closed positive current (theorem 15.2.1).

On a compact Kähler manifold $X$ the local masses are always finite: every tuple of closed positive $(1,1)$-currents has a non-pluripolar product, and its total mass is bounded by a cohomological quantity (theorem 15.4.3). The total mass is, however, no longer determined by the cohomology classes of the factors; it depends on the singularities of the potentials. The central result of the second half of the chapter is that it is monotone in the singularities: if $\theta $ is a smooth closed real $(1,1)$-form, $\varphi ,\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, and $\psi $ is more singular than $\varphi $ in the sense of definition 14.2.6, then the mass of $\varphi $ is at least that of $\psi $ (theorem 15.7.4). When all potentials are locally bounded outside the closed pole set of a fixed quasi-plurisubharmonic function, this follows from Stokes' formula; arbitrary singularities are reduced to this case by an auxiliary potential on $X\times \mathbb P^ N$, whose total mass is an average of the masses along a segment of potentials. Along the way we prove that non-pluripolar products are lower semicontinuous under convergence in capacity, and continuous when no mass is lost in the limit (theorem 15.6.5).

Monotonicity has several consequences that are used in the rest of the book. Mixed masses do not change under bounded perturbations of the potentials, and in a big class the potentials with minimal singularities have the largest masses; these maximal masses define the positive intersection numbers and the volume of pseudo-effective classes. The full-mass classes and the comparison and domination principles at the end of the chapter are basic tools for the envelopes of chapter 16, and the fiber formula of section 15.5 is used for relative energy in chapter 17.

Historical remarks and references for this chapter are collected in section B.15.

15.1 Truncation and plurifine locality

Let $X$ be a complex manifold of dimension $n\geq 1$, let $U\subset X$ be a coordinate domain, and fix an integer $1\leq p\leq n$. Let $u_1,\ldots ,u_ p$ be plurisubharmonic functions on $U$, and recall that $\{ u_ j=-\infty \} $ is the pole set of $u_ j$. For each integer $k\geq 1$ put

\[ \begin{gathered} u_{j,k}=\max \{ u_ j,-k\} ,\qquad O_ k=\bigcap _{j=1}^ p\{ u_ j>-k\} ,\\ S_ k=\mathbf1_{O_ k}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{1,k}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{p,k}. \end{gathered} \]

The functions $u_{j,k}$ are locally bounded and plurisubharmonic, so the product in $S_ k$ is the Bedford–Taylor product of definition 9.1.1, and multiplication by $\mathbf1_{O_ k}$ restricts its coefficient measures to $O_ k$, where every $u_{j,k}$ coincides with $u_ j$. By proposition 10.4.4, the trace measures of the currents $S_ k$ increase with $k$ to a Borel measure $\mu $ for which every pluripolar subset of $U$ has outer measure zero, but $\mu $ may be infinite on compact sets (example 15.1.6 below).

The set $O_ k$ is Borel, because plurisubharmonic functions are upper semicontinuous, and it is plurifine open, because they are plurifine continuous; it need not be open in the ordinary topology. For instance, the countable set $A$ of points with rational coordinates in the unit disc $\Delta \subset \mathbb C$ is polar, each point $a$ being the pole of $\log |z-a|$, so by proposition 4.2.3 there is a finite positive measure $\nu $ with compact support such that the fundamental potential $E_2*\nu $ equals $-\infty $ on $A$. The function $u_1(z)=(E_2*\nu )(z_1)$ is plurisubharmonic on $\mathbb C^ n$ by proposition 3.2.1, proposition 8.2.3, and its pole set contains the dense subset $A\times \mathbb C^{n-1}$ of $\Delta \times \mathbb C^{n-1}$. For $p=1$ and large $k$, the set $O_ k\cap (\Delta \times \mathbb C^{n-1})$ is nonempty, because $E_2*\nu $ is locally integrable, but it has empty interior, so $O_ k$ is not open. We shall often restrict positive currents to Borel sets such as $O_ k$, and we first note that this preserves positivity.

Lemma 15.1.1. Let $R$ be a positive current of bidegree $(p,p)$ on an open set $W\subset \mathbb C^ n$, and let $E\subset W$ be a Borel set. Then the current $\mathbf1_ ER$, obtained by multiplying the coefficient measures of $R$ by $\mathbf1_ E$, is positive. If $R$ is strongly positive, that is, $R(\varphi )\geq 0$ for every test form $\varphi $ that is weakly positive at every point, then so is $\mathbf1_ ER$.

Proof. By theorem 7.2.5, $R$ has measure coefficients. Let $\varphi $ be a test form of bidegree $(n-p,n-p)$ that is strongly positive, respectively weakly positive, at every point. The complex measure $R\wedge \varphi $, whose integral against a test function $\chi $ is $R(\chi \varphi )$, defines a positive distribution, because $\chi \varphi $ has the same positivity as $\varphi $ whenever $\chi \geq 0$; by the uniqueness in theorem 1.4.5, it is a positive measure. Hence $(\mathbf1_ ER)(\varphi )=(R\wedge \varphi )(E)\geq 0$. $\square$

The next lemma shows that local finiteness of $\mu $ is exactly what is needed for the currents $S_ k$ to converge.

Lemma 15.1.2. For $l\geq k$ the current $S_ l-S_ k$ is positive, and $\mathbf1_{O_ k}S_ l=S_ k$. The currents $S_ k$ converge weakly to a positive current $S$ if and only if

15.1
\begin{equation} \sup _ k\int _ K S_ k\wedge \beta ^{n-p}<\infty \quad \text{for every compact set }K\subset U, \end{equation}

where $\beta $ is the Euclidean Kähler form (7.4) of the coordinates. In that case $\mathbf1_{O_ k}S=S_ k$ for every $k$, the coefficient measures of $S_ k$ converge to those of $S$ in total variation on compact subsets of $U$, and the trace measure of $S$ is the measure $\mu $ above. Every pluripolar subset of $U$ has outer measure zero for $\mu $ and for the total variations of the coefficient measures of $S$; consequently every plurifine open subset of $U$ is measurable for the completions of these measures. Moreover, if $E_ k\subset O_ k$ are Borel sets such that $\mathbf1_{E_ k}\to 1$ almost everywhere for the trace measure of $S$, then the coefficient measures of $\mathbf1_{E_ k}S_ k$ converge to those of $S$ when integrated against any bounded Borel function with compact support in $U$. This is the case, in particular, if the sets $E_ k$ increase and $U\setminus \bigcup _ kE_ k$ is pluripolar.

Proof. Let $l\geq k$. By proposition 10.4.4, the level-$l$ and level-$k$ Bedford–Taylor products have the same restriction to $O_ k$; since $O_ k\subset O_ l$, this is the identity $\mathbf1_{O_ k}S_ l=S_ k$. Consequently $S_ l-S_ k=\mathbf1_{O_ l\setminus O_ k}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{1,l}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{p,l}$, and this current is positive by lemma 15.1.1.

Suppose that (15.1) holds. The trace measures $S_ k\wedge \beta ^{n-p}$ increase with $k$ and, by (15.1), converge to a locally finite measure. Hence the trace mass of the positive current $S_ l-S_ k$ on a compact set $K$ tends to zero as $k,l\to \infty $. By theorem 7.2.5, the total variation on $K$ of each coefficient measure of $S_ l-S_ k$ is at most a fixed multiple of this trace mass. Therefore each coefficient measure of $S_ k$ is a Cauchy sequence in total variation on compact sets, and the limits are the coefficients of a current $S$, which is positive as a weak limit of positive currents. Conversely, suppose that $S_ k\to S$ weakly for a positive current $S$. For every smooth compactly supported $\chi \geq 0$, the numbers $\int \chi S_ k\wedge \beta ^{n-p}$ increase with $k$ and converge to $\int \chi S\wedge \beta ^{n-p}$, which therefore bounds them; choosing $\chi =1$ on $K$ gives (15.1).

Assume now that (15.1) holds. Letting $l\to \infty $ in $\mathbf1_{O_ k}S_ l=S_ k$ gives $\mathbf1_{O_ k}S=S_ k$. By the convergence in total variation, the trace measure of $S$ is the increasing limit of the trace measures of the $S_ k$, which is $\mu $. By proposition 10.4.4, every pluripolar subset of $U$ has outer $\mu $-measure zero. The total variation of each coefficient measure of $S$ is at most $C_{n,p}\mu $ (theorem 7.2.5), so pluripolar sets have outer measure zero for these total variations as well, and theorem 10.4.1(2), applied to $\mu $ and to these total variations, shows that plurifine open subsets of $U$ are measurable for their completions. In particular $S$ vanishes on $U\setminus \bigcup _ kO_ k$, the union of the pole sets of the $u_ j$, which is a pluripolar Borel set.

Finally, let $E_ k$ be as in the statement. Since $E_ k\subset O_ k$, we have $\mathbf1_{E_ k}S_ k=\mathbf1_{E_ k}S$. By theorem 7.2.5, the total variation of each coefficient measure of $S$ is locally finite and absolutely continuous with respect to the trace measure. Hence $\mathbf1_{E_ k}\to 1$ almost everywhere for each of these variations, and dominated convergence proves the assertion. If the $E_ k$ increase and $U\setminus \bigcup _ kE_ k$ is pluripolar, then $\mathbf1_{E_ k}\to 1$ outside this pluripolar Borel set, which $S$ does not charge. $\square$

Definition 15.1.3. If (15.1) holds, we say that the non-pluripolar product of $u_1,\ldots ,u_ p$ is locally finite, and we call the limit $S$ of lemma 15.1.2 the non-pluripolar product

\[ \langle \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ p\rangle =S. \]

The angle brackets distinguish this construction from a product that retains mass on pluripolar sets. The empty product ($p=0$) is $1$.

If all $u_ j$ are locally bounded, then every relatively compact open subset of $U$ is contained in $O_ k$ for large $k$. In this case the non-pluripolar product is locally finite and coincides with the Bedford–Taylor product, and we omit the brackets.

Proposition 15.1.4. Every locally finite non-pluripolar product $S$ is strongly positive: $S(\varphi )\geq 0$ for every compactly supported smooth weakly positive form $\varphi $ of bidegree $(n-p,n-p)$.

Proof. First let all potentials be locally bounded. Near each point they are limits of decreasing sequences of smooth plurisubharmonic functions, for instance of their mollifications (proposition 8.2.2(2)). The complex Hessians of these functions are semipositive $(1,1)$-forms (proposition 8.2.2(1)), hence strongly positive, since the two notions of positivity coincide in bidegree $(1,1)$; by proposition 7.2.1, their exterior products are strongly positive, and the product of such a product with a weakly positive form of complementary bidegree is a nonnegative multiple of the volume form. Thus the smooth products pair nonnegatively with every smooth compactly supported weakly positive form. By theorem 9.2.5, the Bedford–Taylor product is their weak limit, and therefore it pairs nonnegatively with these forms as well.

By lemma 15.1.1, applied to the bounded products in the definition of $S_ k$, every $S_ k$ is strongly positive. Since $S_ k\to S$ weakly by lemma 15.1.2, so is $S$. $\square$

Proposition 15.1.5. Let $u_1,\ldots ,u_ p$ and $v_1,\ldots ,v_ p$ be plurisubharmonic on $U$. If $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ j$ for every $j$, then the non-pluripolar product of the $u_ j$ is locally finite if and only if that of the $v_ j$ is, and then the two products are equal; thus local finiteness and the product depend only on the currents $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j$. The product is symmetric in its factors and local in the plurifine topology: if $u_ j=v_ j$ on a plurifine open set $O$ for every $j$ and both products are locally finite, then they have the same restriction to $O$. Finally, the product is multilinear: if $u_1'$ is plurisubharmonic on $U$, then the product of $u_1+u_1',u_2,\ldots ,u_ p$ is locally finite if and only if the products of $u_1,u_2,\ldots ,u_ p$ and of $u_1',u_2,\ldots ,u_ p$ are, and in that case

\[ \begin{aligned} \langle \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u_1+u_1')\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_2\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ p\rangle & =\langle \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_2\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ p\rangle \\ & \quad +\langle \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1'\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_2\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ p\rangle . \end{aligned} \]

Similarly, for real $c>0$ the product of $cu_1,u_2,\ldots ,u_ p$ is locally finite if and only if that of $u_1,u_2,\ldots ,u_ p$ is, and then it is $c$ times the latter.

Proof. In this proof we write $P^{(m)}(w_1,\ldots ,w_ p)=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\max \{ w_1,-m\} \wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\max \{ w_ p,-m\} $ for plurisubharmonic $w_1,\ldots ,w_ p$ and an integer $m\geq 1$.

Symmetry and locality. Each truncated product $S_ k$ is symmetric in its factors, hence so is the limit. Suppose that $u_ j=v_ j$ on the plurifine open set $O$ for every $j$, and that both products are locally finite. By lemma 15.1.2, plurifine open subsets of $U$ are measurable for the completions of the total variations of the coefficient measures of both products, so the restrictions of the products to $O$ and to the plurifine open sets $W_ k=O\cap \bigcap _ j\{ u_ j>-k\} =O\cap \bigcap _ j\{ v_ j>-k\} $ are well defined; the sets $W_ k$ increase with $k$. On $W_ k$ the truncations $\max \{ u_ j,-k\} $ and $\max \{ v_ j,-k\} $ coincide, so the two level-$k$ products agree on $W_ k$ by theorem 10.4.3. Since $W_ k$ is contained in the level-$k$ sets of both tuples, lemma 15.1.2 shows that the two non-pluripolar products also agree on $W_ k$. The union of the $W_ k$ is $O$ minus the union of the pole sets of the $u_ j$, a pluripolar set, which has outer measure zero for these total variations by the same lemma. Letting $k\to \infty $, we conclude that the two products have the same restriction to $O$.

Dependence on the currents $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j$. Suppose that $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ j$ for every $j$. The distribution $v_ j-u_ j$ then has zero Laplacian, so by Weyl's lemma (theorem 2.1.4) it is a smooth function $w_ j$, and its complex Hessian vanishes: $w_ j$ is pluriharmonic. The plurisubharmonic functions $v_ j$ and $u_ j+w_ j$ agree almost everywhere, hence everywhere, since both are subharmonic (theorem 8.2.1, theorem 3.2.2). Fix a relatively compact open set $V\subset U$ and an integer $C$ such that $|w_ j|\leq C$ on $V$ for all $j$. Write $O_ k(u),S_ k(u)$ and $O_ k(v),S_ k(v)$ for the level sets and truncated products of the two tuples. On $V\cap O_ k(v)$ we have $u_ j=v_ j-w_ j>-k-C$, hence

\[ \max \{ v_ j,-k\} =v_ j=\max \{ u_ j,-k-C\} +w_ j . \]

The functions $\max \{ v_ j,-k\} $ and $\max \{ u_ j,-k-C\} +w_ j$ are bounded and plurisubharmonic on $V$ and coincide on $V\cap O_ k(v)$. Moreover, adding the pluriharmonic function $w_ j$ to a factor does not change Bedford–Taylor products. Indeed, by additivity (proposition 9.1.2) and symmetry (theorem 9.2.5), the difference is a sum of products having $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_ j$ as first factor; by proposition 9.1.2(1), such a product is the exterior product of the remaining closed positive current with the smooth form $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_ j=0$. As $V\cap O_ k(v)\subset O_{k+C}(u)$, plurifine locality gives on $V$

\[ S_ k(v)=\mathbf1_{O_ k(v)}P^{(k+C)}(u_1,\ldots ,u_ p)=\mathbf1_{O_ k(v)}S_{k+C}(u)\leq S_{k+C}(u). \]

Thus (15.1) for the $u_ j$ implies (15.1) for the $v_ j$ on compact subsets of $V$, and exchanging the two tuples (that is, replacing $w_ j$ by $-w_ j$) gives the converse. If both products are locally finite, the display and lemma 15.1.2 give $\mathbf1_{O_ k(v)}\langle \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ p\rangle =\mathbf1_{O_ k(v)}\langle \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ p\rangle $ on $V$ for every $k$. The sets $O_ k(v)$ exhaust the complement of the union of the pole sets, which are the same for the two tuples and carry no mass for either product. Hence the two products coincide.

Additivity. Let $u_1'$ be plurisubharmonic and put

\[ E_ k=\{ u_1>-k\} \cap \{ u_1'>-k\} \cap \bigcap _{j=2}^ p\{ u_ j>-k\} . \]

These plurifine open sets increase with $k$ and exhaust the complement of the union of the pole sets of $u_1,u_1',u_2,\ldots ,u_ p$. On $E_ k$ we have $\max \{ u_1+u_1',-2k\} =u_1+u_1'=\max \{ u_1,-k\} +\max \{ u_1',-k\} $ and $\max \{ u_ j,-2k\} =\max \{ u_ j,-k\} $ for $j\geq 2$. Plurifine locality and the additivity of bounded products (proposition 9.1.2) therefore give

15.4
\begin{equation} \begin{aligned} \mathbf1_{E_ k}P^{(2k)}(u_1+u_1',u_2,\ldots ,u_ p) & =\mathbf1_{E_ k}P^{(k)}(u_1,u_2,\ldots ,u_ p)\\ & \quad +\mathbf1_{E_ k}P^{(k)}(u_1',u_2,\ldots ,u_ p). \end{aligned} \end{equation}

Before local finiteness is known we argue with trace measures. By proposition 10.4.4, for each of the three tuples the trace measures of the truncated products increase to a Borel measure, possibly infinite on compact sets, for which every pluripolar set has outer measure zero; for a locally finite product it is the trace measure of the product. The set $E_ k$ is contained in the level-$2k$ set of the first tuple and in the level-$k$ sets of the other two, so by the identity $\mathbf1_{O_ k}S_ l=S_ k$ of lemma 15.1.2, the restriction of each limit measure to $E_ k$ is the restriction of the corresponding trace measure in (15.4). Hence, on $E_ k$, the limit measure of the first tuple is the sum of those of the other two. Letting $k\to \infty $, monotone convergence gives this equality on every Borel subset of $\bigcup _ kE_ k$, and hence everywhere, because the complement of $\bigcup _ kE_ k$ is pluripolar. Since all three measures are positive, the first is locally finite if and only if the other two are; this is the asserted equivalence of local finiteness.

When the products are locally finite, (15.4) and lemma 15.1.2 give the additivity identity on each $E_ k$, and letting $k\to \infty $ proves it. For $c>0$ the same argument applies to the sets $\{ u_1>-k\} \cap \{ cu_1>-k\} \cap \bigcap _{j\geq 2}\{ u_ j>-k\} $, on which $\max \{ cu_1,-k\} =c\max \{ u_1,-k\} $, and proves the homogeneity assertion. $\square$

Let now $T_1,\ldots ,T_ p$ be closed positive $(1,1)$-currents on the complex manifold $X$. By theorem 7.3.2, every point of $X$ has a coordinate neighborhood on which $T_ j=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j$ with $u_ j$ plurisubharmonic. By proposition 15.1.5, local finiteness and the local non-pluripolar product do not depend on the choice of these potentials, and the construction is compatible with restriction to smaller open sets.

They do not depend on the coordinates either. Indeed, let $F$ be a biholomorphism between coordinate domains. Then $\max \{ u_ j,-k\} \circ F=\max \{ u_ j\circ F,-k\} $ and $O_ k(u\circ F)=F^{-1}(O_ k(u))$. Since $F$ is a submersion, the pullback $F^*$ of proposition 7.5.2 satisfies $F^*(\mathbf1_ ES)=\mathbf1_{F^{-1}(E)}F^*S$ for every current $S$ of order zero and every Borel set $E$ (corollary 7.5.3(2)), so proposition 9.1.2(5) gives $F^*S_ k(u)=S_ k(u\circ F)$ for the truncated products. To compare trace measures, note that if $\alpha $ and $\alpha '$ are smooth semipositive $(1,1)$-forms on an open set with $\alpha '\leq c\alpha $, then

\[ (c\alpha )^{n-p}-\alpha '^{n-p}=(c\alpha -\alpha ')\wedge \sum _{i=0}^{n-p-1}(c\alpha )^ i\wedge \alpha '^{n-p-1-i} \]

is a sum of products of semipositive $(1,1)$-forms, hence strongly positive (proposition 7.2.1). Since the product of a positive current with a smooth strongly positive form of complementary bidegree is a positive measure, it follows that

15.7
\begin{equation} R\wedge \alpha '^{n-p}\leq c^{n-p}R\wedge \alpha ^{n-p} \end{equation}

on that open set, for every positive current $R$ of bidegree $(p,p)$. On a neighborhood of a compact set, the Euclidean forms $\beta $ and $\beta '$ of the two coordinate systems satisfy $\beta '\leq c\beta $ for some $c$ (lemma 13.1.1), so by (15.7) the trace measures of a positive current in the two coordinate systems are comparable on compact sets, and local finiteness does not depend on the coordinates. Since $F^*$ is weakly continuous, $F^*\langle \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ p\rangle =\langle \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u_1\circ F)\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u_ p\circ F)\rangle $.

We say that the non-pluripolar product of $T_1,\ldots ,T_ p$ is locally finite if this holds near every point; the local products then glue to a positive current $\langle T_1\wedge \cdots \wedge T_ p\rangle $ on $X$, which is closed by theorem 15.2.1 below. When $X$ is compact we also say simply that the product is finite. Repeated factors are written as powers, as in $\langle T^ j\wedge S^{r-j}\rangle $, and for $T_ j=\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ j$ we also write $\langle (\theta _1+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1)\wedge \cdots \wedge (\theta _ p+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p)\rangle $. The assertions of proposition 15.1.5 carry over to these products, with multilinearity in the factors $T_ j$.

Example 15.1.6. Let $U=\{ (x,y)\in \mathbb C^2:|x|^2<1/2,\ |y|<\mathrm e^{-1}\} $, put $L=-\log |y|$, and define

\[ u(x,y)=(|x|^2-1)\sqrt L\quad (y\ne 0),\qquad u(x,0)=-\infty . \]

On $U\setminus \{ y=0\} $ we have $L>1$, the function $u$ is smooth, and its complex second derivatives are

\[ u_{x\bar x}=\sqrt L,\qquad u_{y\bar y}=\frac{1-|x|^2}{16|y|^2L^{3/2}},\qquad u_{x\bar y}=-\frac{\bar x}{4\bar y\sqrt L},\qquad \det (u_{j\bar k})=\frac{1-2|x|^2}{16|y|^2L}. \]

Since $u_{x\bar x}>0$ and $\det (u_{j\bar k})>0$, the complex Hessian is positive definite, so $u$ is plurisubharmonic off the axis $\{ y=0\} $ (proposition 8.2.2(1)). Moreover $u<-\sqrt L/2$ is bounded above and tends to $-\infty $ at the axis. By theorem 8.4.1, the plurisubharmonic extension across the axis takes the value $\varlimsup u=-\infty $ there, which is the displayed value; hence $u$ is plurisubharmonic on $U$.

Off the axis, $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^2$ is the smooth measure with density a fixed positive multiple of $\det (u_{j\bar k})$, by (9.2). The sets $O_ k=\{ u>-k\} $ are open, since $u$ is continuous as an extended-real function, and they increase to $U\setminus \{ y=0\} $; on $O_ k$ the truncation $\max \{ u,-k\} $ equals $u$, so $S_ k=\mathbf1_{O_ k}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^2$. Take $K=\{ |x|^2\leq 1/4,\ |y|\leq r\} $ with $0<r<\mathrm e^{-1}$. On $K$ we have $1-2|x|^2\geq 1/2$, and polar coordinates in $y$ give

\[ \int _{|y|\leq r}\frac{\mathrm dA(y)}{|y|^2L}=2\pi \int _0^ r\frac{\mathrm dt}{t(-\log t)}=\infty . \]

Hence $\sup _ k\int _ KS_ k=\int _{K\setminus \{ y=0\} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^2=\infty $, and (15.1) fails. Thus the non-pluripolar product of an arbitrary plurisubharmonic function need not be locally finite.

15.2 Closedness and cutoff gradients

The indicators $\mathbf1_{O_ k}$ cannot be differentiated, so closedness of the non-pluripolar product does not follow directly from closedness of the truncated products. We replace the indicators by bounded plurisubharmonic cutoff functions and control the resulting error with the gradient estimates of section 9.13. Here $\mathrm d^{\mathrm c}$ is the real operator of (7.6), so that $\mathrm d\mathrm d^{\mathrm c}=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$.

Theorem 15.2.1. Every locally finite non-pluripolar product is closed.

Proof. Closedness is a local property, so we may shrink $U$ and subtract constants to assume that $u_ j\leq 0$ for every $j$. If $p=n$ the product has top degree and is closed for degree reasons, so let $p<n$. Fix a constant strongly positive $(n-p-1,n-p-1)$-form $\gamma $ and put

\[ \Theta _ k=\gamma \wedge \bigwedge _ j\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{j,k},\qquad S_\gamma =\gamma \wedge \Bigl \langle \bigwedge _ j\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j\Bigr \rangle . \]

The $\Theta _ k$ are closed positive currents of bidimension $(1,1)$. By lemma 15.1.2, $\mathbf1_{O_ k}\Theta _ k=\mathbf1_{O_ k}S_\gamma $ increases to $S_\gamma $, and in particular these restrictions have uniformly bounded mass on compact sets. We shall prove that $\mathrm dS_\gamma =0$.

Cutoff functions. Put $u=\sum _ ju_ j\leq 0$ and $w_ k=\max \{ 1+u/k,0\} $. By proposition 8.2.5, $w_ k$ is plurisubharmonic, and $0\leq w_ k\leq 1$. As $k\to \infty $, $w_ k$ increases to $1$ at every point where $u>-\infty $, because $u/k$ increases to $0$ there, while $w_ k=0$ on the pole set of $u$. Moreover $w_ k=0$ outside $O_ k$: if $u_ j\leq -k$ for some $j$, then $u\leq u_ j\leq -k$, since all $u_ i\leq 0$. Choose a smooth function $\zeta $ on $\mathbb R$ with $0\leq \zeta \leq 1$, $\zeta =0$ on $(-\infty ,1/2]$ and $\zeta =1$ near $1$. Then $\zeta (w_ k)$ and $\zeta '(w_ k)$ vanish outside $O_ k$, so that $\zeta (w_ k)\Theta _ k=\zeta (w_ k)S_\gamma $. At every point where $u>-\infty $ we have $\zeta (w_ k)\to 1$ and $\zeta '(w_ k)\to 0$, and $S_\gamma $ gives the pole set of $u$ zero mass. Dominated convergence therefore gives

\[ \zeta (w_ k)\Theta _ k\longrightarrow S_\gamma , \qquad \zeta '(w_ k)\longrightarrow 0 \quad S_\gamma \text{-almost everywhere}. \]

Gradient bound. Let $\eta $ be a compactly supported smooth real one-form, and let $\tau \geq 0$ be a smooth compactly supported function equal to one near $\operatorname{Supp}\eta $. By (9.84), the gradient measure of $w_ k$ with respect to $\Theta _ k$ is $E_{\Theta _ k}(w_ k)=\frac12\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(w_ k^2\Theta _ k)-w_ k\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_ k\wedge \Theta _ k$, and the last term is a positive measure because $w_ k\geq 0$. Hence

\[ \int \tau E_{\Theta _ k}(w_ k) \leq \tfrac 12\int w_ k^2\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\tau \wedge \Theta _ k \leq C\int _{\operatorname{Supp}\tau } \mathbf1_{O_ k}\Theta _ k\wedge \beta \leq C'. \]

The second inequality uses $w_ k=0$ outside $O_ k$, $w_ k\leq 1$, and the bound of the coefficients of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\tau \wedge \Theta _ k$ by a constant times the trace measure (theorem 7.2.5). The constant $C'$ is independent of $k$ because $\mathbf1_{O_ k}\Theta _ k\leq S_\gamma $ and $S_\gamma $ is locally finite.

Conclusion. By the chain rule (9.85), $\mathrm d(\zeta (w_ k)\Theta _ k)=\zeta '(w_ k)\, \mathrm dw_ k\wedge \Theta _ k$. We apply (9.86) with $a=\zeta '(w_ k)$. The constant $C_\eta $ there depends only on $\eta $, so it is the same for every $k$. The first factor on the right is bounded by the gradient bound, and the second tends to zero, since

\[ |\zeta '(w_ k)|^2\Theta _ k\wedge \beta =|\zeta '(w_ k)|^2\mathbf1_{O_ k}\Theta _ k\wedge \beta \leq |\zeta '(w_ k)|^2 S_\gamma \wedge \beta \]

and the right side tends to zero on $\operatorname{Supp}\eta $ by dominated convergence. Hence the pairing of $\mathrm d(\zeta (w_ k)\Theta _ k)$ with $\eta $ tends to zero. Since $\zeta (w_ k)\Theta _ k\to S_\gamma $, the pairing of $\mathrm dS_\gamma $ with $\eta $ vanishes, so $\mathrm dS_\gamma =0$.

Finally, $\gamma \wedge \mathrm d\langle \bigwedge _ j\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j\rangle =\mathrm dS_\gamma =0$ for every constant strongly positive $\gamma $. These forms span the constant real $(n-p-1,n-p-1)$-forms (proposition 7.2.1), and their products with smooth functions and the coordinate one-forms $\mathrm dz_ i,\mathrm d\bar z_ i$ span the test forms of the two bidegrees against which $\mathrm d\langle \bigwedge _ j\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j\rangle $ is evaluated. Therefore the non-pluripolar product is closed. $\square$

15.3 Bounded factors and the first-degree product

Let $v$ be locally bounded and plurisubharmonic, and let $S=\langle \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_2\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ p\rangle $ be locally finite. Since $S$ is closed and positive, the recursion of definition 9.1.1 defines $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge S$, and we expect this to be the non-pluripolar product of $v,u_2,\ldots ,u_ p$. To prove it we must know that $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge S$, like $S$, gives no mass to the pole sets of $u_2,\ldots ,u_ p$. This follows from a weighted form of the Chern–Levine–Nirenberg inequality. We then identify the non-pluripolar product in degree one, which requires a measurable form of Fubini's theorem for Bedford–Taylor products.

Lemma 15.3.1. Let $T$ be a closed positive current of bidimension $(r,r)$, $r\geq 1$, on a coordinate domain $U\subset \mathbb C^ n$, and let $K,L$ be compact sets with $K\Subset \operatorname{Int}L\subset L\Subset U$. Let $V\leq 0$ be plurisubharmonic on $U$, and let $v$ be locally bounded and plurisubharmonic on $U$. Then

\[ \int _ K(-V)\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge T\wedge \beta ^{r-1} \leq C_{K,L}\| v\| _{L^\infty (L)} \int _ L(-V)T\wedge \beta ^ r, \]

where the right side may be infinite and the constant $C_{K,L}$ depends only on $K$, $L$ and the dimension, and not on $T$, $V$ or $v$.

Proof. We treat $r\geq 2$ and smooth $V$ by an integration by parts on small balls, then general $V$ by monotone approximation, and reduce $r=1$ to $r=2$ by adding a variable.

Step 1: $r\geq 2$ and $V$ smooth. Here $\| v\| _{L^\infty (L)}=\sup _ L|v|$, as in theorem 9.1.3. If it vanishes, then $v=0$ on $L$, so $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v=0$ on $\operatorname{Int}L\supset K$, and the left side vanishes. Otherwise both sides are homogeneous of degree one in $v$, and the left side does not change when a constant is added to $v$; replacing $v$ by $v/(2\| v\| _{L^\infty (L)})-3/2$, it therefore suffices to prove that $\int _ K(-V)\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge T\wedge \beta ^{r-1}\leq C\int _ L(-V)T\wedge \beta ^ r$ for plurisubharmonic functions $v$ satisfying $-2\leq v\leq -1$ on $\operatorname{Int}L$. Cover $K$ by finitely many balls $B(a,R_1)$ such that $\overline{B(a,R)}\subset \operatorname{Int}L$ for some $R>R_1$; it suffices to bound the integral over one ball $B(a,R_1)$.

Put $B=B(a,R)$, $\psi =|z-a|^2-R^2$ and $b=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi =\beta /\pi $. Fix $R_1<R_2<R$, and let $\widetilde v=\max \{ v,A\psi \} $, with $A=2/(R^2-R_2^2)$, be the collar modification (9.8) of $v$ on $\operatorname{Int}L$, with $N=2$ and with $R^2-R_2^2$ in place of $\delta $. Thus $\widetilde v$ is plurisubharmonic on $\operatorname{Int}L$ and takes values in $[-2,0)$ on $B$; it equals $v$ on $B(a,R_2)$, and $A\psi $ on the collar $\{ R^2-1/A<|z-a|^2<R^2\} $. Choose $0<\delta <1/A$ and put $B_\delta =\{ \psi <-\delta \} $, so that $\widetilde v=A\psi $ on $B\setminus B_\delta $ and $B(a,R_1)\subset B_\delta $. Choose a smooth function $\kappa $ with $0\leq \kappa \leq R^2$, with compact support in $B$ and equal to $-\psi $ on a neighborhood of $\overline{B_\delta }$; this is possible because $0<-\psi \leq R^2$ on $B$. Put $S=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\widetilde v\wedge T$ and $Q=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V\wedge T$; since $V$ is smooth and plurisubharmonic, $Q$ is a closed positive current of bidimension $(r-1,r-1)$. Since $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\kappa =-b$ near $\overline{B_\delta }$ and $S=Ab\wedge T$ on $B\setminus B_\delta $,

\[ \begin{aligned} \int _{B_\delta }(-V)S\wedge b^{r-1} & =\int _ B VS\wedge b^{r-2}\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\kappa -A\int _{B\setminus B_\delta } VT\wedge b^{r-1}\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\kappa \\ & =\int _ B\kappa \, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\widetilde v\wedge Q\wedge b^{r-2} -A\int _{B\setminus B_\delta } VT\wedge b^{r-1}\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\kappa . \end{aligned} \]

For the second line we integrate by parts against the smooth compactly supported function $\kappa $: since $V$ is smooth and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\widetilde v\wedge T\wedge b^{r-2}$ is closed, $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(V\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\widetilde v\wedge T\wedge b^{r-2})=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\widetilde v\wedge T\wedge b^{r-2}$, and the Bedford–Taylor product is symmetric with respect to the smooth factor.

We bound the two terms on the right. Choose a compact set $L'$ with $\overline B\subset \operatorname{Int}L'$ and $L'\subset \operatorname{Int}L$. Since $0\leq \kappa \leq R^2$, the Chern–Levine–Nirenberg inequality (theorem 9.1.3) applied to $Q$ bounds the first term by a constant times the trace mass of $Q$ on $\overline B$. To bound this mass, test $Q$ against a smooth cutoff $0\leq \chi \leq 1$ equal to one on $\overline B$ and supported in $L'$:

\[ \int \chi \, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V\wedge T\wedge \beta ^{r-1}=\int V\, T\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi \wedge \beta ^{r-1}\leq C\int _ L(-V)T\wedge \beta ^ r, \]

since $V\leq 0$ and the coefficients of $T\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi \wedge \beta ^{r-1}$ are bounded by a fixed multiple of the trace measure. The collar term has the same bound, because $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\kappa $ is a fixed smooth form. Finally, $S=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge T$ on $B(a,R_1)$ and $-V\geq 0$, so the left side of the display bounds $\pi ^{1-r}\int _{B(a,R_1)}(-V)\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge T\wedge \beta ^{r-1}$. Summing over the finitely many balls proves the estimate, with a constant depending only on the chosen radii.

Step 2: $r\geq 2$ and $V$ arbitrary. Choose a compact set $L_1$ with $K\subset \operatorname{Int}L_1$ and $L_1\subset \operatorname{Int}L$, depending only on $K$ and $L$. The mollifications $V_ l=V*\rho _{\varepsilon _ l}$, with $\varepsilon _ l\downarrow 0$, are defined, smooth and plurisubharmonic on a neighborhood of $L_1$ for large $l$, and they decrease to $V$ (proposition 8.2.2(2)); in particular $V\leq V_ l\leq 0$, so that $0\leq -V_ l\leq -V$. Step 1 only involves the weight on a neighborhood of the larger compact set, so it applies to $V_ l$ and the pair $(K,L_1)$ and gives

\[ \int _ K(-V_ l)\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge T\wedge \beta ^{r-1}\leq C_{K,L_1}\| v\| _{L^\infty (L)}\int _ L(-V)T\wedge \beta ^ r . \]

As $l\to \infty $, $-V_ l$ increases to $-V$, and monotone convergence against the positive measure $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge T\wedge \beta ^{r-1}$ proves the estimate.

Step 3: $r=1$. Let $t$ be the coordinate of $\mathbb C$, put $\beta _ t=\frac{\mathrm i}{2}\mathrm dt\wedge \mathrm d\bar t$, let $D'\Subset D''\Subset D$ be the discs about $0$ of radii $1,2,3$, and let $P\colon U\times D\to U$ be the projection. By proposition 7.5.2, $P^*T$ is a closed positive current of bidimension $(2,2)$ on $U\times D$. Moreover, by corollary 7.5.3(1), the pullback of a current $R=\sum R_{IJ}\, \mathrm dz_ I\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ J$ of order zero on $U$ is $P^*R=\sum (R_{IJ}\otimes \lambda _ D)\, \mathrm dz_ I\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ J$, where $\lambda _ D$ is Lebesgue measure on $D$. Since $v$ is a locally bounded Borel function, $P^*(vT)=(v\circ P)P^*T$ by corollary 7.5.3(2), and since $P^*$ commutes with $\partial $ and $\bar\partial $,

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v\circ P)\wedge P^*T=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\bigl (P^*(vT)\bigr )=P^*(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge T). \]

The functions $V\circ P\leq 0$ and $v\circ P$ are plurisubharmonic (proposition 8.2.3), and $\| v\circ P\| _{L^\infty (L\times \overline{D''})}=\| v\| _{L^\infty (L)}$. We apply Step 2 with $r=2$ to these data, to the Euclidean form $\beta +\beta _ t$ and to the compact sets $K\times \overline{D'}\subset \operatorname{Int}L\times D''=\operatorname{Int}(L\times \overline{D''})$. Since $T\wedge \beta ^2=0$ and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge T\wedge \beta =0$ for bidegree reasons, the trace measures in Step 2 are

\[ P^*T\wedge (\beta +\beta _ t)^2=2P^*(T\wedge \beta )\wedge \beta _ t, \qquad \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v\circ P)\wedge P^*T\wedge (\beta +\beta _ t)=P^*(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge T)\wedge \beta _ t, \]

that is, the products of the measures $2T\wedge \beta $ and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge T$ with Lebesgue measure on $D$. By Fubini's theorem (proposition 1.1.7), the inequality of Step 2 therefore reads

\[ \pi \int _ K(-V)\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge T\leq C\| v\| _{L^\infty (L)}\cdot 8\pi \int _ L(-V)T\wedge \beta , \]

where $C$ is the constant of Step 2 for $K\times \overline{D'}$ and $L\times \overline{D''}$ in dimension $n+1$, which depends only on $K$, $L$ and $n$. This is the case $r=1$, with $C_{K,L}=8C$. $\square$

Lemma 15.3.2. Let $T$ be a closed positive current of bidimension $(r,r)$, $r\geq 1$, on a coordinate domain, whose trace measure gives zero mass to every pluripolar set. Then for every locally bounded plurisubharmonic $v$, the trace measure of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge T$ also gives zero mass to every pluripolar set. Consequently the same holds for every Bedford–Taylor product $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ q\wedge T$, $1\leq q\leq r$, of locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions with base $T$.

Proof. Let $E$ be a pluripolar subset of the coordinate domain $U$ on which $T$ lives. By definition 10.1.1 and since $U$ has a countable basis, $E$ is covered by countably many compact sets $K$, each having a compact neighborhood $L$ with $K\subset \operatorname{Int}L$, together with an open neighborhood $W$ of $L$ and a function $V\in \mathrm{PSH}(W)$ such that $E\cap K\subset \{ V=-\infty \} $; shrinking $W$ and subtracting a constant, we may assume $V\leq 0$. The set $\{ V=-\infty \} $ is a pluripolar Borel set, so it suffices to show that $K\cap \{ V=-\infty \} $ has measure zero for the trace measure of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge T$.

Put $\mu =\mathbf1_ LT\wedge \beta ^ r$. This is a finite measure that does not charge $\{ V=-\infty \} $, so $V$ is finite $\mu $-almost everywhere. Hence $\min \{ -V/R,1\} \to 0$ $\mu $-almost everywhere as $R\to \infty $, and by dominated convergence we may choose numbers $R_ j\geq 2^ j$ such that

\[ \int \min \{ -V/R_ j,1\} \, \mathrm d\mu \leq 2^{-j}\qquad (j\geq 1). \]

Put $\widetilde V=\sum _{j\geq 1}\max \{ V/R_ j,-1\} $. The partial sums are plurisubharmonic by proposition 8.2.5, and they decrease, because every term is nonpositive. Their limit satisfies $\widetilde V\geq \sum _ jV/R_ j\geq V$, since $V\leq 0$ and $\sum _ j1/R_ j\leq 1$; hence it is not identically $-\infty $ on any component of $W$, and it is plurisubharmonic by theorem 8.3.1. Moreover $\widetilde V\leq 0$, $\widetilde V=-\infty $ on $\{ V=-\infty \} $, where every term equals $-1$, and by monotone convergence

\[ \int _ L(-\widetilde V)\, T\wedge \beta ^ r=\sum _{j\geq 1}\int \min \{ -V/R_ j,1\} \, \mathrm d\mu \leq 1. \]

By lemma 15.3.1, applied on $W$, the function $-\widetilde V$ is integrable on $K$ for the trace measure $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge T\wedge \beta ^{r-1}$ of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge T$. Hence this measure gives zero mass to $K\cap \{ \widetilde V=-\infty \} \supset K\cap \{ V=-\infty \} $, which proves the first assertion. The second follows by induction on $q$, since $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ q\wedge T=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_1\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_2\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ q\wedge T)$, where the base current in parentheses is closed and positive of bidimension $(r-q+1,r-q+1)$. $\square$

Proposition 15.3.3. Let $u_1,\ldots ,u_ p$ be plurisubharmonic on a coordinate domain, $1\leq p\leq n$, and suppose that $u_1,\ldots ,u_ q$ are locally bounded for some $0\leq q\leq p$. If $S=\langle \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{q+1}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ p\rangle $ is locally finite, then the non-pluripolar product of $u_1,\ldots ,u_ p$ is locally finite and

\[ \langle \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ p\rangle =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\wedge S, \]

where the right side is the Bedford–Taylor product of definition 9.1.1 with base $S$.

Proof. For $q=0$ there is nothing to prove. Inserting the bounded factors $u_ q,u_{q-1},\ldots ,u_1$ one at a time, we reduce to the case $q=1$. So let $v=u_1$ be locally bounded and $S=\langle \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_2\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ p\rangle $ locally finite. By theorem 15.2.1, $S$ is a closed positive current, so $R=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge S$ is defined; it is closed and positive, and by lemma 15.3.2 it gives zero mass to pluripolar sets, because $S$ does. If $p=1$, then $S=1$ and the assertion is that of bounded Bedford–Taylor theory. Let $p\geq 2$. The assertion is local, so we may assume $u_ j\leq 0$ for $j\geq 2$ and put

\[ \begin{gathered} O_ k=\bigcap _{j\geq 2}\{ u_ j>-k\} ,\qquad \Theta _ k=\bigwedge _{j\geq 2}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\max \{ u_ j,-k\} ,\\ w_ k=\max \Bigl \{ 1+\frac1k\sum _{j\geq 2}u_ j,\, 0\Bigr \} . \end{gathered} \]

The functions $w_ k$ are plurisubharmonic by proposition 8.2.5, and they take values in $[0,1]$ because all $u_ j\leq 0$. They vanish outside $O_ k$: if $u_ i\leq -k$ for some $i\geq 2$, then $\sum _{j\geq 2}u_ j\leq u_ i\leq -k$. The sets $\{ w_ k>0\} =\{ \sum _{j\geq 2}u_ j>-k\} $ increase to the set where all $u_ j$, $j\geq 2$, are finite.

Take smooth plurisubharmonic functions $v_ l$ decreasing to $v$ near a given compact set. By lemma 15.1.2, $S$ and $\Theta _ k$ have the same restriction to $O_ k$; since $w_ k$ vanishes outside $O_ k$ and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ l$ is smooth,

\[ w_ k\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ l\wedge S=w_ k\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ l\wedge \Theta _ k. \]

The weighted form of theorem 9.2.5, applied with the constant sequence of weights $w_ k$ and the fixed base currents $S$ on the left and $\Theta _ k$ on the right, gives $w_ kR=w_ k\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge \Theta _ k$ in the limit $l\to \infty $. Multiplying by the bounded Borel functions $\mathbf1_{\{ w_ k>\varepsilon \} }/w_ k$ and letting $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$, we see that $R$ and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge \Theta _ k$ have the same restriction to $\{ w_ k>0\} $. Now fix an integer $m\geq 1$. For $k\geq m$, the bounded functions $\max \{ u_ i,-k\} $ and $\max \{ u_ i,-m\} $ coincide on $O_ m$, so $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge \Theta _ k=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge \Theta _ m$ on $O_ m$ by theorem 10.4.3. Since the sets $\{ w_ k>0\} \cap O_ m$, $k\geq m$, exhaust $O_ m$, we conclude that $\mathbf1_{O_ m}R=\mathbf1_{O_ m}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge \Theta _ m$.

Consider finally the full tuple $v,u_2,\ldots ,u_ p$ on a relatively compact open set on which $|v|<m_0$. For $m\geq m_0$, its level-$m$ set is $O_ m$ and $v$ needs no truncation, so its truncated product at level $m$ is $\mathbf1_{O_ m}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge \Theta _ m=\mathbf1_{O_ m}R$. These restrictions are bounded by $R$, hence have locally bounded mass, and they increase to $R$ because $R$ gives the pole sets of $u_2,\ldots ,u_ p$ zero mass. Therefore the non-pluripolar product of $v,u_2,\ldots ,u_ p$ is locally finite and equals $R=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge S$. $\square$

To identify the first-degree product we shall integrate planar measures over almost every fiber of a coordinate projection. We first record the measure theory of such fiber integrals, and then prove the fiber formula for bounded potentials, with the Borel weights needed for truncation.

Lemma 15.3.4. Let $Q\subset \mathbb C^ r$ and $D\subset \mathbb C^ m$ be open sets, $r,m\geq 1$, let $G\subset D$ be a Borel set, and for each $z\in G$ let $\mu _ z$ be a positive Radon measure on $Q$. Assume that $z\mapsto \int a\, \mathrm d\mu _ z$ is Borel on $G$ for every continuous function $a$ with compact support in $Q$. Then $z\mapsto \mu _ z(E_ z)$ is Borel on $G$ for every Borel set $E\subset Q\times D$, where $E_ z=\{ x:(x,z)\in E\} $. Moreover, for every nonnegative Borel function $f$ on $D$,

\[ \lambda (E)=\int _ G\mu _ z(E_ z)f(z)\, \mathrm dV(z) \]

is a measure on the Borel sets of $Q\times D$, and

\[ \int h\, \mathrm d\lambda =\int _ G\Bigl (\int _ Qh(x,z)\, \mathrm d\mu _ z(x)\Bigr )f(z)\, \mathrm dV(z) \]

for every nonnegative Borel function $h$ on $Q\times D$, the inner integral being a Borel function of $z\in G$.

Proof. Fix open sets $Q''\Subset Q$ and $D''\Subset D$, so that $\mu _ z(Q'')<\infty $ for every $z$. If $A\subset Q''$ is open, the continuous functions $a_ l(x)=\min \{ 1,\max \{ l\operatorname {dist}(x,\mathbb C^ r\setminus A)-1,0\} \} $ have compact support in $A$ and increase to $\mathbf1_ A$, so $z\mapsto \mu _ z(A)$ is Borel by monotone convergence. Let $\mathcal D$ be the family of Borel sets $A\subset Q''$ for which $z\mapsto \mu _ z(A)$ is Borel on $G$. It contains the open subsets of $Q''$, which are closed under finite intersections and generate the Borel $\sigma $-algebra of $Q''$. It is closed under complements in $Q''$, because $\mu _ z(Q''\setminus A)=\mu _ z(Q'')-\mu _ z(A)$ with finite values, and under countable disjoint unions. By Dynkin's theorem (lemma 1.1.6), $\mathcal D$ contains every Borel subset of $Q''$.

Next, let $\mathcal D'$ be the family of Borel sets $E\subset Q''\times D''$ for which $z\mapsto \mu _ z(E_ z)$ is Borel on $G$. It contains the products $A\times A'$ of Borel sets $A\subset Q''$ and $A'\subset D''$, since $\mu _ z((A\times A')_ z)=\mu _ z(A)\mathbf1_{A'}(z)$; these products are closed under finite intersections and generate the Borel $\sigma $-algebra of $Q''\times D''$, because every open subset of $Q''\times D''$ is a countable union of products of open balls. As before, $\mathcal D'$ is closed under complements and countable disjoint unions, so it contains every Borel subset of $Q''\times D''$. Exhausting $Q$ and $D$ by increasing sequences of such sets $Q''$ and $D''$, and using monotone convergence, we obtain the first assertion.

By the first assertion, $\lambda (E)$ is defined, and $\lambda $ is countably additive by monotone convergence, applied first to the measures $\mu _ z$ and then to the integral over $G$. The formula for $\int h\, \mathrm d\lambda $ holds for indicator functions of Borel sets by definition, hence for nonnegative simple functions by linearity, and for nonnegative Borel functions by monotone convergence, applied in the same order; the inner integrals are Borel in $z$ as increasing limits of Borel functions. $\square$

Lemma 15.3.5. Let $r,m\geq 0$ be integers, let $Q\subset \mathbb C^ r$ and $D\subset \mathbb C^ m$ be open sets with coordinates $x$ and $z$, and let $p_ D\colon Q\times D\to D$ be the projection. Let $u_1,\ldots ,u_ r$ be locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions on $Q\times D$, and put

\[ M=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ r,\qquad M_ z=\bigwedge _{j=1}^ r\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}_ x u_ j(\, \cdot \, ,z), \]

the Bedford–Taylor products on $Q\times D$ and on the fibers $Q\times \{ z\} $; here $u_ j(\, \cdot \, ,z)$ is locally bounded and plurisubharmonic on $Q$ by proposition 8.2.3. For every smooth real $(m,m)$-form $\eta $ on $D$ and every bounded Borel function $h$ on $Q\times D$ with compact support, the fiber integral $z\mapsto \int _ Q h(x,z)\, M_ z(x)$ is Borel measurable and

15.10
\begin{equation} \int _{Q\times D}h\, p_ D^*\eta \wedge M =\int _ D\left(\int _ Q h(x,z)\, M_ z(x)\right)\eta (z). \end{equation}

If $\eta \geq 0$, the identity holds for every nonnegative Borel function $h$, with possibly infinite values.

Proof. If $r=0$ or $m=0$, one factor of $Q\times D$ is a point, carrying counting measure, and the corresponding product is the empty product $1$; then the two sides of (15.10) are the same integral. Let $r,m\geq 1$. For smooth potentials the formula is Fubini's theorem; we pass to the limit for continuous weights $h$, and then extend the formula to Borel weights by the uniqueness of measures.

Approximation. Let $Q'\Subset Q$ and $D'\Subset D$ be open. For $\varepsilon _ l\downarrow 0$, the mollifications $u_{j,l}=u_ j*\rho _{\varepsilon _ l}$ are smooth and plurisubharmonic on a neighborhood of $\overline{Q'\times D'}$ for large $l$, they decrease to $u_ j$ (proposition 8.2.2(2)), and they are bounded there by a constant $B$, since $u_ j\leq u_{j,l}\leq u_{j,l_0}$. Let $M^{(l)}$ and $M_ z^{(l)}$ be the corresponding products. By theorem 9.2.5, $M^{(l)}\to M$ weakly on $Q'\times D'$, and for every $z\in D'$, $M_ z^{(l)}\to M_ z$ weakly on $Q'$, since $u_{j,l}(\, \cdot \, ,z)$ decreases to $u_ j(\, \cdot \, ,z)$. By theorem 9.1.3, for every compact set $A\subset Q'$ the masses $M_ z^{(l)}(A)$ and $M_ z(A)$ are bounded by a constant depending only on $A$, $Q'$ and $B$, uniformly in $l$ and $z\in D'$; similarly for $M^{(l)}$ and $M$ on compact subsets of $Q'\times D'$. For positive measures with locally uniformly bounded masses, weak convergence implies convergence of the integrals of continuous functions with compact support, because such functions are uniform limits of test functions supported in a fixed compact set (lemma 1.4.1).

Continuous weights. Let $\eta =f\, \mathrm dV_ z\geq 0$, where $\mathrm dV_ z$ is the Euclidean volume form of $\mathbb C^ m$ and $f\geq 0$ is smooth, and let $h$ be continuous with compact support in $Q'\times D'$. In the smooth exterior product $M^{(l)}$, wedging with the form $p_ D^*\eta $ of top degree in $z$ kills every term containing a differential $\mathrm dz_ i$ or $\mathrm d\bar z_ i$, so that $p_ D^*\eta \wedge M^{(l)}=f(z)\, M_ z^{(l)}(x)\wedge \mathrm dV_ z$, and Fubini's theorem (proposition 1.1.7) gives (15.10) for the smooth approximations. As $l\to \infty $, the left side converges to $\int h\, p_ D^*\eta \wedge M$, by the approximation step applied to the positive measures $p_ D^*\eta \wedge M^{(l)}$. On the right, the inner integrals are continuous functions of $z$, they converge to $\int _ Qh(x,z)\, M_ z(x)$ for every $z$, and they are bounded by $\sup |h|$ times a constant, uniformly in $l$ and $z$; hence the limit is a Borel function of $z$, and dominated convergence proves (15.10). A finite smooth partition of unity (lemma 1.4.2), subordinate to a cover of $\operatorname{Supp}h$ by such products $Q'\times D'$, treats arbitrary continuous $h$ with compact support.

Measurability. For a continuous function $a$ with compact support in $Q$, the function $z\mapsto \int a\, M_ z$ is Borel on $D$: if the support of $a$ lies in $Q'$, then on $D'$ it is the pointwise limit of the continuous functions $z\mapsto \int a\, M_ z^{(l)}$, and $D$ is covered by countably many such sets $D'$. Hence lemma 15.3.4, applied with $G=D$ and $\mu _ z=M_ z$, shows that $z\mapsto \int _ Qh(x,z)\, M_ z(x)$ is Borel for every nonnegative Borel function $h$, and hence for every bounded Borel function $h$ with compact support.

Borel weights. Let $\eta =f\, \mathrm dV_ z\geq 0$ as before, and let $\lambda $ be the measure of lemma 15.3.4 for $\mu _ z=M_ z$ and this function $f$, so that

\[ \lambda (E)=\int _ D M_ z(E_ z)\, \eta (z) \]

for Borel sets $E\subset Q\times D$. The uniform local bound for the fiber masses makes $\lambda $ finite on compact sets, so it is a Radon measure by proposition 1.1.2. By lemma 15.3.4, $\int h\, \mathrm d\lambda =\int _ D(\int _ Qh(x,z)\, M_ z(x))\, \eta (z)$ for every nonnegative Borel function $h$, hence for every continuous $h$ with compact support. By the step on continuous weights, $\lambda $ and $p_ D^*\eta \wedge M$ therefore have the same integral against every test function, so they are equal by the uniqueness in theorem 1.4.5. This proves the formula for nonnegative Borel $h$, and then for bounded real Borel $h$ with compact support, by splitting $h$ into its positive and negative parts.

Finally, let $\eta =f\, \mathrm dV_ z$ be a smooth real form and $h$ a bounded Borel function supported in $K\times K_ D$, with $K\subset Q$ and $K_ D\subset D$ compact. Choose $\kappa \in C_ c^\infty (D)$ with $0\leq \kappa \leq 1$ and $\kappa =1$ on $K_ D$, and put $c=\sup _{\operatorname{Supp}\kappa }|f|$. Replacing $\eta $ by $\kappa \eta $ changes neither side of (15.10), and $\kappa \eta =\kappa (f+c)\, \mathrm dV_ z-c\kappa \, \mathrm dV_ z$ is the difference of two smooth nonnegative forms. This proves the formula for signed $\eta $. $\square$

Proposition 15.3.6. Let $u$ be plurisubharmonic on a coordinate domain. Then its non-pluripolar product of degree one is locally finite and

15.11
\begin{equation} \langle \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\rangle =\mathbf1_{\{ u>-\infty \} }\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u, \end{equation}

where $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ on the right is the distributional complex Hessian. In particular the current $\mathbf1_{\{ u>-\infty \} }\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ is closed.

Proof. Upper bound. The assertion is local, so we may assume $u\leq 0$. The truncations $u_ l=\max \{ u,-l\} $ decrease to $u$ and converge to it in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$ (theorem 8.3.1), so $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ l\to \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ weakly. For fixed $k$ and every $l\geq k$, plurifine locality (theorem 10.4.3) and lemma 15.1.1 give $\mathbf1_{\{ u>-k\} }\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ k=\mathbf1_{\{ u>-k\} }\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ l\leq \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ l$, and letting $l\to \infty $ yields $\mathbf1_{\{ u>-k\} }\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ k\leq \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$. Hence the truncated products have locally bounded mass, the non-pluripolar product $S=\langle \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\rangle $ is locally finite, and $0\leq S\leq \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$. Since $S$ gives no mass to the pole set of $u$, it follows that $S\leq \mathbf1_{\{ u>-\infty \} }\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$. It remains to prove the reverse inequality.

One variable. Let $n=1$ and put $\mu =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$, a positive measure. Let $V$ be a relatively compact open subset of the domain, and choose a smooth compactly supported function $\kappa \geq 0$ equal to one on $V$. With $\nu =\kappa \mu $ and the fundamental solution $E_2$ of (2.9), theorem 3.2.3 gives

\[ u=4\pi E_2*\nu +h\quad \text{on }V, \]

where $h$ is harmonic; the factor $4\pi $ appears because $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u=(\Delta u)/(4\pi )$ as planar measures, while the Riesz measure is $\Delta u$. Put $p=E_2*\nu $ and $\nu _ f=\mathbf1_{\{ p>-\infty \} }\nu $; on $V$ we have $\{ p>-\infty \} =\{ u>-\infty \} $. The kernel $E_2$ is bounded above on the compact set of differences of points of $\operatorname{Supp}\nu $, so removing positive mass from $\nu $ lowers its potential on $\operatorname{Supp}\nu $ by at most a constant: $E_2*\nu _ f\geq p-C$ there. Hence $E_2*\nu _ f$ is finite $\nu _ f$-almost everywhere, and theorem 4.2.2 writes $\nu _ f=\sum _ i\nu _ i$ with compactly supported measures $\nu _ i$ whose potentials are finite and continuous.

For each $N$, the measure $\nu ^{(N)}=\sum _{i\leq N}\nu _ i$ has the continuous subharmonic potential $v_ N=4\pi E_2*\nu ^{(N)}$, with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ N=\nu ^{(N)}$. On $V$ we have $\mu =\nu \geq \nu _ f\geq \nu ^{(N)}$, so the distribution $u-v_ N$ has positive Laplacian on $V$. By theorem 3.2.2(3), this distribution has a subharmonic representative $s$ on $V$, and by part (2) of that theorem the value of $s$ at each point is the limit of its averages over small discs. These averages are also those of the function $u-v_ N$, and they converge to $u-v_ N$ at every point, by part (2) applied to $u$ and the continuity of $v_ N$. Hence $s=u-v_ N$ everywhere on $V$, and $u-v_ N$ is subharmonic on $V$. Since $v_ N$ is locally bounded, multilinearity (proposition 15.1.5) gives

\[ S=\langle \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ N\rangle +\langle \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u-v_ N)\rangle \geq \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ N=\nu ^{(N)}\quad \text{on }V. \]

Letting $N\to \infty $ yields $S\geq \nu _ f=\mathbf1_{\{ u>-\infty \} }\mu $ on $V$, which proves the proposition for $n=1$.

Several variables. Let $n\geq 2$. The assertion is local, so we may work on a polydisc $P$ such that $u\leq 0$ is plurisubharmonic on a neighborhood of $\overline P$. Fix one of the coordinates, denote it by $x$, and write $z\in \mathbb C^{n-1}$ for the others, so that $P=\Delta \times Z$ with a disc $\Delta \subset \mathbb C$ and a polydisc $Z\subset \mathbb C^{n-1}$. Let $\mathrm dV_ z$ be the Euclidean volume form of the $z$-coordinates, viewed as an $(n-1,n-1)$-form, and $\mathrm dA$ the area measure on $\Delta $. We disintegrate $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\wedge \mathrm dV_ z$ along the fibers $\Delta \times \{ z\} $ and apply the one-variable case on each fiber. Since $u$ is integrable on $\Delta \times Z$, Fubini's theorem (proposition 1.1.7) shows that $G=\{ z\in Z:\int _\Delta |u(x,z)|\, \mathrm dA(x)<\infty \} $ is a Borel set of full measure in $Z$. For $z\in G$ the function $u_ z=u(\, \cdot \, ,z)$ is integrable, hence not identically $-\infty $, so it is subharmonic on $\Delta $ by proposition 8.2.3; let $\mu _ z=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}_ xu_ z$ be its Hessian measure.

For a test function $a$ on $\Delta $, we have $\int a\, \mathrm d\mu _ z=\int _\Delta u_ z\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}_ xa$, and Fubini's theorem, applied to the positive and negative parts of the integrand, shows that this is a Borel function of $z\in G$. Uniform approximation by test functions supported in a fixed compact set extends this to continuous functions $a$ with compact support, since each $\mu _ z$ is finite on compact sets. Hence lemma 15.3.4, with $f=1$, gives a measure $\lambda $ on $\Delta \times Z$ with $\int h\, \mathrm d\lambda =\int _ G(\int h(\, \cdot \, ,z)\, \mathrm d\mu _ z)\, \mathrm dV(z)$ for nonnegative Borel $h$. If $\chi \in C_ c^\infty (\Delta )$ satisfies $0\leq \chi \leq 1$ and equals one on a disc $\Delta '\Subset \Delta $, then $\mu _ z(\Delta ')\leq \int u_ z\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}_ x\chi \leq C_\chi \int _\Delta |u_ z|\, \mathrm dA$, which is integrable over $G$; so $\lambda $ is finite on compact sets. For a test function $\varphi $ on $\Delta \times Z$, only the derivative $\partial _ x\partial _{\bar x}\varphi $ survives in $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \wedge \mathrm dV_ z$, and Fubini's theorem gives

\[ \int \varphi \, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\wedge \mathrm dV_ z=\int u\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \wedge \mathrm dV_ z=\int _ G\Bigl (\int _\Delta u_ z\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}_ x\varphi (\, \cdot \, ,z)\Bigr )\mathrm dV(z)=\int \varphi \, \mathrm d\lambda . \]

By the uniqueness in theorem 1.4.5, the positive measure $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\wedge \mathrm dV_ z$ equals $\lambda $, that is,

15.12
\begin{equation} \int g\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\wedge \mathrm dV_ z=\int _ G\Bigl (\int g(\, \cdot \, ,z)\, \mathrm d\mu _ z\Bigr )\mathrm dV(z) \end{equation}

for every nonnegative Borel function $g$ on $\Delta \times Z$.

Now let $g\geq 0$ be a bounded Borel function with compact support in $\Delta \times Z$, and apply lemma 15.3.5, with $r=1$, $Q=\Delta $, $D=Z$ and $\eta =\mathrm dV_ z$, to the bounded function $u_ k=\max \{ u,-k\} $ and the weight $g\mathbf1_{\{ u>-k\} }$:

\[ \int g\mathbf1_{\{ u>-k\} }\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ k\wedge \mathrm dV_ z =\int \Bigl (\int g\mathbf1_{\{ u_ z>-k\} }\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}_ x\max \{ u_ z,-k\} \Bigr )\mathrm dV_ z. \]

As $k\to \infty $, the left side increases to $\int gS\wedge \mathrm dV_ z$ by lemma 15.1.2, since $\mathbf1_{\{ u>-k\} }\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ k$ is the truncated product $S_ k$ of $u$. For $z\in G$, the inner integral on the right is the integral of $g(\, \cdot \, ,z)$ against the truncated product of $u_ z$ at level $k$; by lemma 15.1.2 and the one-variable case, it increases to $\int g\, \mathrm d\langle \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}_ xu_ z\rangle =\int g\mathbf1_{\{ u_ z>-\infty \} }\, \mathrm d\mu _ z$. Since $Z\setminus G$ is a null set, monotone convergence in $z$ and (15.12), applied to $g\mathbf1_{\{ u>-\infty \} }$, give $\int gS\wedge \mathrm dV_ z=\int g\mathbf1_{\{ u>-\infty \} }\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\wedge \mathrm dV_ z$, so that $S\wedge \mathrm dV_ z=\mathbf1_{\{ u>-\infty \} }\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\wedge \mathrm dV_ z$.

Since $\beta ^{n-1}=(n-1)!\sum _{j=1}^ n\mathrm dV_{(j)}$, where $\mathrm dV_{(j)}$ is the volume form of the coordinates other than the $j$th, summing over the $n$ coordinate directions shows that the trace measures of $S$ and of $\mathbf1_{\{ u>-\infty \} }\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ coincide. Thus $\mathbf1_{\{ u>-\infty \} }\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u-S$ is a positive current with zero trace measure, and it vanishes by theorem 7.2.5. This proves (15.11); closedness follows from theorem 15.2.1. $\square$

Proposition 15.3.7. Let $\mu $ be a probability measure supported on a compact set $K\subset \mathbb C\subset \mathbb P^1$, and let $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$ be the Fubini–Study form of example 14.5.6, which has total mass one on $\mathbb P^1$. Then

\[ q(z)=\int _ K\log \frac{|z-a|^2}{(1+|z|^2)(1+|a|^2)}\, \mathrm d\mu (a) \]

defines a nonpositive $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$-plurisubharmonic function on $\mathbb P^1$ with $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q=\mu $. Its Lelong number at every point $x\in \mathbb P^1$ is $\mu (\{ x\} )$, where $\mu $ is regarded as a measure on $\mathbb P^1$.

Proof. In the affine chart put $\rho (z)=\log (1+|z|^2)$, so that $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho $. The integrand is nonpositive, since $|z-a|^2\leq (|z|+|a|)^2\leq (1+|z|^2)(1+|a|^2)$ by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality. On the affine chart,

\[ q+\rho =p_\mu -c,\qquad p_\mu (z)=\int _ K\log |z-a|^2\, \mathrm d\mu (a),\qquad c=\int _ K\log (1+|a|^2)\, \mathrm d\mu (a). \]

Since $\log |z-a|^2=4\pi E_2(z-a)$ with the fundamental solution $E_2$ of (2.9), the function $p_\mu =4\pi E_2*\mu $ is subharmonic and locally integrable with $\Delta p_\mu =4\pi \mu $, by proposition 3.2.1. Since $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}=\Delta /(4\pi )$ on functions of one complex variable, as planar measures, this gives $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p_\mu =\mu $ on the affine chart, and $q=p_\mu -c-\rho $ is quasi-plurisubharmonic there.

Near infinity use the coordinate $w=1/z$, in which the integrand becomes

\[ \log |1-aw|^2-\log (1+|w|^2)-\log (1+|a|^2). \]

Let $R_ K=\max _{a\in K}|a|$. For $|w|<1/(2R_ K+2)$ we have $|1-aw|\geq 1/2$, so this expression is a smooth function of $(a,w)$ on $K\times \{ |w|<1/(2R_ K+2)\} $ whose derivatives in $w$ of every order are bounded uniformly in $a\in K$; at $w=0$ it takes the value $-\log (1+|a|^2)$. Differentiating under the integral sign, we see that $q$ extends to a smooth function near infinity, and that $q+\log (1+|w|^2)=\int _ K\log |1-aw|^2\, \mathrm d\mu (a)-c$ is harmonic there, because each $\log |1-aw|^2$ is harmonic in $w$. Since $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log (1+|w|^2)$ in this chart and $\mu $ vanishes near infinity, $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q=0=\mu $ there. Hence $q$ is quasi-plurisubharmonic on $\mathbb P^1$, $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q=\mu \geq 0$ globally, and $q$ is a nonpositive $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$-plurisubharmonic function.

At infinity both the Lelong number and the point mass vanish, because $q$ is smooth there and $K\subset \mathbb C$. At an affine point $x$, the Lelong number of $q$ is that of the local subharmonic function $q+\rho $ (corollary 12.3.2), and by (12.5) and (12.3) it equals $\lim _{r\downarrow 0}\mu (B(x,r))=\mu (\{ x\} )$. $\square$

Example 15.3.8. Let $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$ be the Fubini–Study form on $\mathbb P^1$, as in proposition 15.3.7. There is a nonpositive $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$-plurisubharmonic function $q$ on $\mathbb P^1$ such that

\[ \nu (q,x)=0\quad \text{for every }x,\qquad \mathcal I(tq)=\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^1}\quad (t>0),\qquad \langle \omega _{\mathrm{FS}}+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q\rangle =0. \]

Let $\mu $ be the atom-free probability measure on the compact polar set $K$ of proposition 4.2.4, and let $q$ be its potential from proposition 15.3.7. Since $\mu $ has no atoms, all Lelong numbers of $q$ vanish, and corollary 12.3.2 gives the assertion on multiplier ideals. On the affine chart, $q$ differs from the function $p(z)=\int _ K\log |z-a|^2\, \mathrm d\mu (a)$ of proposition 4.2.4 by the smooth function $-\log (1+|z|^2)$ and a constant, and $p=-\infty $ on $K$; hence $q=-\infty $ on $K$. Applying proposition 15.3.6 to local potentials of $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q$, we obtain

\[ \langle \omega _{\mathrm{FS}}+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q\rangle =\mathbf1_{\{ q>-\infty \} }\mu =0, \]

because $\mu $ is carried by $K$. By contrast, the potential $0$ has the same multiplier ideals and non-pluripolar mass one. Thus exponential integrability at every scale does not determine the non-pluripolar mass.

15.4 Global finiteness and bounded cohomological mass

From now on, $X$ is a connected compact Kähler manifold of dimension $n\geq 1$ with a fixed Kähler form $\omega $, and $\theta $ and $\theta _ j$ always denote smooth closed real $(1,1)$-forms on $X$. (We exclude dimension zero, where the only product is the empty one.) If $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ (definition 13.1.3) is locally bounded, then near each point $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(h+\varphi )$, where $h$ is a smooth local potential of $\theta $ and $h+\varphi $ is bounded and plurisubharmonic. By proposition 15.1.5, the Bedford–Taylor products of such local potentials do not depend on the choice of $h$; hence they glue to closed positive currents $\bigwedge _ j(\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ j)$ on $X$, which can be multiplied by smooth forms. We first prove Stokes' formula for these products and then show that non-pluripolar products on $X$ are always finite.

Proposition 15.4.1. Let $1\leq p\leq n$, let $\varphi _ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta _ j)$ be locally bounded for $1\leq j\leq p$, and let $\eta $ be a smooth closed $(n-p,n-p)$-form on $X$. Then

\[ \int _ X\bigwedge _{j=1}^ p(\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ j)\wedge \eta =\int _ X\theta _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \theta _ p\wedge \eta . \]

Proof. Fix $j$, let $R$ be the closed positive current $\bigwedge _{i>j}(\theta _ i+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ i)$, and let $\eta '$ be a smooth closed form of pure complementary bidegree. Locally $\theta _ j=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h_ j$ with $h_ j$ smooth, and since $R$ is closed, the recursion gives

\[ (\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ j)\wedge R=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}((h_ j+\varphi _ j)R)=\theta _ j\wedge R+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\varphi _ jR). \]

Here $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(h_ jR)=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h_ j\wedge R$ because $h_ j$ is smooth and $R$ is closed of pure bidegree, so that $\partial R=\bar\partial R=0$. Both sides are globally defined, because $\varphi _ j$ is a global bounded function. Moreover $\int _ X\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\varphi _ jR)\wedge \eta '=\int _ X\varphi _ jR\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\eta '=0$, since $\eta '$ is closed of pure bidegree, so that $\partial \eta '=\bar\partial \eta '=0$ and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\eta '=0$. Hence $\int _ X(\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ j)\wedge R\wedge \eta '=\int _ X\theta _ j\wedge R\wedge \eta '$. Apply this successively for $j=1,2,\ldots ,p$, with $\eta '=\theta _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \theta _{j-1}\wedge \eta $ at the $j$th step, which is smooth and closed. After $p$ steps the integral becomes $\int _ X\theta _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \theta _ p\wedge \eta $. $\square$

Lemma 15.4.2. Let $R$ be a closed positive current of bidimension $(1,1)$ on $X$, and let $a,b$ be bounded quasi-plurisubharmonic functions on $X$, or differences of such functions. Then $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b\wedge R=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(bR)$ and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a\wedge R=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(aR)$ are finite signed measures, and

\[ \int _ X a\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b\wedge R =\int _ X b\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a\wedge R =-\int _ X\mathrm da\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}b\wedge R. \]

Here the mixed gradient

\[ \mathrm da\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}b\wedge R=\tfrac 12\bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(abR)-a\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b\wedge R-b\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a\wedge R\bigr ) \]

is a finite signed measure on $X$, symmetric in $a$ and $b$. It is the exterior product of $\mathrm da\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}b$ with $R$ when $a$ and $b$ are smooth, and on an open set where $a=b$ is plurisubharmonic it is the gradient measure $E_ R(a)$ of (9.84).

Proof. The measures. Near each point of $X$, a bounded quasi-plurisubharmonic function $b$ is the difference $b_1-b_2$ of two bounded plurisubharmonic functions: if $b+g$ is plurisubharmonic on a coordinate ball with $g$ smooth, and $q=C|z|^2$ with $C$ so large that $g+q$ is plurisubharmonic on a smaller ball, take $b_1=b+g+q$ and $b_2=g+q$. Hence $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(bR)=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b_1\wedge R-\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b_2\wedge R$ is locally the difference of two Bedford–Taylor products, which are positive measures. Covering the compact manifold $X$ by finitely many such neighborhoods, we see that $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(bR)$ is a finite signed measure; the same holds for $a$.

For bounded plurisubharmonic functions $A$ and $B$ on an open set, additivity of Bedford–Taylor products (proposition 9.1.2) and the formula (9.84) give, after expanding $(A+B)^2$,

\[ E_ R(A+B)=E_ R(A)+E_ R(B)+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(ABR)-A\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}B\wedge R-B\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}A\wedge R, \]

so that $\frac12(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(ABR)-A\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}B\wedge R-B\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}A\wedge R)=\frac12(E_ R(A+B)-E_ R(A)-E_ R(B))$ is a signed measure, by lemma 9.13.1. Since the expression defining the mixed gradient is bilinear in $(a,b)$, writing $a=a_1-a_2$ and $b=b_1-b_2$ locally shows that the mixed gradient is a signed measure near each point, hence a finite signed measure on $X$. It is symmetric by definition. For $a=b$ plurisubharmonic it equals $\frac12\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(a^2R)-a\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a\wedge R=E_ R(a)$. For smooth $a$ and $b$ we have $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(ab)=a\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b+b\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a+\mathrm da\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}b+\mathrm db\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}a$, and the $(1,1)$-parts of $\mathrm da\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}b$ and $\mathrm db\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}a$ both equal $\frac{\mathrm i}{4\pi }(\partial a\wedge \bar\partial b+\partial b\wedge \bar\partial a)$; since only $(1,1)$-parts contribute in products with $R$ and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(abR)=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(ab)\wedge R$, the mixed gradient is $\mathrm da\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}b\wedge R$.

A local identity. Let $\chi $ be a test function with support in a coordinate ball $W$, and let $A$ and $B$ be bounded plurisubharmonic functions on a neighborhood of $\overline W$. We claim that

15.13
\begin{equation} \int \chi A\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(BR)-\int \chi B\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(AR)=-\int AB\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi \wedge R-2\, \mathrm d(BR)(A\, \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\chi ), \end{equation}

where $\mathrm d(BR)$ is the current of order zero of lemma 9.13.1 and its value on the bounded Borel one-form $A\, \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\chi $ with compact support is computed with its coefficient measures. Suppose first that $A$ is smooth. Then $\int \chi A\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(BR)=(BR)(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\chi A))$ and

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\chi A)=\chi \, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}A+A\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi +\mathrm d\chi \wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}A+\mathrm dA\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\chi . \]

The first term contributes $\int \chi B\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}A\wedge R$. Since $BR$ has bidegree $(n-1,n-1)$, it sees only the $(1,1)$-parts of the last two terms, which agree as above; so these terms contribute $2(BR)(\mathrm dA\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\chi )$. Finally $\mathrm dA\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\chi =\mathrm d(A\, \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\chi )-A\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi $, and $(BR)(\mathrm d\psi )=-\mathrm d(BR)(\psi )$ for one-forms $\psi $, because $BR$ has even degree. Collecting terms gives (15.13).

In general, let $A_ l$ be smooth plurisubharmonic functions decreasing to $A$ on a neighborhood of $\overline W$, for instance mollifications (proposition 8.2.2(2)). Then (15.13) holds for $A_ l$, and every term converges to the corresponding term for $A$. For the first, third and last terms this follows by dominated convergence against the fixed signed measures $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(BR)$, $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi \wedge R$ and the coefficient measures of $\mathrm d(BR)$, since the $A_ l$ are uniformly bounded on $\operatorname{Supp}\chi $ and converge pointwise to $A$. For the second term it follows from the weighted form of theorem 9.2.5, with the constant sequence of weights $B$. This proves the claim. Since both sides of (15.13) are bilinear in $(A,B)$, the identity also holds for differences of bounded plurisubharmonic functions, and in particular for $A=a$ and $B=b$ near $\overline W$.

Conclusion. Let $(\chi _ i)$ be a finite smooth partition of unity on $X$ subordinate to coordinate balls as above (proposition 7.1.3). Summing (15.13) for $\chi =\chi _ i$, $A=a$ and $B=b$ over $i$, and using that $abR$, $\mathrm d(bR)$ and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(aR)$, $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(bR)$ are global currents, we obtain

\[ \int _ Xa\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b\wedge R-\int _ Xb\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a\wedge R=-\int _ Xab\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Bigl (\sum _ i\chi _ i\Bigr )\wedge R-2\, \mathrm d(bR)\Bigl (a\, \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\sum _ i\chi _ i\Bigr )=0, \]

because $\sum _ i\chi _ i=1$. This is the first equality. For the second, note that $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(abR)$ is a finite signed measure, being a combination of the mixed gradient and the measures $a\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b\wedge R$ and $b\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a\wedge R$, and that its total integral is $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(abR)(1)=(abR)(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}1)=0$, because the constant function $1$ is a test function on the compact manifold $X$. Integrating the definition of the mixed gradient over $X$ and using the first equality, we get $\int _ X\mathrm da\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}b\wedge R=-\int _ Xa\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b\wedge R$. $\square$

Theorem 15.4.3. Let $T_1,\ldots ,T_ p$, $1\leq p\leq n$, be closed positive $(1,1)$-currents on the compact Kähler manifold $X$. Then their non-pluripolar product is finite. More precisely, write $T_ j=\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ j$ with $\varphi _ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta _ j)$, and choose $C\geq 0$ such that the forms $\tau _ j=\theta _ j+C\omega $ are semipositive. Then

15.14
\begin{equation} \int _ X\langle T_1\wedge \cdots \wedge T_ p\rangle \wedge \omega ^{n-p} \leq \int _ X\tau _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \tau _ p\wedge \omega ^{n-p}. \end{equation}

In particular, if the forms $\theta _ j$ are semipositive, we may take $C=0$.

Proof. The decomposition $T_ j=\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ j$ exists by theorem 14.1.7. Since $\tau _ j\geq \theta _ j$ and $\tau _ j\geq 0$, $\varphi _ j$ is $\tau _ j$-plurisubharmonic, and so is every constant; hence $\max \{ \varphi _ j,-k\} $ is a bounded $\tau _ j$-plurisubharmonic function, and

\[ R_{j,k}=\tau _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\max \{ \varphi _ j,-k\} \]

is a closed positive current. By proposition 15.4.1, the total mass $\int _ XR_{1,k}\wedge \cdots \wedge R_{p,k}\wedge \omega ^{n-p}$ equals the right side of (15.14) for every $k$.

Local finiteness. Let $W$ be a relatively compact coordinate chart, and choose smooth functions $h_ j$ and $h$ near $\overline W$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h_ j=\theta _ j$ and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h=\omega $. Then $u_ j=\varphi _ j+h_ j$ is a plurisubharmonic potential of $T_ j$ on $W$. Let $B$ be an integer with $h_ j\leq B$ on $W$ for all $j$, fix an integer $l\geq 1$, and put $O_ l(u)=\bigcap _ j\{ u_ j>-l\} $. On $W\cap O_ l(u)$ we have $\varphi _ j>-l-B$, so for every integer $k\geq l+B$ the bounded plurisubharmonic functions $\max \{ u_ j,-l\} +Ch$ and $\max \{ \varphi _ j,-k\} +h_ j+Ch$ both equal $u_ j+Ch$ there; the second is a local potential of $R_{j,k}$. By plurifine locality,

\[ \mathbf1_{O_ l(u)}\bigwedge _ j(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\max \{ u_ j,-l\} +C\omega )=\mathbf1_{O_ l(u)}\bigwedge _ jR_{j,k}\quad \text{on }W. \]

Expanding the left side into positive terms gives

\[ \mathbf1_{O_ l(u)}\bigwedge _ j\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\max \{ u_ j,-l\} \leq \mathbf1_{O_ l(u)}\bigwedge _ j R_{j,k}\leq \bigwedge _ jR_{j,k}. \]

Let $K\subset W$ be compact. On a neighborhood of $K$ in $W$ we have $\beta \leq c\, \omega $ for some $c>0$ (lemma 13.1.1), so by the last display and (15.7), the trace masses on $K$ of the truncated local products are at most $c^{n-p}$ times the right side of (15.14), uniformly in $l$. Hence the non-pluripolar product is locally finite everywhere, and $\langle T_1\wedge \cdots \wedge T_ p\rangle $ is a closed positive current by theorem 15.2.1.

Mass bound. By the same argument, the currents $T_ j+C\omega =\tau _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ j$, with local potentials $u_ j+Ch$, have a locally finite non-pluripolar product. The sets $G_ k=\bigcap _ j\{ \varphi _ j>-k\} $ increase and exhaust the complement of the union of the pole sets of the $\varphi _ j$. On $G_ k$ the global truncations $\max \{ \varphi _ j,-k\} $ coincide with $\varphi _ j$.
