If $B'$ is an integer with $h_ j+Ch\geq -B'$ on $W$ for all $j$, then on $G_ k\cap W$ all local potentials satisfy $u_ j+Ch>-k-B'$, so they coincide there with their truncations at level $k+B'$, and also with the local potentials $\max \{ \varphi _ j,-k\} +h_ j+Ch$ of $R_{j,k}$. By plurifine locality and lemma 15.1.2, $\mathbf1_{G_ k}\bigwedge _ jR_{j,k}=\mathbf1_{G_ k}\langle \bigwedge _ j(T_ j+C\omega )\rangle $, and these restrictions increase to $\langle \bigwedge _ j(T_ j+C\omega )\rangle $. Their total masses against $\omega ^{n-p}$ are at most the right side of (15.14), hence so is that of the limit. Finally, expanding $\langle \bigwedge _ j(T_ j+C\omega )\rangle $ by multilinearity (proposition 15.1.5) into a sum of positive currents, one of which is $\langle \bigwedge _ jT_ j\rangle $, we obtain $\langle \bigwedge _ j T_ j\rangle \leq \langle \bigwedge _ j(T_ j+C\omega )\rangle $, which proves (15.14). $\square$

Example 15.4.4. On $\mathbb P^1$, the current of integration $[0]$ at the point $0$ satisfies $\langle [0]\rangle =0$. Indeed, near $0$ it has the potential $\log |z|^2$ (theorem 7.4.5), whose pole set is $\{ 0\} $, so $\langle [0]\rangle =\mathbf1_{\{ z\neq 0\} }[0]=0$ there by proposition 15.3.6; away from $0$ the current vanishes. Thus $[0]$ has mass one while $\langle [0]\rangle $ has mass zero: discarding the poles can change the cohomology class, even in degree one.

15.4.1 Mixed lower bounds

Lower bounds $\langle T_ i^ n\rangle \geq f_ i\nu $ by densities with respect to a common measure $\nu $ pass to mixed products, as they do for bounded potentials (theorem 9.11.1): we apply the bounded inequality to the truncated potentials and restrict to the set where no truncation is active. A pluripolar set charged by $\nu $ causes no difficulty, because there the densities $f_ i$ vanish $\nu $-almost everywhere.

Proposition 15.4.5. Let $T_1,\ldots ,T_ n$ be closed positive $(1,1)$-currents on a compact Kähler manifold $X$ of dimension $n\geq 1$, not necessarily distinct. Let $\nu $ be a positive Radon measure on $X$, and let $f_1,\ldots ,f_ n$ be nonnegative Borel functions on $X$ such that

\[ \langle T_ i^ n\rangle \geq f_ i\nu \qquad (1\leq i\leq n). \]

Then

\[ \langle T_1\wedge \cdots \wedge T_ n\rangle \geq \left(\prod _{i=1}^ n f_ i^{1/n}\right)\nu . \]

All the products are finite by theorem 15.4.3; in particular each $f_ i$ is $\nu $-integrable, and so is $\prod _ if_ i^{1/n}$.

Proof. For $n=1$ the conclusion is the hypothesis, so let $n\geq 2$. Work on a relatively compact coordinate domain on which $T_ i=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_ i$ with plurisubharmonic potentials $a_ i$ bounded above, and put

\[ a_{i,j}=\max \{ a_ i,-j\} ,\qquad G_ j=\bigcap _{i=1}^ n\{ a_ i>-j\} . \]

Since $G_ j$ is contained in the level-$j$ set $\{ a_ i>-j\} $ of the tuple $(a_ i,\ldots ,a_ i)$, lemma 15.1.2 shows that the bounded measure $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_{i,j})^ n$ agrees with $\langle T_ i^ n\rangle $ on $G_ j$. By positivity, therefore, on the whole coordinate domain

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_{i,j})^ n\geq \mathbf1_{G_ j}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_{i,j})^ n\geq \mathbf1_{G_ j}f_ i\nu \qquad (1\leq i\leq n). \]

Now theorem 9.11.1, applied to the densities $\mathbf1_{G_ j}f_ i$, gives $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_{1,j}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_{n,j}\geq \mathbf1_{G_ j}\prod _ if_ i^{1/n}\nu $. Since $G_ j$ is the level-$j$ set of the tuple $(a_1,\ldots ,a_ n)$, the left side agrees on $G_ j$ with the non-pluripolar product of the $T_ i$, by lemma 15.1.2. Restricting to $G_ j$ and using positivity (lemma 15.1.1), we obtain

\[ \langle T_1\wedge \cdots \wedge T_ n\rangle \geq \mathbf1_{G_ j}\prod _ i f_ i^{1/n}\nu . \]

The complement of $\bigcup _ jG_ j$ is the union $P$ of the pole sets of the $a_ i$. Every $\langle T_ i^ n\rangle $ gives zero mass to the whole pluripolar set $P$, not only to the pole set of $a_ i$, so the inequality $\langle T_ i^ n\rangle \geq f_ i\nu $ forces $f_ i=0$ $\nu $-almost everywhere on $P$, for every $i$. Hence $\mathbf1_{G_ j}\prod _ if_ i^{1/n}$ increases to $\prod _ if_ i^{1/n}$ $\nu $-almost everywhere as $j\to \infty $, and monotone convergence on every Borel subset of the coordinate domain removes the indicator. Since finitely many such coordinate domains cover $X$, and an inequality of measures that holds on each set of a finite open cover holds on $X$ (decompose a Borel set into Borel pieces, each contained in one set of the cover), the asserted inequality follows.

Finally, $\int _ Xf_ i\, \mathrm d\nu \leq \int _ X\langle T_ i^ n\rangle <\infty $. To integrate the product, we use the inequality between weighted geometric and arithmetic means: for $y_1,\ldots ,y_ m\in [0,\infty ]$ and weights $\lambda _ i>0$ with $\sum _ i\lambda _ i=1$,

15.15
\begin{equation} \prod _ iy_ i^{\lambda _ i}\leq \sum _ i\lambda _ iy_ i . \end{equation}

If all $y_ i$ are positive and finite, this is lemma 1.1.4 for the exponential function on the probability space $\{ 1,\ldots ,m\} $ with masses $\lambda _ i$, on which the integrand takes the values $\log y_ i$; otherwise the left side vanishes or the right side is infinite. With $\lambda _ i=1/n$, it gives $\int _ X\prod _ if_ i^{1/n}\, \mathrm d\nu \leq \frac1n\sum _ i\int _ Xf_ i\, \mathrm d\nu <\infty $. $\square$

Corollary 15.4.6. Let $T,S$ be closed positive $(1,1)$-currents on a compact Kähler manifold $X$ of dimension $n\geq 1$. Let $\nu $ be a positive Radon measure on $X$, and let $f,g$ be nonnegative Borel functions on $X$ such that

\[ \langle T^ n\rangle \geq f\nu ,\qquad \langle S^ n\rangle \geq g\nu . \]

Then for every integer $1\leq k\leq n-1$,

\[ \langle T^ k\wedge S^{n-k}\rangle \geq f^{k/n}g^{(n-k)/n}\nu . \]

The endpoint cases $k=0$ and $k=n$ are the hypotheses; for $n=1$ there are no other cases.

Proof. Apply proposition 15.4.5 with $k$ factors equal to $T$, with density $f$, and $n-k$ factors equal to $S$, with density $g$. $\square$

Corollary 15.4.7. Under the hypotheses of corollary 15.4.6, for every $0\leq t\leq 1$,

\[ \left\langle \big ((1-t)T+tS\big )^ n\right\rangle \geq \big ((1-t)f^{1/n}+tg^{1/n}\big )^ n\nu . \]

Proof. For $t=0$ and $t=1$ this is the hypothesis. Otherwise, multilinearity (proposition 15.1.5) expands the left side as $\sum _{k=0}^ n\binom nk(1-t)^ kt^{n-k}\langle T^ k\wedge S^{n-k}\rangle $. Bounding the terms with $1\leq k\leq n-1$ by corollary 15.4.6 and the terms with $k=0,n$ by the hypotheses, we obtain

\[ \left\langle \big ((1-t)T+tS\big )^ n\right\rangle \geq \sum _{k=0}^ n\binom nk(1-t)^ kt^{n-k}f^{k/n}g^{(n-k)/n}\, \nu =\big ((1-t)f^{1/n}+tg^{1/n}\big )^ n\nu \]

by the binomial formula. The measure on the right is finite, because $f$ and $g$ are $\nu $-integrable by the hypotheses and each density $f^{k/n}g^{(n-k)/n}$ is at most $\frac knf+\frac{n-k}ng\leq f+g$: for $k=0$ and $k=n$ this is an equality, and for $1\leq k\leq n-1$ it is (15.15). $\square$

15.5 Fiber integration and modifications

We now pass the fiber formula of lemma 15.3.5 through the truncations that define non-pluripolar products on a product of compact Kähler manifolds. The resulting identity, weighted by a bounded difference of potentials, is the form needed for relative energy in chapter 17. We also show that non-pluripolar products commute with modifications.

Corollary 15.5.1. Let $X$ and $Z$ be connected compact Kähler manifolds of dimensions $r$ and $m$, with projections $p_ X,p_ Z$ from $X\times Z$. Let $\theta $ be a smooth closed real $(1,1)$-form on $X$, let $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, and let $\gamma \geq 0$ be a smooth closed $(1,1)$-form on $Z$. Put

\[ \Theta =p_ X^*\theta +p_ Z^*\gamma ,\qquad \Phi =p_ X^*\varphi ,\qquad T=\Theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}W,\quad S=\Theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Phi , \]

where $W\in \mathrm{PSH}(X\times Z,\Theta )$ satisfies $\Phi -C\leq W\leq \Phi +C$ for a constant $C\geq 0$. Then for every $z\in Z$ the function $W_ z=W(\, \cdot \, ,z)$ belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, and for every integer $0\leq j\leq r$ and every smooth real $(m,m)$-form $\eta $ on $Z$,

15.16
\begin{equation} \begin{aligned} & \int _{X\times Z}(W-\Phi )\, p_ Z^*\eta \wedge \langle T^ j\wedge S^{r-j}\rangle \\ & \qquad =\int _ Z\left(\int _ X(W_ z-\varphi ) \langle (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}W_ z)^ j\wedge (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^{r-j}\rangle \right)\eta (z). \end{aligned} \end{equation}

The same identity holds with both difference weights replaced by $1$. The weights are undefined only on the common pole set of the two potentials involved, where they may be given any bounded values.

Proof. In product charts choose smooth local potentials $a(x)$ of $\theta $ and $b(z)$ of $\gamma $. Then $W+a+b$ is plurisubharmonic, so by proposition 8.2.3 its restriction $W_ z+a+b(z)$ to a fiber is plurisubharmonic in $x$ or identically $-\infty $; the second alternative is excluded, because $W_ z\geq \varphi -C$ and $\varphi $ is not identically $-\infty $ on any open set. Hence $W_ z$ is quasi-plurisubharmonic with $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}W_ z\geq 0$, that is, $W_ z\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. All the products in (15.16) are finite by theorem 15.4.3, and (15.14) bounds the fiber masses uniformly in $z$, because the shifted background can be chosen from the fixed form $\theta $, independently of $W_ z$.

Put $w=W+a+b$, $v=\Phi +a+b$ and $O_ k=\{ w>-k\} \cap \{ v>-k\} $ in a product chart. Let $\rho \geq 0$ be a smooth function with compact support in the chart, let $G\geq 0$ be bounded and Borel, and let $\eta \geq 0$. Apply (15.10) to the bounded functions $\max \{ w,-k\} $ and $\max \{ v,-k\} $, with multiplicities $j$ and $r-j$, and to the weight $\rho G\mathbf1_{O_ k}$. The set $O_ k$ is contained in the level-$k$ set of the tuple formed by $j$ copies of $w$ and $r-j$ copies of $v$ (if $j=0$ or $j=r$, one of the two conditions defining $O_ k$ concerns a potential that does not occur in the tuple). Hence, by lemma 15.1.2, the restriction to $O_ k$ of the bounded mixed product equals the restriction to $O_ k$ of the non-pluripolar product, on the total space and, with $(O_ k)_ z$ in place of $O_ k$, on every fiber. These restrictions increase with $k$, and they increase to the non-pluripolar products, because the complements of $\bigcup _ kO_ k$ and of $\bigcup _ k(O_ k)_ z$ are contained in the pole sets of $w,v$ and of $w_ z,v_ z$, which are pluripolar and carry no mass. Monotone convergence, on the total space and in $z$, therefore gives the fiber formula for the non-pluripolar products with weight $\rho G$.

Taking $G=1$ gives the unweighted identity. Since $\Phi -C\leq W\leq \Phi +C$, the potentials $W$ and $\Phi $ have the same pole set, and so do $W_ z$ and $\varphi $; take $G=W-\Phi +C\in [0,2C]$ off this set and $G=C$ on it, and subtract $C$ times the unweighted identity. Summing over a finite partition of unity by such functions $\rho $ (proposition 7.1.3), and writing a signed $\eta $ as a difference of nonnegative forms, gives (15.16). All integrals are finite by the uniform mass bound and $|W-\Phi |\leq C$, and the values assigned on the common pole set do not matter, because this set is pluripolar and carries no mass for any of the products. $\square$

Proposition 15.5.2. Let $\pi \colon \widetilde X\to X$ be a modification between compact Kähler manifolds, that is, a proper bimeromorphic holomorphic map, and let $1\leq p\leq \dim X$. For closed positive $(1,1)$-currents $T_1,\ldots ,T_ p$ on $X$,

\[ \pi _*\langle \pi ^*T_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \pi ^*T_ p\rangle =\langle T_1\wedge \cdots \wedge T_ p\rangle , \]

and for closed positive $(1,1)$-currents $R_1,\ldots ,R_ p$ on $\widetilde X$,

\[ \pi _*\langle R_1\wedge \cdots \wedge R_ p\rangle =\langle \pi _*R_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \pi _*R_ p\rangle . \]

In particular, in top degree the corresponding total masses agree.

Proof. The pullback $\pi ^*T_ j$ is the closed positive current of proposition 7.5.4, defined by pulling back local plurisubharmonic potentials; a pulled-back potential is not identically $-\infty $ on any open set, because $\pi $ is biholomorphic over a dense open set whose preimage is dense (proposition A.7.3 and the definition of bimeromorphic maps in section A.3). The pushforward $\pi _*R$ of a closed positive current is closed and positive, since pullback of smooth forms commutes with $\mathrm d$ and preserves strong positivity (proposition 7.2.1). All products in the statement are therefore defined, and they are finite by theorem 15.4.3.

Choose a nowhere dense analytic subset $A\subset X$ such that $\pi ^{-1}(A)$ is nowhere dense and $\pi $ restricts to a biholomorphism $\pi ^{-1}(X\setminus A)\to X\setminus A$. Both $A$ and $\pi ^{-1}(A)$ are pluripolar: near each point, a nowhere dense analytic set is contained in the zero set of a holomorphic function $f$ that does not vanish identically on a connected neighborhood, hence in the pole set of $\log |f|^2$. For a point $\widetilde x\in \pi ^{-1}(X\setminus A)$, use on a neighborhood of $\widetilde x$ the coordinates $z\circ \pi $, where $z$ are coordinates near $\pi (\widetilde x)$. In these coordinates $\pi $ is the identity, and the local potentials $u_ j\circ \pi $ of $\pi ^*T_ j$ are the local potentials $u_ j$ of $T_ j$. Since non-pluripolar products do not depend on the choice of coordinates or of local potentials, the two sides of the first identity agree on $X\setminus A$.

It remains to show that neither side charges $A$. The right side gives the pluripolar set $A$ zero mass. The product $S=\langle \pi ^*T_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \pi ^*T_ p\rangle $ gives $\pi ^{-1}(A)$ zero mass, and hence $\pi _*S$ gives $A$ zero mass. Indeed, in a chart of $X$, the trace measure of $\pi _*S$ is the image under $\pi $ of the positive measure $S\wedge \pi ^*\beta ^{n-p}$, which does not charge $\pi ^{-1}(A)$, and the coefficient measures of $\pi _*S$ are bounded by a multiple of this trace measure (theorem 7.2.5). Hence the two sides of the first identity have the same coefficient measures on $X\setminus A$ and on $A$, and they are equal. The same argument on $X\setminus A$, where now the local potentials of $\pi _*R_ j$ are those of $R_ j$ in the coordinates $z\circ \pi $, proves the second identity. In top degree, the total mass of $\pi _*S$ is $(\pi _*S)(1)=S(\pi ^*1)=S(1)$, which proves the assertion on masses. $\square$

15.6 Capacity convergence and loss of mass

Bedford–Taylor products of locally uniformly bounded potentials are continuous under convergence in capacity (theorem 9.4.8). For unbounded potentials this fails, because mass can escape into the poles in the limit (example 15.6.6). What survives is a lower semicontinuity statement, which becomes continuity when the total masses do not drop in the limit. Throughout, capacities are the local Monge–Ampère capacities $\operatorname {Cap}_ D$ of definition 9.3.2, used in coordinate charts as in definition 9.4.5, and not the global capacity $c_\omega $ of section 14.8. Accordingly, a Borel function on $X$ is quasi-continuous (definition 9.4.1), and a net of Borel functions on $X$ converges locally in capacity (definition 9.4.5), if this holds in the charts of a finite atlas whose charts have bounded images; by the following lemma, this does not depend on the atlas. We then extend convergence in capacity to functions with poles.

Lemma 15.6.1. Let $(W,z)$ and $(W',z')$ be holomorphic charts of a complex manifold whose images $D=z(W)$ and $D'=z'(W')$ are bounded, and let $K\subset W\cap W'$ be compact. Then there is a constant $C$ such that

\[ \operatorname {Cap}_{D'}^*(z'(E))\leq C\operatorname {Cap}_ D^*(z(E))\qquad \text{for every set }E\subset K, \]

and $\operatorname {Cap}_{D'}(z'(E))\leq C\operatorname {Cap}_ D(z(E))$ for every Borel set $E\subset K$. Consequently, for a net of Borel functions on a complex manifold, convergence locally in capacity in the charts of an atlas whose charts have bounded images implies the same in every chart with bounded image; similarly for quasi-continuity of a Borel function.

Proof. For every $x\in K$ choose $r_ x>0$ such that the closed ball $\overline{B(z(x),2r_ x)}$ lies in $z(W\cap W')$. Finitely many of the sets $z^{-1}(B(z(x_ i),r_ i))$, $r_ i=r_{x_ i}$, cover $K$; put $B_ i=B(z(x_ i),2r_ i)$ and $K_ i=K\cap z^{-1}(\overline{B(z(x_ i),r_ i)})$. The map $F_ i=z'\circ z^{-1}$ is a biholomorphism from $B_ i$ onto the bounded open set $B_ i'=F_ i(B_ i)\subset D'$. Let $E\subset K$ and $E_ i=E\cap K_ i$. Every competitor for $\operatorname {Cap}_{D'}$ restricts to a competitor for $\operatorname {Cap}_{B_ i'}$, so $\operatorname {Cap}_{D'}(G)\leq \operatorname {Cap}_{B_ i'}(G)$ for Borel sets $G\subset B_ i'$, and taking infima over open sets, $\operatorname {Cap}^*_{D'}\leq \operatorname {Cap}^*_{B_ i'}$ on subsets of $B_ i'$. By proposition 9.3.3(5) and (4), applied to the ball $B_ i\Subset D$ and the compact set $\overline{B(z(x_ i),r_ i)}\subset B_ i$, there is a constant $C_ i$ with

\[ \begin{aligned} \operatorname {Cap}^*_{D'}(z'(E_ i))& \leq \operatorname {Cap}^*_{B_ i'}(z'(E_ i))=\operatorname {Cap}^*_{B_ i}(z(E_ i))\\ & \leq C_ i\operatorname {Cap}^*_ D(z(E_ i))\leq C_ i\operatorname {Cap}^*_ D(z(E)). \end{aligned} \]

By subadditivity of $\operatorname {Cap}^*_{D'}$ (proposition 9.3.3), the assertion holds with $C=\sum _ iC_ i$. If $E$ is a Borel set, then so are $E_ i$, $z(E_ i)$ and $z'(E_ i)$, because $z$ and $z'$ are homeomorphisms onto their images. The restriction argument above and proposition 9.3.3(4),(5) also apply to the capacities of Borel sets, so the same chain of inequalities holds with $\operatorname {Cap}$ in place of $\operatorname {Cap}^*$, and the subadditivity of $\operatorname {Cap}_{D'}$ gives the second assertion with the same constant.

For the consequences, let $(W',z')$ be a chart with bounded image and $K'\subset W'$ compact. Cover $K'$ by finitely many compact sets $K_ i'\subset W'\cap W_ i$, where the $(W_ i,z_ i)$ are charts of the given atlas. Suppose that $(f_ a)$ converges to $f$ locally in capacity in the charts $W_ i$. By subadditivity, the outer capacity in $D'=z'(W')$ of the image of $K'\cap \{ f_ a>f+\delta \} $ is at most the sum over $i$ of the outer capacities of the images of $K_ i'\cap \{ f_ a>f+\delta \} $. By the first part, each of these is at most a constant times the corresponding outer capacity in $z_ i(W_ i)$, which tends to zero; similarly for $\{ f_ a<f-\delta \} $.

If $f$ is quasi-continuous in the charts $W_ i$ and $\varepsilon >0$, choose open sets $G_ i\subset z_ i(W_ i)$ of small capacity such that $f$ is continuous on $K_ i'\setminus z_ i^{-1}(G_ i)$. By the first part, $z'(K_ i'\cap z_ i^{-1}(G_ i))$ has outer capacity at most $C_ i\operatorname {Cap}_{z_ i(W_ i)}(G_ i)$ in $D'$, so it is contained in an open set $G_ i'\subset D'$ with $\operatorname {Cap}_{D'}(G_ i')<\varepsilon /N$, where $N$ is the number of sets $K_ i'$, provided the $G_ i$ are chosen small enough. Then $G'=\bigcup _ iG_ i'$ satisfies $\operatorname {Cap}_{D'}(G')<\varepsilon $, and the set $F=K'\setminus z'^{-1}(G')$ is the union of the finitely many relatively closed sets $F\cap K_ i'$, on each of which $f$ is continuous; hence $f$ is continuous on $F$. $\square$

Definition 15.6.2. For $s,t\in [-\infty ,\infty )$ put $d_*(s,t)=|\arctan s-\arctan t|$, where $\arctan (-\infty )=-\pi /2$. A net $(u_ a)$ of plurisubharmonic functions on a bounded open set $D\subset \mathbb C^ n$ converges in extended capacity to a plurisubharmonic function $u$ if, for every compact set $K\subset D$ and every $\delta >0$, the outer capacity of $K\cap \{ d_*(u_ a,u)>\delta \} $ tends to zero, the capacity being taken as in definition 9.4.5. A net of $\theta $-plurisubharmonic functions on a compact manifold converges in extended capacity if the local plurisubharmonic functions obtained by adding smooth local potentials of $\theta $ do so on the charts of a finite coordinate cover by charts with bounded images. This does not depend on the choice of the local potentials or of the cover, by lemma 15.6.3 below.

Lemma 15.6.3. Let $(u_ k)$ be a sequence of plurisubharmonic functions on a bounded open set $D\subset \mathbb C^ n$ that is locally uniformly bounded above, and let $u$ be plurisubharmonic on $D$. Then $u_ k\to u$ in extended capacity if and only if $\max \{ u_ k,-C\} \to \max \{ u,-C\} $ locally in capacity for every $C>0$. A decreasing sequence of plurisubharmonic functions with plurisubharmonic limit converges to it in extended capacity. Extended capacity convergence of $\theta $-plurisubharmonic functions on a compact manifold does not depend on the choices made in definition 15.6.2. If a sequence $(u_ k)$ in $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ converges in extended capacity to an element of $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, then the suprema $\sup _ Xu_ k$ are bounded above.

Proof. Fix a compact set $K$ and let $M$ be an upper bound for all $u_ k$ and $u$ on $K$. On $[-\infty ,M]$, the function $\max \{ s,-C\} $ is a Lipschitz function of $\arctan s$: it is constant for $\arctan s\leq \arctan (-C)$, and the derivative of the tangent is bounded on the compact interval $[\arctan (-C),\arctan M]$. Hence extended capacity convergence implies capacity convergence of the truncations. Conversely, $|\arctan s-\arctan \max \{ s,-C\} |\leq \pi /2-\arctan C$ for all $s$, so that

\[ d_*(u_ k,u)\leq |\max \{ u_ k,-C\} -\max \{ u,-C\} |+2(\pi /2-\arctan C), \]

where we also used $|\arctan s-\arctan t|\leq |s-t|$ for finite $s,t$. Choosing $C$ with $2(\pi /2-\arctan C)<\delta /2$, the set $\{ d_*(u_ k,u)>\delta \} $ is contained in $\{ |\max \{ u_ k,-C\} -\max \{ u,-C\} |>\delta /2\} $, which proves the equivalence.

Adding the same bounded real function $h$ to $s$ and $t$ changes $d_*$ by at most a bounded factor: since $1+\sigma ^2\leq 2(1+h^2)(1+(\sigma +h)^2)$, the formula $\arctan t-\arctan s=\int _ s^ t\mathrm d\sigma /(1+\sigma ^2)$ gives $d_*(s+h,t+h)\leq 2(1+h^2)d_*(s,t)$. Now let $(W_ i,h_ i)$ and $(W'_ l,h'_ l)$ be two choices of a finite cover of $X$ by charts with bounded images and of smooth local potentials $h_ i$ of $\theta $ on $W_ i$ and $h'_ l$ on $W'_ l$, and suppose that $u_ k+h_ i\to u+h_ i$ in extended capacity on every $W_ i$. Fix $l$, a compact set $K\subset W'_ l$ and $\delta >0$, and cover $K$ by finitely many compact sets $K_ i\subset W_ i\cap W'_ l$. The smooth function $h'_ l-h_ i$ is bounded on $K_ i$, say by $H_ i$, so the inequality just proved, applied with $h=h'_ l-h_ i$, gives

\[ K_ i\cap \{ d_*(u_ k+h'_ l,u+h'_ l)>\delta \} \subset K_ i\cap \{ d_*(u_ k+h_ i,u+h_ i)>\delta /(2+2H_ i^2)\} . \]

By lemma 15.6.1, the outer capacity of the left side, computed in the chart $W'_ l$, is at most a constant times the outer capacity of the right side, computed in $W_ i$, which tends to zero. By subadditivity of outer capacity, the outer capacity of $K\cap \{ d_*(u_ k+h'_ l,u+h'_ l)>\delta \} $ in $W'_ l$ tends to zero. Thus extended capacity convergence does not depend on the choice of the cover or of the local potentials.

If $u_ k$ decreases to $u$, then for every $C$ the truncations decrease to the bounded plurisubharmonic function $\max \{ u,-C\} $, so they converge in capacity by proposition 9.4.6; the equivalence just proved gives extended capacity convergence.

For the global assertion, put $m_ k=\sup _ Xu_ k$ and $v_ k=u_ k-m_ k$. By proposition 14.2.1, $\int _ X|v_ k|\, \omega ^ n\leq M_0$ for a constant $M_0$. Suppose that $m_ k\to +\infty $ along a subsequence, to which we pass. By Markov's inequality, the $\omega ^ n$-volume of $\{ u_ k<m_ k/2\} =\{ v_ k<-m_ k/2\} $ is at most $2M_0/m_ k$, which tends to zero. On the other hand, let $(W,z)$ be one of the charts of definition 15.6.2, with smooth local potential $h$ of $\theta $, and let $K\subset W$ be a compact set of positive volume, with $|h|\leq H$ and $u+h\leq M$ on $K$. On $K\cap \{ u_ k\geq m_ k/2\} $ we have $u_ k+h\geq m_ k/2-H$, so for large $k$ this set is contained in $K\cap \{ d_*(u_ k+h,u+h)>\delta _0\} $, where $\delta _0=(\pi /2-\arctan M)/2$. The outer capacity in $z(W)$ of the image of the latter set tends to zero, and hence so does its Lebesgue measure, by proposition 9.3.3(3). Since Lebesgue measure in the chart and the $\omega ^ n$-volume are comparable on $K$, the volume of $K\cap \{ u_ k\geq m_ k/2\} $ tends to zero, whereas by the first observation it tends to the volume of $K$, which is positive. This contradiction shows that the suprema are bounded above. $\square$

The lower semicontinuity of non-pluripolar products under convergence in capacity is proved with the following cutoff weights. They vanish where one of the potentials is at most $-C$, and multiplied by them, non-pluripolar products become Bedford–Taylor products of bounded potentials.

Lemma 15.6.4. Let $D\subset \mathbb C^ n$ be a bounded open set and $1\leq p\leq n$. For $1\leq j\leq p$ let $(v_{j,k})_{k\geq 1}$ be a sequence of plurisubharmonic functions on $D$ that is locally uniformly bounded above and converges in extended capacity to a plurisubharmonic function $v_ j$, and suppose that the non-pluripolar products $R_ k=\langle \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_{1,k}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_{p,k}\rangle $ and $R=\langle \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ p\rangle $ are locally finite. For $C,\varepsilon >0$ put

\[ v_{j,k}^ C=\max \{ v_{j,k},-C\} ,\qquad a_{j,k}=\frac{v_{j,k}^ C+C}{v_{j,k}^ C+C+\varepsilon },\qquad a_ k=\prod _ j a_{j,k}, \]

and define $v_ j^ C$, $a_ j$ and $a$ from the $v_ j$ in the same way. Then:

  1. the functions $a_ k$ and $a$ are Borel and take values in $[0,1]$, the function $a$ is quasi-continuous, and $a_ k\to a$ locally in capacity as $k\to \infty $;

  2. $a_ kR_ k=a_ k\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_{1,k}^ C\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_{p,k}^ C$ and $aR=a\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_1^ C\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ p^ C$, where the products on the right are Bedford–Taylor products;

  3. for fixed $C$, the weight $a$ increases to the indicator function of $\bigcap _ j\{ v_ j>-C\} $ as $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$, and these indicator functions increase to the indicator function of the complement of the union of the pole sets of the $v_ j$ as $C\to \infty $.

Proof. (1) The weights are Borel functions of the Borel functions $v_{j,k}$ and $v_ j$, and they take values in $[0,1]$ because $v_{j,k}^ C+C\geq 0$ and $v_ j^ C+C\geq 0$. Let $K\subset D$ be compact and $\delta >0$. By theorem 9.4.2 there are open sets $G_ j\subset D$ with $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(G_ j)<\delta /p$ such that $v_ j$ is continuous on $K\setminus G_ j$. Then $a$, a continuous function of $v_1^ C,\ldots ,v_ p^ C$, is continuous on $K\setminus \bigcup _ jG_ j$, and $\operatorname {Cap}_ D(\bigcup _ jG_ j)<\delta $ by subadditivity; thus $a$ is quasi-continuous. By lemma 15.6.3, $v_{j,k}^ C\to v_ j^ C$ locally in capacity. Since $s\mapsto (s+C)/(s+C+\varepsilon )$ is Lipschitz with constant $1/\varepsilon $ on $[-C,\infty )$, and since $|\prod _ jb_ j-\prod _ jb_ j'|\leq \sum _ j|b_ j-b_ j'|$ for numbers in $[0,1]$, the set where $|a_ k-a|>\delta $ is contained in the union over $j$ of the sets where $|v_{j,k}^ C-v_ j^ C|>\varepsilon \delta /p$. By subadditivity of outer capacity, $a_ k\to a$ locally in capacity.

(2) Fix $k$, and let $O=\bigcap _ j\{ v_{j,k}>-C\} $, a plurifine open set. For every integer $m\geq C$, the set $O$ is contained in the level-$m$ set $O_ m=\bigcap _ j\{ v_{j,k}>-m\} $ of the tuple $(v_{j,k})_ j$, and on $O$ the truncations $\max \{ v_{j,k},-m\} $ coincide with $v_{j,k}$, hence with $v_{j,k}^ C$. By lemma 15.1.2, $\mathbf1_{O_ m}R_ k$ is the Bedford–Taylor product of the truncations at level $m$ restricted to $O_ m$, and by theorem 10.4.3 this product coincides on $O$ with $\bigwedge _ j\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_{j,k}^ C$. Hence $\mathbf1_ OR_ k=\mathbf1_ O\bigwedge _ j\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_{j,k}^ C$. The weight $a_ k$ vanishes outside $O$, because $a_{j,k}=0$ wherever $v_{j,k}\leq -C$, and multiplying by $a_ k$ gives the first identity. The same argument, applied to the tuple $(v_ j)_ j$, gives the second.

(3) Where $v_ j>-C$ we have $v_ j^ C+C>0$, so $a_ j$ increases to $1$ as $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$; where $v_ j\leq -C$ we have $a_ j=0$. Hence $a$ increases to the indicator function of $\bigcap _ j\{ v_ j>-C\} $, and these sets increase to $\bigcap _ j\{ v_ j>-\infty \} $ as $C\to \infty $. $\square$

Theorem 15.6.5. Let $T_{j,k}=\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{j,k}$ and $T_ j=\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j$ be closed positive currents on $X$, for $1\leq j\leq p\leq n$ and $k\geq 1$, and suppose that $u_{j,k}\to u_ j$ in extended capacity as $k\to \infty $, for every $j$. Put $R_ k=\langle T_{1,k}\wedge \cdots \wedge T_{p,k}\rangle $ and $R=\langle T_1\wedge \cdots \wedge T_ p\rangle $. Then for every smooth strongly positive $(n-p,n-p)$-form $\eta $ on $X$,

\[ \varliminf _ k\int _ X R_ k\wedge \eta \geq \int _ X R\wedge \eta . \]

If moreover $\varlimsup _ k\int _ X R_ k\wedge \omega ^{n-p}\leq \int _ X R\wedge \omega ^{n-p}$, then $R_ k\to R$ weakly. The lower inequality also holds locally, on any complex manifold and for compactly supported $\eta $, whenever the local products are finite and the potentials converge in extended capacity and are locally uniformly bounded above.

Proof. Lower inequality. Work on a coordinate ball $B$ such that $\overline B$ lies in a larger coordinate ball, add fixed smooth local potentials of the $\theta _ j$, defined near $\overline B$, and denote the resulting plurisubharmonic functions by $v_{j,k}$ and $v_ j$. By lemma 15.6.3 (including its independence assertion, which allows us to use the chart $B$), $v_{j,k}\to v_ j$ in extended capacity on $B$, and the $v_{j,k}$ are uniformly bounded above on $B$, because the suprema of the $u_{j,k}$ are bounded above. The local non-pluripolar products of the $v_{j,k}$ and of the $v_ j$ are the restrictions of $R_ k$ and $R$ to $B$, and they are finite by theorem 15.4.3. For $C,\varepsilon >0$ let $v_{j,k}^ C$, $v_ j^ C$, $a_ k$ and $a$ be as in lemma 15.6.4. By that lemma, the weights take values in $[0,1]$, $a$ is quasi-continuous, $a_ k\to a$ locally in capacity, and

\[ a_ k R_ k=a_ k\bigwedge _ j\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_{j,k}^ C,\qquad aR=a\bigwedge _ j\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ j^ C \]

on $B$. Moreover, the plurisubharmonic functions $v_{j,k}^ C\geq -C$ are uniformly bounded on $B$, and they converge to $v_ j^ C$ locally in capacity, by lemma 15.6.3.

Let $\chi \geq 0$ be a smooth function with compact support in $B$. By the weighted capacity continuity of theorem 9.4.8,

\[ \int \chi \, aR\wedge \eta =\lim _ k\int \chi \, a_ kR_ k\wedge \eta \leq \varliminf _ k\int \chi R_ k\wedge \eta , \]

where the inequality holds because $0\leq a_ k\leq 1$ and $R_ k\wedge \chi \eta $ is a positive measure. Now let $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ and then $C\to \infty $. By lemma 15.6.4(3), the weights $a$ increase to the indicator function of the complement of the union of the pole sets of the $v_ j$, which $R$ does not charge; so monotone convergence gives $\int \chi R\wedge \eta \leq \varliminf _ k\int \chi R_ k\wedge \eta $. This is the local lower inequality; it used only the finiteness of the local products, the extended capacity convergence and the local upper bounds. A finite partition of unity by such functions $\chi $ (proposition 7.1.3) gives the global inequality.

Convergence. By (15.14), the masses $\int _ XR_ k\wedge \omega ^{n-p}$ are bounded, since the shifted backgrounds depend only on the fixed forms $\theta _ j$. Near a compact subset $L$ of a chart with Euclidean form $\beta $, we have $\beta \leq c\, \omega $ for some $c>0$ (lemma 13.1.1), and hence $R_ k\wedge \beta ^{n-p}\leq c^{n-p}R_ k\wedge \omega ^{n-p}$ by (15.7). Therefore the trace measures of the $R_ k$ are locally uniformly bounded, and by theorem 7.2.5 every subsequence of $(R_ k)$ has a weakly convergent subsequence. Its limit $R'$ is positive, and $R'-R$ is positive, by the lower inequality applied to all smooth strongly positive forms $\eta $ on $X$. The additional hypothesis gives $\int _ X(R'-R)\wedge \omega ^{n-p}\leq 0$, because $\omega ^{n-p}$ is a test form on the compact manifold $X$. Hence the positive measure $(R'-R)\wedge \omega ^{n-p}$ vanishes, so the trace measures of $R'-R$, which are bounded on compact subsets of charts by multiples of it, vanish as well, and $R'-R=0$ by theorem 7.2.5. Thus every subsequence of $(R_ k)$ has a further subsequence converging weakly to $R$, which proves $R_ k\to R$. $\square$

Example 15.6.6. On $\mathbb P^1$ let $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$ be the Fubini–Study form of example 14.5.6, which has total mass one, and put $u=\log |z|^2-\log (1+|z|^2)$ and $u_ k=\max \{ u,-k\} $. As computed in that example, $u$ extends smoothly across infinity and $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u=[0]$. Each $u_ k$ is bounded and $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$-plurisubharmonic, so $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ k$ has mass one by proposition 15.4.1. The sequence $u_ k$ decreases to $u$, hence converges to it in extended capacity (lemma 15.6.3), and the measures $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ k$ converge weakly to $[0]$, because $u_ k\to u$ in $L^1$. However, $\langle [0]\rangle =0$ by example 15.4.4. The weak limit retains the mass that the non-pluripolar product discards at the pole, so the mass hypothesis in theorem 15.6.5 cannot be omitted.

15.6.1 Ordered error sets, monotone nets and weights

Convergence locally in capacity in the sense of definition 9.4.5 controls the two one-sided error sets $\{ u_ a>u+\delta \} $ and $\{ u_ a<u-\delta \} $, while extended capacity convergence controls the arctangent distance. For plurisubharmonic functions the two notions coincide, even when the limit has poles; the comparison uses the quasi-continuity of the limit. We then show that monotone nets of plurisubharmonic functions converge in this sense, and we extend theorem 15.6.5 to varying bounded weights.

Proposition 15.6.7. Let $(u_ a)$ be a net of plurisubharmonic functions and $u$ a plurisubharmonic function on a bounded open set $D\subset \mathbb C^ n$, $n\geq 1$. Then $u_ a\to u$ locally in capacity in the sense of definition 9.4.5 if and only if $u_ a\to u$ in extended capacity. A decreasing net whose pointwise limit $u$ is plurisubharmonic converges to $u$ in this sense. So does an increasing net whose regularized upper envelope $u=(\sup _ au_ a)^*$ is plurisubharmonic; in particular, an increasing net that converges almost everywhere to a plurisubharmonic function $u$ converges to $u$ in this sense.

Proof. Equivalence. Fix a compact set $K\subset D$ and $\varepsilon >0$. By theorem 9.4.2 there is an open set $G$ of outer capacity less than $\varepsilon $, containing the poles of $u$ near $K$, such that $u$ is finite and continuous on the compact set $F=K\setminus G$. Then $u$ is bounded on $F$, say $-C\leq u\leq M$. For $\delta >0$, the two continuous positive functions

\[ s\longmapsto \arctan (s+\delta )-\arctan s, \qquad s\longmapsto \arctan s-\arctan (s-\delta ) \]

have a common positive lower bound $\kappa $ on $[-C,M]$. Hence, at a point of $F$, an ordered error of size $\delta $ in either direction implies $d_*(u_ a,u)>\kappa /2$. By subadditivity, the outer capacities of the two ordered error sets in $K$ are at most $\varepsilon $ plus the outer capacity of $K\cap \{ d_*(u_ a,u)>\kappa /2\} $. Thus extended capacity convergence, followed by $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$, gives convergence in the ordered sense. Conversely, $|\arctan s-\arctan t|\leq |s-t|$ for finite $s,t$; if one value is $-\infty $ and the other finite, there is an ordered error for every finite threshold; and equal infinite values have extended distance zero. Hence the set where $d_*(u_ a,u)>\delta $ is contained in the union of the two ordered error sets of size $\delta $, and subadditivity proves the converse.

Decreasing nets. Let $u_ a$ decrease to $u$, so that $u_ a\geq u$. With $F$ and $G$ as above, the functions $u_ a-u$ are finite and upper semicontinuous on the compact set $F$, and decrease pointwise to zero. Hence the closed sets $\{ u_ a-u\geq \delta \} \cap F$ have empty intersection, so finitely many of them already have empty intersection, and since the net is decreasing, all sets with a sufficiently large index are empty. Consequently, for large $a$ the only ordered error in $K$ lies in $G$, whose capacity is less than $\varepsilon $. This proves convergence. Only compactness is used, so the argument applies to nets and not only to sequences.

Increasing nets. Let the net increase, with $u=(\sup _ au_ a)^*$ plurisubharmonic. By theorem 8.3.2, there are countably many indices $b_1,b_2,\ldots $ such that $(\sup _ iu_{b_ i})^*=u$. Since the index set is directed, there is an increasing sequence of indices $a_ k$ with $a_ k\geq b_1,\ldots ,b_ k$, so that $u_{a_ k}\geq \max \{ u_{b_1},\ldots ,u_{b_ k}\} $. Since every $u_ a\leq \sup _ au_ a\leq u$, the regularized supremum of the sequence $(u_{a_ k})$ is $u$, and by theorem 8.3.2 the increasing limit $\lim _ ku_{a_ k}=\sup _ ku_{a_ k}$ equals $u$ almost everywhere. Hence, for every $C>0$, the truncations $\max \{ u_{a_ k},-C\} $, which lie between $-C$ and $\max \{ u,-C\} $, are locally uniformly bounded and increase almost everywhere to $\max \{ u,-C\} $, so they converge in capacity by theorem 9.5.1. By lemma 15.6.3 and the equivalence just proved, the sequence $(u_{a_ k})$ converges to $u$ locally in capacity in the ordered sense. For $a\geq a_ k$, monotonicity gives

\[ \{ u_ a<u-\delta \} \subseteq \{ u_{a_ k}<u-\delta \} , \qquad \{ u_ a>u+\delta \} =\varnothing , \]

so the estimates for the sequence give convergence of the whole net; the indices $a_ k$ need not be cofinal. Finally, suppose that the increasing net converges to a plurisubharmonic function $u$ outside a Lebesgue null set $N$. For $x\notin N$ we have $\sup _ au_ a(x)=\lim _ au_ a(x)=u(x)$, so $u_ a\leq u$ almost everywhere, and hence everywhere: $\max \{ u_ a,u\} $ and $u$ are plurisubharmonic and agree almost everywhere, so they agree everywhere (theorem 8.2.1, theorem 3.2.2). Thus $v=\sup _ au_ a\leq u$, so that $v^*\leq u$, as $u$ is upper semicontinuous. Moreover $v^*$ is plurisubharmonic and equals $v$, hence $u$, almost everywhere, by theorem 8.3.2; therefore $v^*=u$, and the previous case applies. $\square$

Corollary 15.6.8. Let $T_{j,k}$, $T_ j$, $1\leq j\leq p\leq n$, and $R_ k$, $R$ be as in theorem 15.6.5. Let $\chi _ k$ and $\chi $ be Borel functions on $X$ with $0\leq \chi _ k,\chi \leq A<\infty $, where $\chi $ is quasi-continuous, and suppose that $\chi _ k\to \chi $ locally in capacity. Then for every smooth strongly positive $(n-p,n-p)$-form $\eta $ on $X$,

\[ \varliminf _ k\int _ X\chi _ k R_ k\wedge \eta \geq \int _ X\chi R\wedge \eta . \]

If moreover

\[ \varlimsup _ k\int _ XR_ k\wedge \omega ^{n-p} \leq \int _ XR\wedge \omega ^{n-p}, \]

then $\chi _ kR_ k\to \chi R$ weakly as currents, and weakly as Radon measures when $p=n$; this holds in particular when $\int _ XR_ k\wedge \omega ^{n-p}\to \int _ XR\wedge \omega ^{n-p}$. The lower inequality also holds locally for finite local products whose potentials are locally uniformly bounded above. By proposition 15.6.7, the hypothesis of extended capacity convergence is satisfied when, for each $j$, the potentials $u_{j,k}$ decrease to $u_ j$, or increase to $u_ j$ almost everywhere.

Proof. Lower inequality. Let $B$ be a coordinate ball such that $\overline B$ lies in a larger coordinate ball, add fixed smooth local potentials of the $\theta _ j$, defined near $\overline B$, and denote the resulting plurisubharmonic functions on $B$ by $v_{j,k}$ and $v_ j$. By lemma 15.6.3, $v_{j,k}\to v_ j$ in extended capacity on $B$, and the $v_{j,k}$ are uniformly bounded above on $B$, because the suprema of the $u_{j,k}$ are bounded above; the local products are finite by theorem 15.4.3. For $C,\varepsilon >0$ let $v_{j,k}^ C$, $v_ j^ C$ and $a_ k$, $a$ be the truncations and cutoff weights of lemma 15.6.4; thus $0\leq a_ k,a\leq 1$, $a$ is quasi-continuous and $a_ k\to a$ locally in capacity. We may assume $A>0$. The bounded Borel weights $\chi _ ka_ k$ converge locally in capacity to the bounded quasi-continuous function $\chi a$, because $|\chi _ ka_ k-\chi a|\leq A|a_ k-a|+|\chi _ k-\chi |$, so that the set where $|\chi _ ka_ k-\chi a|>\delta $ is contained in the union of the sets where $|a_ k-a|>\delta /(2A)$ and where $|\chi _ k-\chi |>\delta /2$. The product $\chi a$ is quasi-continuous, since $\chi $ and $a$ are continuous outside open sets of arbitrarily small capacity and capacity is subadditive.

By lemma 15.6.4(2) and $0\leq a_ k\leq 1$,

\[ \chi _ kR_ k\geq \chi _ ka_ kR_ k =\chi _ ka_ k\bigwedge _{j=1}^ p\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_{j,k}^ C. \]

The functions $v_{j,k}^ C\geq -C$ are uniformly bounded on $B$ and converge to $v_ j^ C$ locally in capacity (lemma 15.6.3). By the weighted capacity continuity of bounded products (theorem 9.4.8), the right side converges weakly to $\chi a\bigwedge _ j\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ j^ C=\chi aR$, the last identity by lemma 15.6.4(2). Testing against $g\eta $ for a nonnegative test function $g$ on $B$, we obtain $\int g\chi aR\wedge \eta \leq \varliminf _ k\int g\chi _ kR_ k\wedge \eta $, and we let $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ and then $C\to \infty $. By lemma 15.6.4(3), the weight $a$ increases to one outside the union of the pole sets, which $R$ does not charge, so monotone convergence gives the local lower inequality, and a finite partition of unity (proposition 7.1.3) gives the global one. The local argument uses only the local upper bounds, the extended capacity convergence and the finiteness of the local products.

Convergence. Under the additional mass hypothesis, theorem 15.6.5 gives $R_ k\to R$ weakly, and in particular $\int _ XR_ k\wedge \eta \to \int _ XR\wedge \eta $. Apply the lower inequality also to the weights $A-\chi _ k$, which converge in capacity to $A-\chi $. Since

\[ \int _ X\chi _ kR_ k\wedge \eta =A\int _ XR_ k\wedge \eta -\int _ X(A-\chi _ k)R_ k\wedge \eta , \]

this gives the matching upper limit, so $\int _ X\chi _ kR_ k\wedge \eta \to \int _ X\chi R\wedge \eta $.

Every smooth real $(n-p,n-p)$-form $\eta $ on $X$ is the difference of two smooth strongly positive forms. Indeed, if $(\rho _ i)$ is a finite partition of unity subordinate to coordinate charts (proposition 7.1.3), then $\rho _ i\eta =\sum _ lf_ le_ l$ with smooth real functions $f_ l$ supported in $\operatorname{Supp}\rho _ i$ and constant strongly positive forms $e_ l$, which span the real forms by proposition 7.2.1. If $\kappa _ i\geq 0$ is a test function of the chart equal to one on $\operatorname{Supp}\rho _ i$ and $c_ l\geq \sup |f_ l|$, then $f_ le_ l=(f_ l+c_ l\kappa _ i)e_ l-c_ l\kappa _ ie_ l$ is a difference of smooth strongly positive forms. Hence the convergence holds for all smooth real forms, and by linearity for all smooth forms, so $\chi _ kR_ k\to \chi R$ weakly as currents. Since $\chi _ k\leq A$ and the trace masses of $R_ k$ are bounded, the coefficient measures of $\chi _ kR_ k$ have bounded total variation; when $p=n$, approximating continuous functions on the compact manifold $X$ uniformly by smooth ones, using a partition of unity and lemma 1.4.1, upgrades the convergence to weak convergence of Radon measures. Finally, for monotone sequences the extended capacity hypothesis follows from proposition 15.6.7, applied to the local potentials. $\square$

15.7 Mass comparison and projective fibers

Recall from definition 14.2.6 that $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ is more singular than $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, written $\psi \preceq \varphi $, if $\psi \leq \varphi +C$ for a constant $C$; we also write this as $\varphi \geq \psi -O(1)$. We now prove that the total mass of a non-pluripolar product does not increase when the potentials become more singular. If all potentials are locally bounded outside a closed set $A$ that is the pole set of a quasi-plurisubharmonic function, this follows from Stokes' formula, because the relevant difference of potentials can be arranged to have compact support in $X\setminus A$. The general case is reduced to this one by a construction on $X\times \mathbb P^ N$: the new potential is bounded outside a fixed pole set, and its total mass is an average of masses along a segment of potentials, with weights that concentrate at the endpoint as $N\to \infty $.

Lemma 15.7.1. Let $\varphi _ j,\psi _ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta _ j)$ for $1\leq j\leq p\leq n$, and put $T_ j=\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ j$ and $T'_ j=\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi _ j$. Suppose that $\varphi _ j\geq \psi _ j-O(1)$ for every $j$. Assume moreover that there is a quasi-plurisubharmonic function $\tau $ on $X$ such that $A=\{ \tau =-\infty \} $ is closed and $\tau $ and all the $\varphi _ j,\psi _ j$ are locally bounded on $X\setminus A$. Then

\[ \int _ X\langle T_1\wedge \cdots \wedge T_ p\rangle \wedge \omega ^{n-p} \geq \int _ X\langle T'_1\wedge \cdots \wedge T'_ p\rangle \wedge \omega ^{n-p}. \]

If every difference $\varphi _ j-\psi _ j$ is bounded, equality holds.

Proof. We replace the factors $T_ j$ by $T'_ j$ one at a time; the intermediate tuples satisfy the same hypotheses, so it suffices to treat one step, in which a single factor changes. Write it as $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $ before and $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi $ after the change, and let $\Theta $ be the non-pluripolar product of the remaining factors (some primed, some not) wedged with $\omega ^{n-p}$. By theorem 15.4.3, theorem 15.2.1, $\Theta $ is a closed positive current of bidimension $(1,1)$, and it gives zero mass to the set $A$, which is pluripolar and Borel, being closed and locally contained in the pole sets of plurisubharmonic functions.

On the open set $X\setminus A$ all potentials are locally bounded, so there the non-pluripolar products of the remaining factors are Bedford–Taylor products, and by proposition 15.3.3 the products of the two tuples, wedged with $\omega ^{n-p}$, are the Bedford–Taylor products $(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )\wedge \Theta $ and $(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi )\wedge \Theta $ with base $\Theta $; here $(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )\wedge \Theta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(h+\varphi )\wedge \Theta $ for smooth local potentials $h$ of $\theta $. More generally, if $\Omega '$ is a smooth closed real $(1,1)$-form and $\varrho \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\Omega ')$ is locally bounded on $X\setminus A$, then the measure $(\Omega '+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varrho )\wedge \Theta $ on $X\setminus A$ is the restriction of a global non-pluripolar product wedged with $\omega ^{n-p}$, so it has finite mass by theorem 15.4.3. Since none of these products charges $A$, it suffices to prove

\[ \int _{X\setminus A}(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi )\wedge \Theta \leq \int _{X\setminus A}(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )\wedge \Theta . \]

We prove it by applying Stokes' formula on $X\setminus A$ to functions that equal $\varphi -k$ near $A$. After adding constants we may assume $\varphi \geq \psi $ and $\tau \leq 0$. Since $\tau $ is quasi-plurisubharmonic and $X$ is compact, there is $C_\tau \geq 0$ with $C_\tau \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\tau \geq 0$ (lemma 13.1.4(4)). Put $\Omega =(C_\tau +1)\omega $. Fix $0<\varepsilon \leq 1$ and put $f_ k=\max \{ \psi +\varepsilon \tau ,\varphi -k\} $. Since $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi \geq 0$ and $\varepsilon (C_\tau \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\tau )\geq 0$, both $\psi +\varepsilon \tau $ and $\varphi -k$ are $(\theta +\Omega )$-plurisubharmonic, hence so is $f_ k$, and $f_ k$ is locally bounded on $X\setminus A$. Since $\psi +\varepsilon \tau -\varphi \leq \varepsilon \tau $, we have $f_ k=\varphi -k$ on the open neighborhood $\{ \tau <-k/\varepsilon \} $ of $A$. Hence on $X\setminus A$ the function $g_ k=f_ k-\varphi +k=\max \{ \psi +\varepsilon \tau -\varphi +k,0\} $ takes values in $[0,k]$, and it vanishes outside the compact set $L_ k=\{ \tau \geq -k/\varepsilon \} \subset X\setminus A$.

Stokes' formula. With a smooth local potential $h$ of $\theta +\Omega $, the Bedford–Taylor recursion gives, on $X\setminus A$,

\[ (\theta +\Omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f_ k)\wedge \Theta -(\theta +\Omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )\wedge \Theta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\bigl ((f_ k+h)\Theta \bigr )-\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\bigl ((\varphi +h)\Theta \bigr )=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(g_ k\Theta ), \]

because $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(k\Theta )=0$. The signed measure $\lambda _ k=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(g_ k\Theta )$ vanishes outside $L_ k$. Let $\kappa $ be a smooth function with compact support in $X\setminus A$ that equals one on a neighborhood of $L_ k$. Then $\lambda _ k(X\setminus A)=\int \kappa \, \mathrm d\lambda _ k=(g_ k\Theta )(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\kappa )=0$, because $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\kappa $ vanishes near the support of $g_ k\Theta $. Since the measures on the left have finite mass, we obtain

\[ \int _{X\setminus A}(\theta +\Omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f_ k)\wedge \Theta =\int _{X\setminus A}(\theta +\Omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )\wedge \Theta . \]

Passage to the limit. On $X\setminus A$ the functions $f_ k$ decrease to the locally bounded function $\psi +\varepsilon \tau $ as $k\to \infty $. By decreasing continuity with the fixed base $\Theta $ (theorem 9.2.5), applied locally, the positive measures $(\theta +\Omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f_ k)\wedge \Theta $ converge weakly on $X\setminus A$ to $(\theta +\Omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\psi +\varepsilon \tau ))\wedge \Theta $. The mass of the limit on the open set $X\setminus A$ is at most the lower limit of the masses, because it is the supremum of the integrals of test functions $0\leq \kappa \leq 1$ supported in $X\setminus A$, and each such integral is the limit of the corresponding integrals for the approximating measures. Therefore

\[ \int _{X\setminus A}(\theta +\Omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\psi +\varepsilon \tau ))\wedge \Theta \leq \int _{X\setminus A}(\theta +\Omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )\wedge \Theta . \]

By additivity of Bedford–Taylor products, the measure on the left is the sum of the positive measures $(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi )\wedge \Theta $, $\varepsilon (C_\tau \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\tau )\wedge \Theta $ and $(\Omega -\varepsilon C_\tau \omega )\wedge \Theta $, and the measure on the right is the sum of $(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )\wedge \Theta $ and $\Omega \wedge \Theta $; all these measures have finite mass on $X\setminus A$. Dropping the second term on the left, we get

\[ \int _{X\setminus A}(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi )\wedge \Theta \leq \int _{X\setminus A}(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )\wedge \Theta +\varepsilon C_\tau \int _ X\omega \wedge \Theta , \]

and letting $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ proves the desired inequality. If the differences are bounded, the same argument with the roles of $\varphi $ and $\psi $ exchanged gives equality. $\square$

Lemma 15.7.2. Let $U\subset \mathbb C^ n$ be open, let $u$ be smooth and plurisubharmonic and $v$ plurisubharmonic on $U$, and let $N\geq 1$ be an integer. Put $\Sigma _ N=\{ x\in \mathbb R^ N:x_ i\geq 0,\ t(x)=\sum _ i x_ i\leq 1\} $, and define on $U\times \mathbb C^ N$, with coordinates $(w,z)$,

\[ F=\sup _{x\in \Sigma _ N} \left((1-t(x))u+t(x)v+ \sum _ i x_ i\log |z_ i|^2-\sum _ i x_ i^2\right), \]

with the convention that a zero coefficient times $-\infty $ is zero. Then $F$ is plurisubharmonic and locally bounded on $U\times \mathbb C^ N$, and $F\geq u$. If $v$ is locally bounded, then, with $p\colon U\times \mathbb C^ N\to U$ the projection,

15.21
\begin{equation} p_*(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^{n+N} =\binom {n+N}{n}N\int _0^1 (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}((1-t)u+tv))^ n t^{N-1}\, \mathrm dt, \end{equation}

in the sense that both sides have the same integral against every bounded Borel function with compact support in $U$; in particular the pushforward is locally finite. Moreover, for such $v$, if $v-u\geq -C$ on an open set $E\subset U$, then over $E$ the measure $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^{n+N}$ is carried by $\{ |z_ i|\leq \mathrm e^{1+C/2}\text{ for all }i\} $.

Proof. After showing that $F$ is plurisubharmonic, we compute $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^{n+N}$ explicitly for smooth $v$, and we treat locally bounded $v$ by decreasing approximation. Points of $U\times \mathbb C^ N$ are also denoted by $q$.

Step 1: $F$ is plurisubharmonic. For fixed $x\in \Sigma _ N$, the function

\[ \phi _ x=(1-t(x))u+t(x)v+\sum _ ix_ i\log |z_ i|^2-|x|^2 \]

is plurisubharmonic on $U\times \mathbb C^ N$, as a sum of nonnegative multiples of plurisubharmonic functions and a constant (proposition 8.2.5). Since $\phi _0=u$ and $\phi _ x\leq \max \{ u,v\} +\sum _ i\max \{ \log |z_ i|^2,0\} $ for all $x$, the family $(\phi _ x)$ is locally uniformly bounded above, and $F$ lies between $u$ and the right side of this inequality; hence $F$ is locally bounded. The function $(x,q)\mapsto \phi _ x(q)$ on $\Sigma _ N\times (U\times \mathbb C^ N)$ is upper semicontinuous. Indeed, if $c\geq 0$ is a continuous function of $x$ and $f$ is an upper semicontinuous function of $q$ with values in $[-\infty ,M]$ for some $M\in \mathbb R$, then $cf$ is upper semicontinuous, with the convention $0\cdot (-\infty )=0$: at a point where $c=0$, we have $cf\leq cM^+\to 0$ nearby, and at a point where $c>0$ this follows from the upper semicontinuity of $f$. This applies to the terms $(1-t(x))u$, $t(x)v$ and $x_ i\log |z_ i|^2$, and a finite sum of upper semicontinuous functions with values in $[-\infty ,\infty )$ is upper semicontinuous. Since $\Sigma _ N$ is compact, $F$ is upper semicontinuous: if $q_ k\to q$ and $x_ k\in \Sigma _ N$ satisfy $\phi _{x_ k}(q_ k)\geq F(q_ k)-1/k$, then along a subsequence with $\varlimsup _ kF(q_ k)$ as limit, $x_ k\to x$, and $\varlimsup _ kF(q_ k)\leq \phi _ x(q)\leq F(q)$. Hence $F$ coincides with its upper semicontinuous regularization, which is plurisubharmonic by theorem 8.3.2.

Step 2: regularity of $F$ for smooth $v$. Let $v$ be smooth, and work on $G=U\times (\mathbb C^*)^ N$. Put $a=v-u$, $y_ i=\log |z_ i|^2$ and $b_ i=(y_ i+a)/2$. Since

\[ (1-t(x))u+t(x)v+\sum _ ix_ iy_ i-\sum _ ix_ i^2=u+|b|^2-|x-b|^2, \]

we have $F=u+\gamma (b)$ with $\gamma (b)=\max _{x\in \Sigma _ N}(2x\cdot b-|x|^2)=|b|^2-|b-P(b)|^2$, where $P(b)$ is the closest point of $\Sigma _ N$ to $b$; it exists by compactness and is unique because $x\mapsto |x-b|^2$ is strictly convex on the convex set $\Sigma _ N$. Differentiating $\sigma \mapsto |b-P(b)-\sigma (x'-P(b))|^2$ at $\sigma =0^+$ for $x'\in \Sigma _ N$ gives the variational inequality $(b-P(b))\cdot (x'-P(b))\leq 0$. Applying it to $b$ with $x'=P(b')$ and to $b'$ with $x'=P(b)$ and adding, we get $|P(b)-P(b')|^2\leq (b-b')\cdot (P(b)-P(b'))$, so $P$ is $1$-Lipschitz. Moreover $\gamma (b')\geq 2P(b)\cdot b'-|P(b)|^2=\gamma (b)+2P(b)\cdot (b'-b)$, and symmetrically, so that

\[ 0\leq \gamma (b')-\gamma (b)-2P(b)\cdot (b'-b)\leq 2(P(b')-P(b))\cdot (b'-b)\leq 2|b'-b|^2. \]

Hence $\gamma $ is differentiable, with the Lipschitz gradient $2P$. Consequently $F$ is of class $C^1$ on $G$ with locally Lipschitz first derivatives, and

15.22
\begin{equation} \mathrm dF=\mathrm du+\sum _ iP_ i(b)\, (\mathrm dy_ i+\mathrm da)=(1-t)\, \mathrm du+t\, \mathrm dv+\sum _ ix_ i\, \mathrm dy_ i,\qquad x=P(b),\ t=t(x). \end{equation}

The projection is explicit. Let $\lambda (b)=0$ if $\sum _ i\max \{ b_ i,0\} \leq 1$; otherwise let $\lambda (b)>0$ be the unique solution of $\sum _ i\max \{ b_ i-\lambda ,0\} =1$, which exists because the left side is continuous, nonincreasing, and strictly decreasing where it is positive. The point $x$ with coordinates $\max \{ b_ i-\lambda (b),0\} $ lies in $\Sigma _ N$ and satisfies the variational inequality: if $I$ is the set of indices with $b_ i>\lambda (b)$, then for $x'\in \Sigma _ N$ the left side of the inequality is $\sum _{i\in I}\lambda (b)(x'_ i-x_ i)+\sum _{i\notin I}b_ ix'_ i\leq \lambda (b)(t(x')-t(x))\leq 0$, since $t(x)=1$ when $\lambda (b)>0$. A point $x\in \Sigma _ N$ satisfying the variational inequality for all $x'\in \Sigma _ N$ is the closest point, because $|b-x'|^2=|b-x|^2+|x'-x|^2-2(b-x)\cdot (x'-x)\geq |b-x|^2$; hence $P_ i(b)=\max \{ b_ i-\lambda (b),0\} $.

For a nonempty set $I\subset \{ 1,\ldots ,N\} $ put $\lambda _ I(b)=(\sum _{i\in I}b_ i-1)/|I|$, and consider the open sets

\[ \begin{aligned} \Omega _{I,0}& =\{ b:\ b_ i>0\ (i\in I),\ b_ i<0\ (i\notin I),\ \textstyle \sum _{i\in I}b_ i<1\} ,\\ \Omega _{I,+}& =\{ b:\ \lambda _ I(b)>0,\ b_ i>\lambda _ I(b)\ (i\in I),\ b_ i<\lambda _ I(b)\ (i\notin I)\} , \end{aligned} \]

and $\Omega _{\varnothing ,0}=\{ b:b_ i<0\text{ for all }i\} $. On $\Omega _{I,0}$ we have $\lambda (b)=0$ and $P(b)=(b_ i\mathbf1_ I(i))_ i$, and on $\Omega _{I,+}$ we have $\lambda (b)=\lambda _ I(b)$, by the uniqueness of $\lambda $, and $P_ i(b)=(b_ i-\lambda _ I(b))\mathbf1_ I(i)$; in either case $P$ is affine there. Let $\mathcal H$ be the finite family of affine hyperplanes $\{ b_ i=0\} $, $\{ \sum _{i\in I}b_ i=1\} $ and $\{ b_ i=\lambda _ I(b)\} $, for $1\leq i\leq N$ and nonempty $I$, omitting the empty sets $\{ b_ i=\lambda _{\{ i\} }(b)\} $. Every $b$ outside $\bigcup \mathcal H$ lies in one of the sets $\Omega _{I,0}$, $\Omega _{I,+}$: if $\lambda (b)=0$, it lies in $\Omega _{I,0}$ with $I=\{ i:b_ i>0\} $, and if $\lambda (b)>0$, then $\lambda (b)=\lambda _ I(b)$ for $I=\{ i:b_ i>\lambda (b)\} $, and $b\in \Omega _{I,+}$ because no $b_ i$ equals $\lambda _ I(b)$.

Let $Z\subset G$ be the closed set of points with $b\in \bigcup \mathcal H$. For a fixed base point, each hyperplane of $\mathcal H$ is given by a nonconstant affine equation in $y=(\log |z_ i|^2)_ i$; in polar coordinates $z_ i=r_ i\mathrm e^{\mathrm i\alpha _ i}$, the corresponding set is the product of the image of a hyperplane of $\mathbb R^ N$ under the diffeomorphism $y\mapsto (\mathrm e^{y_ i/2})_ i$ with the torus of angles, so it is a Lebesgue null set. By Fubini's theorem, $Z$ is a Lebesgue null set. On $G\setminus Z$ the function $F=u+|b|^2-|b-P(b)|^2$ is smooth, because $P$ is locally affine there.

Step 3: for smooth $v$, $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^{n+N}$ is carried by the interior region. On the interior region $\Omega _{\{ 1,\ldots ,N\} ,0}=\{ b:b_ i>0,\ \sum _ ib_ i<1\} $, the interior of $\Sigma _ N$, we have $x=P(b)=b$ and $F=u+\sum _ ix_ i^2$. On every other region $\Omega $, the complex Hessian of $F$, which is positive semidefinite by proposition 8.2.2(1), has a vertical null vector, and hence zero determinant. Indeed, if $j\notin I$, then $x_ j=P_ j(b)=0$ on $\Omega $, so $\partial F/\partial z_ j=x_ j/z_ j=0$ there by (15.22), and all second derivatives $\partial ^2F/\partial z_ j\partial \bar z_ k$ vanish. If instead $I=\{ 1,\ldots ,N\} $ and $\lambda >0$, then $\lambda =\bar b-1/N$ with $\bar b=N^{-1}\sum _ ib_ i$, and since $|b|^2=\sum _ i(b_ i-\bar b)^2+N\bar b^2$,

\[ F=u+|b|^2-N\lambda ^2=v+\frac1N\sum _ iy_ i-\frac1N+\sum _ i(b_ i-\bar b)^2. \]

For $\zeta \in \mathbb C$ near $0$, the point $(w,\mathrm e^\zeta z_1,\ldots ,\mathrm e^\zeta z_ N)$ stays in the region, every $y_ i$ increases by $2\operatorname{Re}\zeta $, and the differences $b_ i-\bar b=(y_ i-\bar y)/2$ do not change, so $F(w,\mathrm e^\zeta z)=F(w,z)+2\operatorname{Re}\zeta $ is harmonic in $\zeta $. Its Laplacian at $\zeta =0$ is a positive multiple of the value of the complex Hessian of $F$ on the vector $\xi =\sum _ iz_ i\, \partial /\partial z_ i$, which therefore vanishes; since the Hessian is positive semidefinite, $\xi $ is a null vector by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality.

We now identify $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^{n+N}$ on $G$. Let $D$ be a first derivative of $F$ with respect to a real coordinate; by Step 2 it has a Lipschitz constant $L$ on a given ball $B\Subset G$. For a test function $\chi \geq 0$ supported in a smaller ball and a real coordinate $q_ l$,

\[ |\langle \partial _{q_ l}D,\chi \rangle |=\Bigl |\lim _{h\to 0}\int \frac{D(q+he_ l)-D(q)}{h}\chi (q)\, \mathrm dq\Bigr |\leq L\int \chi \, \mathrm dq, \]

so by corollary 1.7.2 the distributional derivative $\partial _{q_ l}D$ is a locally bounded function. Hence the distributional second derivatives of $F$ are locally bounded functions on $G$, and on the open set $G\setminus Z$ they coincide almost everywhere with the classical ones, since $F$ is smooth there. As $Z$ is a null set, the Hessian determinant $\det (F_{j\bar k})$, formed with these functions, is a locally bounded function that equals the classical determinant on $G\setminus Z$.

Let $F_\varepsilon =F*\rho _\varepsilon $ be the mollifications, defined, smooth and plurisubharmonic on a relatively compact open set $G'\Subset G$ for small $\varepsilon $ (proposition 8.2.2(2)). Their second derivatives are the mollifications of those of $F$ (proposition 1.4.3); by lemma 1.4.1 they converge in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(G')$ and are locally uniformly bounded. Since the determinant is a polynomial in the entries, $\det (F_{\varepsilon ,j\bar k})\to \det (F_{j\bar k})$ in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(G')$. On the other hand $F_\varepsilon \to F$ locally uniformly, because $F$ is continuous, so $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F_\varepsilon )^{n+N}\to (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^{n+N}$ weakly by proposition 9.1.4. With (9.2) for the smooth functions $F_\varepsilon $, we conclude that $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^{n+N}=\frac{(n+N)!}{\pi ^{n+N}}\det (F_{j\bar k})\, \mathrm dV$ on $G$. In particular this measure does not charge the null set $Z$, and it vanishes on all regions except the interior one. It does not charge the pluripolar complement of $G$ either (corollary 10.2.2), so $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^{n+N}$ is carried by the set $G_0=\{ (w,z)\in G:b(w,z)\in \operatorname{Int}\Sigma _ N\} $ over the interior region.

Step 4: the formula for smooth $v$. On $G_0$ we have $F=u+\sum _ ix_ i^2$ with $x_ i=(y_ i+a)/2$. Since $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(x_ i^2)=2x_ i\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}x_ i+2\, \mathrm dx_ i\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}x_ i$ and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}x_ i=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a/2$, the functions $y_ i$ being pluriharmonic on $G$,

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F=(1-t)\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u+t\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v+\sum _ i\Gamma _ i,\qquad \Gamma _ i=2\, \mathrm dx_ i\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}x_ i, \]

with $t=t(x)$. The form $\Lambda =(1-t)\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u+t\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ contains only differentials of the base variables, so $\Lambda ^{n+1}=0$, and $\Gamma _ i\wedge \Gamma _ i=0$. Since forms of even degree commute, the binomial expansion leaves only the term $\binom {n+N}{n}\Lambda ^ n\wedge (\sum _ i\Gamma _ i)^ N$, and $(\sum _ i\Gamma _ i)^ N=N!\, \Gamma _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \Gamma _ N$. The map $(w,z)\mapsto (w,x,\alpha )$, with $\alpha _ i=\arg z_ i$, is an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism of $G_0$ onto $U\times \operatorname{Int}\Sigma _ N\times (\mathbb R/2\pi \mathbb Z)^ N$, with inverse $z_ i=\mathrm e^{x_ i-a(w)/2}\mathrm e^{\mathrm i\alpha _ i}$. With our normalization, $\mathrm d^{\mathrm c}y_ i=\mathrm d\alpha _ i/(2\pi )$, so $\Gamma _ i=\mathrm dx_ i\wedge \mathrm d\alpha _ i/(2\pi )+\mathrm dx_ i\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}a$, and the second term contains a differential of the base variables and disappears in the product with $\Lambda ^ n$. Hence, in these coordinates,

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^{n+N}=\binom {n+N}{n}N!\, \bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}((1-t(x))u+t(x)v)\bigr )^ n(w)\wedge \bigwedge _ i\Bigl (\mathrm dx_ i\wedge \frac{\mathrm d\alpha _ i}{2\pi }\Bigr ). \]

Let $g\geq 0$ be a bounded Borel function with compact support in $U$. Since $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^{n+N}$ is carried by $G_0$, integrating $g(w)$ against this measure in the coordinates $(w,x,\alpha )$, first over the angles by Fubini's theorem, we obtain

\[ \int (g\circ p)\, (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^{n+N}=\binom {n+N}{n}N!\int _{\Sigma _ N}\Phi (t(x))\, \mathrm dx, \]

where $\Phi (t)=\int _ Ug\, (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}((1-t)u+tv))^ n$. By multilinearity of Bedford–Taylor products, $\Phi $ is a polynomial in $t$. The image of Lebesgue measure on $\Sigma _ N$ under $t$ is $t^{N-1}\, \mathrm dt/(N-1)!$ on $[0,1]$, because $\{ x\in \Sigma _ N:t(x)\leq t_0\} =t_0\Sigma _ N$ has volume $t_0^ N|\Sigma _ N|$ and $|\Sigma _ N|=1/N!$ by iterated integration, and finite measures on $[0,1]$ that agree on the intervals $[0,t_0]$ agree (lemma 1.1.6). This proves (15.21) for smooth $v$; for signed bounded Borel $g$ split $g$ into its positive and negative parts.

Step 5: support for smooth $v$. If $a\geq -C$ at a base point, then at the points of $G_0$ over it we have $y_ i=2x_ i-a\leq 2+C$, because $x_ i<1$; hence $|z_ i|\leq \mathrm e^{1+C/2}$. Since $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^{n+N}$ is carried by $G_0$, this proves the support assertion for smooth $v$.

Step 6: general $v$. Let $v$ be locally bounded, let $U'\Subset U$ be open, and let $v_ l$ be smooth plurisubharmonic functions on a neighborhood of $\overline{U'}$ decreasing to $v$, for instance mollifications (proposition 8.2.2(2)). Let $F_ l$ be the corresponding functions. For each $x$, the branch of $F_ l$ exceeds that of $F$ by $t(x)(v_ l-v)\in [0,v_ l-v]$, so $F\leq F_ l\leq F+(v_ l-v)$, and the $F_ l$ decrease pointwise to $F$; by Step 1 they are locally uniformly bounded. By theorem 9.2.5, $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F_ l)^{n+N}\to (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^{n+N}$ weakly on $U'\times \mathbb C^ N$. If $v-u\geq -C$ on an open set $E$, then also $v_ l-u\geq -C$ there, so by Step 5 the measures $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F_ l)^{n+N}$ give no mass to the open set of points over $E\cap U'$ at which some $|z_ i|>\mathrm e^{1+C/2}$. The weak limit gives no mass to this open set either, since the mass of an open set is the supremum of the integrals of test functions $0\leq \chi \leq 1$ supported in it. This proves the support assertion.

Now let $g$ be continuous with compact support in $U'$. Since $v$ is bounded below and $u$ is bounded above on a relatively compact neighborhood $E$ of $\operatorname{Supp}g$, there is $C$ with $v-u\geq -C$ on $E$, so all the measures $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F_ l)^{n+N}$ and $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^{n+N}$ over $E$ are carried by $E\times \{ |z_ i|\leq \mathrm e^{1+C/2}\} $. Multiplying $g\circ p$ by a test function of $z$ equal to one on this polydisc, we see that $\int (g\circ p)(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F_ l)^{n+N}\to \int (g\circ p)(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^{n+N}$; here we use that weakly convergent positive measures with locally bounded masses converge against continuous functions with compact support.

On the right side of (15.21), the functions $(1-t)u+tv_ l$ decrease to $(1-t)u+tv$ for each $t$, so $\int g\, (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}((1-t)u+tv_ l))^ n\to \int g\, (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}((1-t)u+tv))^ n$ by theorem 9.2.5; these integrals are bounded uniformly in $t\in [0,1]$ and $l$ by theorem 9.1.3, and dominated convergence in $t$ applies. Hence (15.21) holds on $U'$ when tested against continuous functions with compact support. Both sides are positive measures that are finite on compact subsets of $U'$, so they agree as measures by the uniqueness in theorem 1.4.5, and therefore also against bounded Borel functions with compact support. Exhausting $U$ by such sets $U'$ proves the lemma. $\square$

Proposition 15.7.3. Let $\varphi _0\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, and assume that its pole set $P=\{ \varphi _0=-\infty \} $ is closed and that $\varphi _0$ is smooth on $X\setminus P$. Fix an integer $N\geq 1$, let $p_ X,p_{\mathbb P^ N}$ be the projections of $X\times \mathbb P^ N$, and let $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$ be the Fubini–Study form of example 14.5.6. For $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and homogeneous coordinates $[Z_0:\cdots :Z_ N]$ put

\[ s_ i=\log \frac{|Z_ i|^2}{\sum _{l=0}^ N|Z_ l|^2},\qquad \widetilde\theta =p_ X^*\theta +p_{\mathbb P^ N}^*\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}, \]

and let $\Phi $ be the upper semicontinuous regularization of

\[ \Phi _0=\sup _{x\in \Sigma _ N} \left((1-t(x))(\varphi _0+s_0)+t(x)\varphi +\sum _{i=1}^ N x_ i s_ i-\sum _{i=1}^ N x_ i^2\right), \]

with the convention that a zero coefficient times $-\infty $ is zero. Then $\Phi $ is $\widetilde\theta $-plurisubharmonic and locally bounded outside the closed set $A=(P\times \mathbb P^ N)\cup (X\times \{ Z_0=0\} )$, which is the pole set of the quasi-plurisubharmonic function $\varphi _0+s_0$, and

15.25
\begin{equation} \begin{aligned} & \int _{X\times \mathbb P^ N}\langle (\widetilde\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Phi )^{n+N}\rangle \\ & \quad =\binom {n+N}{n}N\int _0^1 \left(\int _ X\langle (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}((1-t)\varphi _0+t\varphi ))^ n\rangle \right) t^{N-1}\, \mathrm dt. \end{aligned} \end{equation}

If $\{ \theta \} $ is big, a potential $\varphi _0$ with these properties exists.

Proof. The potential $\Phi $. Each function in the supremum is quasi-plurisubharmonic and $\widetilde\theta $-plurisubharmonic: its coefficients of the $\theta $-plurisubharmonic functions $\varphi _0$ and $\varphi $ are nonnegative and add up to one, and so do its coefficients of the functions $s_ i$, since $(1-t(x))+\sum _ ix_ i=1$. The $s_ i$ are $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$-plurisubharmonic: in the standard chart $\{ Z_ l\neq 0\} $, where $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log \sum _ m|Z_ m/Z_ l|^2$ as in example 14.5.6, the function $s_ i+\log \sum _ m|Z_ m/Z_ l|^2=\log |Z_ i/Z_ l|^2$ is plurisubharmonic. The family is uniformly bounded above, because $\varphi _0$ and $\varphi $ are bounded above on the compact manifold $X$ and $s_ i\leq 0$. Hence, by theorem 8.3.2 applied after adding smooth local potentials of $\widetilde\theta $, its regularized supremum $\Phi $ is $\widetilde\theta $-plurisubharmonic. The branch $x=0$ is $\tau =\varphi _0+s_0$, which is quasi-plurisubharmonic, equal to $-\infty $ exactly on $A$, and locally bounded outside $A$, because $\varphi _0$ is smooth on $X\setminus P$ and $s_0$ is smooth on $\{ Z_0\neq 0\} $. Since $\Phi \geq \Phi _0\geq \tau $ and $\Phi $ is bounded above, $\Phi $ is locally bounded outside $A$; the set $A$ is closed because $P$ is.

Local form. Let $W\subset X\setminus P$ be a coordinate chart, choose a smooth function $h$ on $W$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h=\theta $, and use the affine coordinates $z_ i=Z_ i/Z_0$ on $\{ Z_0\neq 0\} =\mathbb C^ N$. There $s_0=-\log (1+|z|^2)$ and $s_ i=\log |z_ i|^2-\log (1+|z|^2)$, and $\log (1+|z|^2)$ is a potential of $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$. Since the coefficients of the $s_ i$ add up to one, adding the smooth function $h+\log (1+|z|^2)$ to each function in the supremum turns $\Phi _0$ into the function $F$ of lemma 15.7.2, formed with $u=\varphi _0+h$, which is smooth and plurisubharmonic on $W$, and $v=\varphi +h$, which is plurisubharmonic on $W$. By that lemma, $F$ is plurisubharmonic, hence upper semicontinuous, so $\Phi _0$ is upper semicontinuous on the open set $(X\setminus P)\times \{ Z_0\neq 0\} =(X\times \mathbb P^ N)\setminus A$, and $\Phi =\Phi _0$ there. Thus $F$ is a locally bounded plurisubharmonic potential of $\widetilde\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Phi $ on $W\times \mathbb C^ N$, and the non-pluripolar product $\langle (\widetilde\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Phi )^{n+N}\rangle $ is the Bedford–Taylor measure $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^{n+N}$ there.

The mass formula. For $C>0$ put $v_ C=\max \{ v,u-C\} $, a locally bounded plurisubharmonic function on $W$, and let $F_ C$ be the function of lemma 15.7.2 formed with $u$ and $v_ C$. At base points $w$ with $v(w)>u(w)-C$ we have $v_ C(w)=v(w)$, so $F_ C=F$ on the set $Y_ C=\{ (w,z):v(w)>u(w)-C\} $. This set is plurifine open in $W\times \mathbb C^ N$, because $(w,z)\mapsto v(w)$ is plurisubharmonic and $u$ is continuous. By plurifine locality (theorem 10.4.3), $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^{n+N}$ and $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F_ C)^{n+N}$ agree on $Y_ C$.

Similarly, for $0\leq t\leq 1$, the function $(1-t)u+tv$ is a local potential of the closed positive current $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}((1-t)\varphi _0+t\varphi )$, whose non-pluripolar products are finite by theorem 15.4.3, and on the plurifine open set $\{ v>u-C\} $ it coincides with the bounded function $(1-t)u+tv_ C$; so by proposition 15.1.5,

\[ \mathbf1_{\{ v>u-C\} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}((1-t)u+tv_ C))^ n=\mathbf1_{\{ v>u-C\} }\langle (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}((1-t)u+tv))^ n\rangle . \]

Let $g\geq 0$ be a bounded Borel function with compact support in $W$. Applying lemma 15.7.2 to $F_ C$ with the Borel weight $g\mathbf1_{\{ v>u-C\} }$, we obtain

\[ \begin{aligned} & \int _{Y_ C}(g\circ p_ X)(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^{n+N}\\ & \qquad =\binom {n+N}{n}N\int _0^1\Bigl (\int _ Wg\, \mathbf1_{\{ v>u-C\} }\langle (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}((1-t)u+tv))^ n\rangle \Bigr )t^{N-1}\, \mathrm dt. \end{aligned} \]

Let $C\to \infty $. The sets $\{ v>u-C\} $ increase to $\{ v>-\infty \} $, whose complement is the pole set of $v$; this set and its product with $\mathbb C^ N$ are pluripolar Borel sets, which carry no mass for the non-pluripolar products on $W$ and for the Bedford–Taylor measure $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^{n+N}$ (corollary 10.2.2). By monotone convergence, first in the inner integrals and then in $t$,

\[ \begin{aligned} & \int _{W\times \mathbb C^ N}(g\circ p_ X)\, \langle (\widetilde\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Phi )^{n+N}\rangle \\ & \qquad =\binom {n+N}{n}N\int _0^1\Bigl (\int _ Xg\, \langle (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}((1-t)\varphi _0+t\varphi ))^ n\rangle \Bigr )t^{N-1}\, \mathrm dt. \end{aligned} \]

The non-pluripolar product on $X\times \mathbb P^ N$ gives zero mass to the pluripolar Borel set $A$, and the products on $X$ give zero mass to $P$. Hence the left side is the integral of $g\circ p_ X$ over $X\times \mathbb P^ N$. Now cover $X\setminus P$ by countably many compact sets $K_ k$, each contained in a chart $W_ k\subset X\setminus P$, and put $B_ k=K_ k\setminus \bigcup _{j<k}K_ j$. For a nonnegative Borel function $g$ on $X$, applying the identity to the functions $\min \{ g,M\} \mathbf1_{B_ k}$ and using monotone convergence in $M$ and in the sum over $k$, we obtain the identity for $g\mathbf1_{X\setminus P}$, and hence for $g$, since both sides give $P$ zero mass. Taking $g=1$ gives (15.25).

Existence of $\varphi _0$. If $\{ \theta \} $ is big, it contains a Kähler current $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi \geq \delta \omega $ with $\delta >0$, that is, $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta -\delta \omega )$. By lemma 13.4.1(1), applied to $\psi $ with the background $\theta -\delta \omega $, for every integer $m\geq 1$ there is a quasi-plurisubharmonic function $G_ m=m^{-1}\log R_ m$ with $R_ m$ smooth and $\theta -\delta \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}G_ m\geq -(c'/m)\omega $, where the constant $c'>0$ does not depend on $m$. For $m\geq c'/\delta $, the function $\varphi _0=G_ m$ is $\theta $-plurisubharmonic; its pole set $\{ R_ m=0\} $ is closed, and $\varphi _0$ is smooth on its complement, where $R_ m>0$. $\square$

Theorem 15.7.4. Let $\varphi ,\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $\varphi \geq \psi -O(1)$. Then

\[ \int _ X\langle (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ n\rangle \geq \int _ X\langle (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi )^ n\rangle . \]

Proof. Suppose first that $\{ \theta \} $ is big. Choose a reference potential $\varphi _0$ as in proposition 15.7.3, which exists because the class is big, and construct the corresponding potentials $\Phi _ N$ from $\varphi $ and $\Psi _ N$ from $\psi $. If $\varphi \geq \psi -C$, then for every $x\in \Sigma _ N$ the branch defining $\Phi _ N$ is at least the branch defining $\Psi _ N$ minus $t(x)C\leq C$; hence the suprema and their regularizations satisfy $\Phi _ N\geq \Psi _ N-C$, with a constant independent of $N$. By proposition 15.7.3, both are locally bounded outside the same closed set $A$, which is the pole set of the quasi-plurisubharmonic function $\varphi _0+s_0$, and this function is locally bounded outside $A$. The product $X\times \mathbb P^ N$ is a connected compact Kähler manifold of dimension $n+N$, with the Kähler form $p_ X^*\omega +p_{\mathbb P^ N}^*\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$, so the results of this section apply to it.

For $0\leq t\leq 1$ put

\[ f_\varphi (t)=\int _ X\langle (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}((1-t)\varphi _0+t\varphi ))^ n\rangle , \]

and define $f_\psi $ in the same way. Applying lemma 15.7.1 on $X\times \mathbb P^ N$, with all $n+N$ factors equal to $\widetilde\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Phi _ N$, respectively $\widetilde\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Psi _ N$, and cancelling the common binomial coefficient in (15.25), we obtain

\[ \int _0^1f_\varphi (t)\, Nt^{N-1}\, \mathrm dt\geq \int _0^1f_\psi (t)\, Nt^{N-1}\, \mathrm dt. \]

Writing $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}((1-t)\varphi _0+t\varphi )=(1-t)T_0+tT_\varphi $ with $T_0=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _0$ and $T_\varphi =\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $, multilinearity shows that $f_\varphi $ is a polynomial in $t$, in particular continuous on $[0,1]$, and $f_\varphi (1)$ is the mass of $\varphi $. The probability measures $Nt^{N-1}\, \mathrm dt$ concentrate at $1$ as $N\to \infty $, since their mass on $[0,1-\delta ]$ is $(1-\delta )^ N$. By uniform continuity the two integrals tend to $f_\varphi (1)$ and $f_\psi (1)$, which proves the theorem for big classes.

In general, $\{ \theta \} $ is pseudo-effective, because it contains $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $. For $\varepsilon >0$ the class of $\theta +\varepsilon \omega $ is big, since it contains the Kähler current $\theta +\varepsilon \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \geq \varepsilon \omega $, and $\varphi ,\psi $ are $(\theta +\varepsilon \omega )$-plurisubharmonic. By the big case, the mass of $\varphi $ with respect to $\theta +\varepsilon \omega $ is at least that of $\psi $. By multilinearity and theorem 15.4.3, both masses are polynomials in $\varepsilon $ with finite coefficients, and letting $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ proves the theorem. $\square$

Corollary 15.7.5. Let $u_ j,v_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta _ j)$ for $1\leq j\leq p\leq n$, and put $T_ j=\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j$ and $T'_ j=\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ j$. If every difference $u_ j-v_ j$ is bounded, then

\[ \int _ X\langle T_1\wedge \cdots \wedge T_ p\rangle \wedge \omega ^{n-p} =\int _ X\langle T'_1\wedge \cdots \wedge T'_ p\rangle \wedge \omega ^{n-p}. \]

If only $u_ j\geq v_ j-O(1)$ for every $j$, then the left side is at least the right side.

Proof. Bounded differences. For real numbers $a_0,a_1,\ldots ,a_ p>0$, apply theorem 15.7.4 in both directions to the potentials $\sum _ ja_ ju_ j$ and $\sum _ ja_ jv_ j$, whose difference is bounded, with respect to the background $a_0\omega +\sum _ ja_ j\theta _ j$. The two resulting masses are equal. By multilinearity, both are homogeneous polynomials of degree $n$ in $(a_0,\ldots ,a_ p)$, and they agree on an open set; fixing all but one variable and using the uniqueness of polynomials in one variable successively, we see that their coefficients agree. By proposition 15.3.3, the coefficient of $a_0^{n-p}a_1\cdots a_ p$ is $n!/(n-p)!$ times the mixed mass in the statement, which proves equality.

The ordered case. Put $v_{j,k}=\max \{ u_ j-k,v_ j\} $.
