Since $v_ j\leq u_ j+C_ j$ for constants $C_ j$, we have $u_ j-k\leq v_{j,k}\leq u_ j+C_ j$, so each tuple $(v_{j,k})_ j$ has the same mixed mass as $(u_ j)_ j$, by the first part. As $k\to \infty $, $v_{j,k}$ decreases to $v_ j$ and therefore converges to it in extended capacity (lemma 15.6.3). The lower inequality of theorem 15.6.5, with test form $\omega ^{n-p}$, bounds the mixed mass of the $v_ j$ by these equal masses. $\square$

Corollary 15.7.6. Suppose that $u_{j,k}\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta _ j)$ increase almost everywhere to $u_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta _ j)$ as $k\to \infty $, for $1\leq j\leq p\leq n$. Then the non-pluripolar products $\langle \bigwedge _ j(\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{j,k})\rangle $ converge weakly to $\langle \bigwedge _ j(\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)\rangle $, and their mixed masses against $\omega ^{n-p}$ converge.

Proof. The inequality $u_{j,k}\leq u_ j$ holds almost everywhere, hence everywhere, since $\max \{ u_{j,k},u_ j\} $ and $u_ j$ are quasi-plurisubharmonic functions that agree almost everywhere. On coordinate charts add smooth local potentials and truncate below at a fixed level $-C$. The resulting locally uniformly bounded plurisubharmonic sequences increase almost everywhere to the truncations of the limits, so they converge in capacity by theorem 9.5.1; by lemma 15.6.3, the original sequences converge in extended capacity. Since $u_{j,k}\leq u_ j$, corollary 15.7.5 bounds every mixed mass of the sequence by that of the limit, and theorem 15.6.5 gives weak convergence and convergence of the masses. This applies in particular to the regularized supremum of an increasing sequence in $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta _ j)$ that is bounded above: it belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta _ j)$ by theorem 8.3.2, and it differs from the pointwise supremum only on a pluripolar set, by corollary 10.2.2, hence only on a Lebesgue null set, because plurisubharmonic functions are locally integrable. $\square$

15.8 Maximal mass and comparison

By the monotonicity just proved, potentials with minimal singularities have the largest non-pluripolar masses in a big class, and these maximal masses are invariants of the classes. We extend them to pseudo-effective classes by perturbing with a Kähler class, and then derive the comparison and domination principles for non-pluripolar Monge–Ampère measures. Recall that $V_\theta $ denotes the extremal potential of definition 14.2.6.

Definition 15.8.1. For big classes $\alpha _ j=\{ \theta _ j\} $, $1\leq j\leq p\leq n$, the positive mixed intersection number relative to the Kähler class $\{ \omega \} $ is

\[ I_\omega (\alpha _1,\ldots ,\alpha _ p) =\int _ X\left\langle \bigwedge _{j=1}^ p (\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V_{\theta _ j})\right\rangle \wedge \omega ^{n-p}. \]

For pseudo-effective classes $\alpha _ j$ that are not all big, we define $I_\omega (\alpha _1,\ldots ,\alpha _ p)$ as the limit of the numbers $I_\omega (\alpha _1+\varepsilon \{ \omega \} ,\ldots ,\alpha _ p+\varepsilon \{ \omega \} )$, which decrease as $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ (proposition 15.8.2); when all $\alpha _ j$ are big, the same limit equals the number defined by the display. In top degree we put $\operatorname {vol}(\alpha )=I_\omega (\alpha ,\ldots ,\alpha )$, the volume of $\alpha $; the factor $\omega ^{n-p}$ is then absent.

Proposition 15.8.2. The numbers of definition 15.8.1 do not depend on the chosen smooth representatives or minimal potentials, nor on the Kähler form within its class. The limit in definition 15.8.1 exists for all pseudo-effective classes, does not depend on the Kähler class used for the perturbation, and equals the number defined by the display when all $\alpha _ j$ are big. For closed positive currents $T_ j\in \alpha _ j$,

\[ \int _ X\langle T_1\wedge \cdots \wedge T_ p\rangle \wedge \omega ^{n-p} \leq I_\omega (\alpha _1,\ldots ,\alpha _ p). \]

The numbers are positively homogeneous in each class and increase when a pseudo-effective class is added to any argument. For nef classes they equal the cohomological intersection numbers $\int _ X\theta _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \theta _ p\wedge \omega ^{n-p}$. Big classes have positive volume.

Proof. Big classes. Any two potentials with minimal singularities in a fixed big class differ by a bounded function, and a change of smooth representative changes each potential by a smooth function. Hence corollary 15.7.5 shows that $I_\omega $ does not depend on these choices. The same corollary and minimality give the upper bound for closed positive currents in big classes. If $\omega '=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f$ is another Kähler form in the class of $\omega $, with $f$ smooth, then $\omega '^{n-p}-\omega ^{n-p}=\mathrm d\bigl (\mathrm d^{\mathrm c}f\wedge \sum _{i=0}^{n-p-1}\omega '^ i\wedge \omega ^{n-p-1-i}\bigr )$ is a smooth exact form. Its integral against the closed current $\langle T_1\wedge \cdots \wedge T_ p\rangle $ vanishes, because $S(\mathrm d\psi )=\pm (\mathrm dS)(\psi )=0$ for a closed current $S$ and a smooth form $\psi $ on the compact manifold $X$. Hence the numbers for big classes do not depend on $\omega $ within its class, and neither do their limits, since the perturbing classes $\varepsilon \{ \omega \} $ are the same.

Let $\alpha $ be big and $\gamma $ pseudo-effective, represented by a closed positive current $S$. Adding $S$ to a current with minimal singularities in $\alpha $ gives a current in the big class $\alpha +\gamma $, which by the upper bound has mixed mass at most the number with $\alpha $ replaced by $\alpha +\gamma $. By multilinearity and positivity, its mixed mass is at least the number for $\alpha $. Hence, when all arguments are big, replacing an argument $\alpha $ by $\alpha +\gamma $ increases the number. Homogeneity for big classes follows by scaling a minimal potential together with its background: if $c>0$, then $cV_\theta $ has minimal singularities in $\mathrm{PSH}(X,c\theta )$, and the product is homogeneous in each factor by proposition 15.1.5.

Pseudo-effective classes. By this monotonicity for big classes, the perturbed numbers decrease as $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$; they are nonnegative, so the limit exists. Every Kähler form $\kappa $ satisfies $c^{-1}\omega \leq \kappa \leq c\omega $ for some $c\geq 1$ (lemma 13.1.1), so monotonicity places the perturbation by $\varepsilon \kappa $ between the perturbations by $c^{-1}\varepsilon \omega $ and $c\varepsilon \omega $, with the same fixed test form $\omega ^{n-p}$; this proves independence of the perturbing class. The same comparison shows that perturbing the arguments by different fixed multiples of $\varepsilon \omega $ gives the same limit, which extends homogeneity to pseudo-effective classes. Monotonicity also extends: if $\gamma $ is pseudo-effective, then for every $\varepsilon >0$ the number with an argument $\alpha +\varepsilon \{ \omega \} $ replaced by $\alpha +\gamma +\varepsilon \{ \omega \} $ is at least the original one, by the big case, and we let $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$.

If all $\alpha _ j$ are big, choose $\delta >0$ such that each $\alpha _ j$ contains a Kähler current $R_ j\geq \delta \omega $. Then $(1+\varepsilon /\delta )\alpha _ j-(\alpha _ j+\varepsilon \{ \omega \} )=(\varepsilon /\delta )\{ R_ j-\delta \omega \} $ is pseudo-effective, so monotonicity and homogeneity give

\[ I_\omega (\alpha _1,\ldots ,\alpha _ p)\leq I_\omega (\alpha _1+\varepsilon \{ \omega \} ,\ldots ,\alpha _ p+\varepsilon \{ \omega \} )\leq (1+\varepsilon /\delta )^ pI_\omega (\alpha _1,\ldots ,\alpha _ p), \]

and the limit is $I_\omega (\alpha _1,\ldots ,\alpha _ p)$. For arbitrary closed positive currents $T_ j\in \alpha _ j$, the upper bound in the big classes $\alpha _ j+\varepsilon \{ \omega \} $, applied to $T_ j+\varepsilon \omega $, and multilinearity give $\int _ X\langle \bigwedge _ jT_ j\rangle \wedge \omega ^{n-p}\leq I_\omega (\alpha _1+\varepsilon \{ \omega \} ,\ldots )$, and letting $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ proves the upper bound.

Nef and big classes. If the $\alpha _ j$ are nef, their perturbations are Kähler classes: $\alpha _ j+\varepsilon \{ \omega \} $ contains $\theta _{j,\varepsilon /2}+\varepsilon \omega \geq \varepsilon \omega /2$, where $\theta _{j,\varepsilon /2}\geq -(\varepsilon /2)\omega $ is a smooth representative of $\alpha _ j$ given by definition 14.3.1. If $\theta _ j+\varepsilon \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f$ is a Kähler form with $f$ smooth, then $f\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta _ j+\varepsilon \omega )$, so $V_{\theta _ j+\varepsilon \omega }\geq f-\sup _ Xf$ by lemma 14.2.7; since also $V_{\theta _ j+\varepsilon \omega }\leq 0$ by the same lemma, the minimal potentials are bounded. By proposition 15.4.1, the perturbed numbers are the integrals $\int _ X\bigwedge _ j(\theta _ j+\varepsilon \omega )\wedge \omega ^{n-p}$, and their limits are the cohomological intersection numbers. Finally, let $\alpha $ be big and $T_0\in \alpha $ a Kähler current with $T_0\geq \delta \omega $. Expanding $T_0=\delta \omega +(T_0-\delta \omega )$ by multilinearity gives $\langle T_0^ n\rangle \geq \delta ^ n\omega ^ n$, so $\operatorname {vol}(\alpha )\geq \delta ^ n\int _ X\omega ^ n>0$ by the upper bound. $\square$

Conversely, a pseudo-effective class of positive volume is big. The proof requires the construction of a Kähler current in the class and is given in section 17.4.4, after Monge–Ampère equations with prescribed measures have been solved.

Definition 15.8.3. A closed positive $(1,1)$-current $T\in \alpha $ has full Monge–Ampère mass if $\int _ X\langle T^ n\rangle =\operatorname {vol}(\alpha )$. We write $\mathcal E(X,\theta )$ for the set of $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ such that $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ has full Monge–Ampère mass. For $\phi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, the class $\mathcal E(X,\theta ,\phi )$ consists of the potentials $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $u\leq \phi +O(1)$ and

\[ \int _ X\langle (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n\rangle =\int _ X\langle (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi )^ n\rangle ; \]

these potentials have full mass relative to $\phi $. In a big class, $\mathcal E(X,\theta )=\mathcal E(X,\theta ,V_\theta )$, because every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfies $u\leq V_\theta +\sup _ Xu$ and $\operatorname {vol}(\{ \theta \} )$ is the mass of $V_\theta $.

The class $\mathcal E(X,\theta )$ should not be confused with the local Cegrell class $\mathcal E_0(B)$ of definition 9.10.1, nor with the pseudo-effective cone of definition 14.3.1, a cone of cohomology classes.

Corollary 15.8.4. Let $u_ k\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfy $u_ k\leq \phi +C_ k$ for constants $C_ k$, let $u\in \mathcal E(X,\theta ,\phi )$, and suppose that $u_ k\to u$ in extended capacity. Then

\[ \langle (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ k)^ n\rangle \longrightarrow \langle (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n\rangle \]

weakly, and the masses converge. The same holds, without the constants $C_ k$, if $u\in \mathcal E(X,\theta )$; in particular it holds for every sequence in $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ that decreases to an element of $\mathcal E(X,\theta )$.

Proof. By theorem 15.7.4, the mass of each $u_ k$ is at most that of $\phi $, which equals the mass of $u$. Hence theorem 15.6.5, with $p=n$, gives weak convergence, and its lower inequality together with this bound gives convergence of the masses. If $u\in \mathcal E(X,\theta )$, then by proposition 15.8.2 the mass of each $u_ k$ is at most $\operatorname {vol}(\{ \theta \} )$, which is the mass of $u$, and the same argument applies. Finally, decreasing sequences converge in extended capacity by lemma 15.6.3. $\square$

Proposition 15.8.5. For $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ write $\mu _ u=\langle (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n\rangle $. Then for all $u,v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$,

\[ \int _{\{ u<v\} }\mu _ v \leq \int _{\{ u<v\} }\mu _ u+\operatorname {vol}(\{ \theta \} )-\mu _ u(X). \]

The last two terms may be omitted if $u$ has full mass or if $u\geq v-O(1)$.

Proof. Let $\varepsilon >0$. The function $w=\max \{ u,v-\varepsilon \} $ is $\theta $-plurisubharmonic. The sets $\{ u<v-\varepsilon \} $ and $\{ u>v-\varepsilon \} $ are disjoint and plurifine open. Indeed, near each point we have $u-v=(u+h)-(v+h)$ for a smooth local potential $h$ of $\theta $, and $u+h$, $v+h$ are plurisubharmonic, so by proposition 10.5.1 the function $u-v$ is plurifinely continuous off the common pole set of $u$ and $v$, which is disjoint from both sets. Moreover $w$ equals $v-\varepsilon $ on the first set and $u$ on the second. By plurifine locality (proposition 15.1.5),

\[ \mu _ w(X)\geq \mu _ v(\{ u<v-\varepsilon \} )+\mu _ u(\{ u>v-\varepsilon \} ). \]

The left side is at most $\operatorname {vol}(\{ \theta \} )$ by proposition 15.8.2. The complement of $\{ u>v-\varepsilon \} $ consists of $\{ u\leq v-\varepsilon \} $, which is contained in $\{ u<v\} $ up to the common pole set of $u$ and $v$, and that pluripolar set carries no mass. Hence

\[ \mu _ v(\{ u<v-\varepsilon \} )\leq \operatorname {vol}(\{ \theta \} )-\mu _ u(X)+\mu _ u(\{ u<v\} ). \]

As $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$, the sets $\{ u<v-\varepsilon \} $ increase to $\{ u<v\} $, which proves the inequality. If $u$ has full mass, the last two terms cancel. If $u\geq v-O(1)$, then $w-u$ is bounded, so $\mu _ w(X)=\mu _ u(X)$ by corollary 15.7.5, and the same computation with $\mu _ u(X)$ in place of the volume gives the inequality without these terms. $\square$

Corollary 15.8.6. Let $u,v,\rho \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $u\geq v-O(1)$ and $u\geq \rho -O(1)$. If $u\geq v$ almost everywhere for $\mu _ u$, then $u\geq v$ almost everywhere for $\mu _\rho $. If, in addition, the class $\{ \theta \} $ is big and $u$ has minimal singularities, then $u\geq v$ everywhere on $X$.

Proof. After adding a constant to $\rho $ we may assume $\rho \leq u$. For $0<t<1$ put $q_ t=(1-t)v+t\rho $. Then $u\geq q_ t-O(1)$, and $\{ u<q_ t\} \subset \{ u<v\} $, because $u<(1-t)v+t\rho \leq (1-t)v+tu$ forces $u<v$. Multilinearity and proposition 15.8.5 therefore give

\[ t^ n\mu _\rho (\{ u<q_ t\} ) \leq \mu _{q_ t}(\{ u<q_ t\} ) \leq \mu _ u(\{ u<q_ t\} )\leq \mu _ u(\{ u<v\} )=0. \]

As $t\downarrow 0$, every point where $u<v$ and $\rho >-\infty $ eventually lies in $\{ u<q_ t\} $, so Fatou's lemma, applied along a sequence $t\downarrow 0$, gives $\mu _\rho (\{ u<v\} \cap \{ \rho >-\infty \} )=0$. Since $\mu _\rho $ gives no mass to the pole set of $\rho $, this proves the first assertion.

Now let the class be big and $u$ have minimal singularities. The class contains a Kähler current $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq \delta \omega $ with $\delta >0$. Then $u\geq \rho -O(1)$ by minimality, and $\mu _\rho \geq \delta ^ n\omega ^ n$, because multilinearity expands $\langle (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho )^ n\rangle =\langle (\delta \omega +(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho -\delta \omega ))^ n\rangle $ into positive terms, one of which is $\delta ^ n\omega ^ n$. The first assertion gives $u\geq v$ almost everywhere with respect to $\omega ^ n$, that is, almost everywhere for Lebesgue measure in charts. Near each point, $u$ and $v$ become plurisubharmonic after adding a common smooth local potential $h$ of $\theta $, and then $\max \{ u,v\} +h$ and $u+h$ are plurisubharmonic functions that agree almost everywhere; by theorem 8.2.1, theorem 3.2.2 they agree everywhere, that is, $u\geq v$ everywhere. $\square$

2 Non-pluripolar products

Let $X$ be a complex manifold and let $\varphi _1,\ldots ,\varphi _ p\in \mathrm{PSH}(X)$. When the functions $\varphi _ i$ are smooth, the differential form

2.1
\begin{equation} \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p \end{equation}

is defined by the usual calculus. A central problem in pluripotential theory is to extend this product to singular plurisubharmonic functions in a reasonable way.

In this book we use the non-pluripolar product. This construction, originating in the work of Bedford–Taylor and developed in the global setting by Guedj–Zeriahi and Boucksom–Eyssidieux–Guedj–Zeriahi, has two features that are essential for us: it is defined for arbitrary quasi-plurisubharmonic singularities, and it satisfies the monotonicity theorem for non-pluripolar masses. These two properties make it possible to compare singularity types through Monge–Ampère mass.

The chapter is organized as follows. We first recall the Bedford–Taylor product for locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions. We then introduce the non-pluripolar product and record its basic functorial and locality properties. After a short discussion of quasi-continuous functions and capacity, we prove the convergence and concentration results used later in the theory of envelopes. The key tools extracted from this chapter are the monotonicity theorem, semicontinuity theorem, the concentration theorem for envelopes and the domination principle.

The reader may consult [BEGZ10] and [DDNLsurv] for broader background. Our presentation is adapted to the later use of envelopes and singularity types, so some estimates are stated in a form slightly more specific than in the standard references.

2.1 Bedford–Taylor theory

Let $X$ be a complex manifold and $\varphi _1,\ldots ,\varphi _ p\in \mathrm{PSH}(X)$ ($p\in \mathbb {N}$) be locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions on $X$
Note. In the literature, some people use $\mathrm{PSH}(X)\cap L^{\infty }_{\mathrm{loc}}(X)$ to denote the set of such functions, which is an abuse of notation. However, this is legitimate thanks to the rigidity theorem 1.1.14.
. In this case, there is a canonical definition of the Monge–Ampère type product (2.1).

Definition 2.1.1. We define the closed positive $(p,p)$-current (2.1) on $X$ as follows: We proceed by induction on $p\geq 0$. When $p=0$, we define (2.1) as the $(0,0)$-current $[X]$. When $p>0$, we let

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p\coloneqq \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\left( \varphi _1\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _2 \wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p \right). \]

We call this product the Bedford–Taylor product .

Remark 2.1.2. There is also a slightly more general version of this construction. Given a closed positive current $T$, one can also define the product

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p \wedge T \]

in a very similar way.

Proposition 2.1.3. The product $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p$ is a closed positive $(p,p)$-current on $X$. Moreover, the product is symmetric in the $\varphi _ i$'s.

Proof. The recursive construction here is the same as the one in the positive Bedford--Taylor recursion. That result proves that each step is a positive current of order zero, so multiplication by the next locally bounded potential is defined; applying $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm c}$ makes the next product closed. Symmetry is proved, together with monotone continuity, in the Bedford--Taylor continuity theorem. Its proof uses the common-collar integration-by-parts identity and the Chern--Levine--Nirenberg bound, not the incorrect claim that $\mathrm du\wedge\mathrm d^{\mathrm c}u\wedge T$ is closed. Since all constructions agree in overlapping charts, they define the stated global current.

See [Proposition 3.3, Corollary 3.12, GZ17].
Note. In [Proposition 3.3, GZ17], the authors claim that $\mathrm{d}u\wedge \mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{c}}u\wedge T$ is closed, which is wrong already in the smooth case.
The proof relies crucially on an important estimate, known as the Chern–Levine–Nirenberg inequality. See [CLN69] or [Theorem 3.9, GZ17].

The Bedford–Taylor theory has many satisfactory properties.

Theorem 2.1.4. Let $(\varphi _ i^ j)_{j\in \mathbb {Z}_{>0}}$ be decreasing sequences (resp. increasing sequences) of locally bounded psh functions on $X$ converging (resp. converging a.e.) to locally bounded psh functions $\varphi _ i$'s, where $i=0,\ldots ,p$. Then

\[ \varphi _0^ j\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1^ j\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p^ j\rightharpoonup \varphi _0\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p \]

as $j\to \infty $. In particular, if $(\varphi _0^ j)_ j$ is the constant sequence $1$, we have

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1^ j\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p^ j\rightharpoonup \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p. \]

Proof. On a relatively compact coordinate ball, the decreasing case, including the factor $\varphi_0^j$, is weighted Bedford--Taylor decreasing continuity. The increasing case is weighted increasing continuity, which allows almost-everywhere convergence to the specified locally bounded representatives. The local bounds needed in these results hold on a slightly larger compact set: in the decreasing case they are bounded below by the limit and above by the first term; in the increasing case they are bounded below by the first term and above by the limit, using almost-everywhere order and uniqueness of plurisubharmonic representatives.

For a compactly supported test form on $X$, use a finite partition of unity subordinate to such balls and sum the local limits. This proves weak convergence of the currents globally. Taking the auxiliary factor constantly equal to $1$ gives the unweighted assertion.

Here the notation $\rightharpoonup $ denotes the weak-* convergence of currents.

We refer to [Theorem 3.18, Theorem 3.23, GZ17] for the proofs.

By contrast, we emphasize that the Bedford–Taylor product is not continuous with respect to the $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$-convergence in general. A simple example can be found in [Example 3.25, GZ17].

Lemma 2.1.5. Let $Q\subseteq \mathbb {C}^ n$ and $D\subseteq \mathbb {C}^ m$ be domains, let $p_2\colon Q\times D\to D$ be the second projection, and let $\eta $ be a smooth real $(m,m)$-form on $D$. Let $u_1,\ldots ,u_ n$ be locally bounded psh functions on $Q\times D$, and put

\[ M=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ n,\qquad M_ z=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}_ x(u_1)_ z\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}_ x(u_ n)_ z. \]

Here $(u_ r)_ z(x)=u_ r(x,z)$. For every bounded Borel function $h$ with compact support in $Q\times D$,

2.2
\begin{equation} \int _{Q\times D}h\, p_2^*\eta \wedge M =\int _ D\left(\int _ Q h_ z\, M_ z\right)\eta (z), \qquad h_ z(x)=h(x,z). \end{equation}

Proof. By linearity, it suffices to consider $\eta \geq 0$. We first prove (2.2) when $h$ is continuous. On a product neighborhood of $\operatorname{Supp}h$, choose smooth psh functions $u_ r^{(l)}\searrow u_ r$, $1\leq r\leq n$. For the smooth approximants, decompose each $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ r^{(l)}$ according to the $x$- and $z$-directions. After wedging with $p_2^*\eta $, every term containing a factor $\mathrm{d}z_\alpha $ or $\mathrm{d}\bar z_\beta $ vanishes; hence only the factors $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}_ x(u_ r^{(l)})_ z$ remain, and (2.2) follows from the usual Fubini theorem. As $l\to \infty $, the Bedford–Taylor monotone continuity theorem theorem 2.1.4 gives convergence on $Q\times D$ and on every fibre. Fix $K\Subset Q$ and $L\Subset D$ containing the projections of $\operatorname{Supp}h$. The Chern–Levine–Nirenberg estimate [Theorem 3.9, GZ17], together with the local uniform bounds for $u_ r^{(l)}$, bounds the masses of the fibre products on $K$ uniformly for $z\in L$ and $l$. Since $h_ z=0$ for $z\notin L$, dominated convergence proves (2.2).

It remains to pass from continuous to bounded Borel functions. Fix relatively compact open sets $U\Subset Q$ and $V\Subset D$. If $\chi \in C_ c(U)$, then

\[ F_\chi (z)\coloneqq \int _ U\chi \, M_ z, \qquad z\in V, \]

is Borel measurable. Indeed, choose the smooth decreasing approximations on a neighborhood of $\overline U\times \overline V$ and set

\[ M_ z^{(l)}\coloneqq \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}_ x(u_1^{(l)})_ z\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}_ x(u_ n^{(l)})_ z. \]

The functions $z\mapsto \int _ U\chi \, M_ z^{(l)}$ are continuous on $V$ and converge pointwise to $F_\chi $ by theorem 2.1.4.

Let $\mathcal{A}_{U,V}$ be the vector space of bounded Borel functions $\chi $ on $U$ for which $F_\chi $ is Borel measurable on $V$. It is closed under bounded increasing pointwise limits of non-negative functions by the monotone convergence theorem. Taking compactly supported continuous cut-off functions increasing to $1$ on $U$ shows that $1\in \mathcal{A}_{U,V}$. Thus $\mathcal{A}_{U,V}$ contains the algebra $\mathbb {R}+C_ c(U)$, which generates the Borel $\sigma $-algebra of $U$. The functional monotone class theorem therefore shows that $\mathcal{A}_{U,V}$ contains every bounded Borel function on $U$.

Next, let $\mathcal{B}_{U,V}$ be the vector space of bounded Borel functions $g$ on $U\times V$ such that

\[ z\longmapsto \int _ U g_ z\, M_ z, \qquad g_ z(x)=g(x,z), \]

is Borel measurable on $V$. This space is again closed under bounded increasing pointwise limits of non-negative functions. It contains every product $g(x,z)=\chi (x)\psi (z)$ of bounded Borel functions, by the preceding paragraph. Finite sums of such products form an algebra containing the indicators of all Borel rectangles $A\times B\subseteq U\times V$; hence this algebra generates the Borel $\sigma $-algebra of $U\times V$. A second application of the functional monotone class theorem shows that $\mathcal{B}_{U,V}$ contains every bounded Borel function on $U\times V$.

To pass from $U\times V$ to $Q\times D$, choose exhaustions $U_ j\Subset Q$ and $V_ j\Subset D$ by relatively compact open sets. For a Borel set $E\subseteq Q\times D$, the preceding paragraph, applied to the restriction of $\mathds {1}_ E$ to $U_ j\times V_ j$, shows that

\[ G_ j(z)\coloneqq \mathds {1}_{V_ j}(z)\int _{U_ j}\mathds {1}_ E(x,z)\, M_ z \]

is Borel measurable on $D$. We may take both exhaustions increasing, so $G_ j\nearrow M_ z(E_ z)$ pointwise, where

\[ E_ z\coloneqq \{ x\in Q:(x,z)\in E\} . \]

Thus $z\mapsto M_ z(E_ z)$ is Borel measurable on $D$. We may therefore define

\[ \lambda (E)\coloneqq \int _ D M_ z(E_ z)\, \eta (z). \]

The monotone convergence theorem, first on each fibre and then on $D$, shows that $\lambda $ is a Borel measure on $Q\times D$. The local uniform mass bound above shows that $\lambda $ is locally finite and hence Radon. Approximation by non-negative simple functions gives

\[ \int _{Q\times D}h\, \mathrm{d}\lambda =\int _ D\left(\int _ Qh_ z\, M_ z\right)\eta (z) \]

for every non-negative Borel function $h$. For continuous compactly supported $h$, the first part of the proof shows that $\lambda $ agrees with $p_2^*\eta \wedge M$. The uniqueness of Radon measures then gives the same equality for every bounded Borel function $h$ with compact support. $\square$

2.2 The non-pluripolar products

The proofs of the standard results recalled in this section can be found in [BEGZ10].

Let $X$ be a complex manifold.

Definition 2.2.1. Let $\varphi _1,\ldots ,\varphi _ p\in \mathrm{PSH}(X)$. We set

\[ O_ k \coloneqq \bigcap _{j=1}^ p \{ \varphi _ j>-k\} ,\quad k\in \mathbb {N}. \]

We say that $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p$ is well-defined if for each connected open subset $U\subseteq X$, any smooth positive $(1,1)$-form $\omega $ on $U$, and each compact subset $K\subseteq U$, we have

2.3
\begin{equation} \sup _{k\geq 0} \int _{K\cap O_ k}\left.\left(\bigwedge _{j=1}^ p \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\varphi _ j\lor (-k))\right)\right|_ U\wedge \omega ^{\dim U-p}<\infty . \end{equation}

In this case, we define the non-pluripolar product $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p$ by

2.4
\begin{equation} \mathds {1}_{O_ k}\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p=\mathds {1}_{O_ k}\bigwedge _{j=1}^ p \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\left( \varphi _ j\lor (-k)\right) \end{equation}

on $\bigcup _{k\geq 0} O_ k$ and extend it by zero to $X$.

As recalled in section 1.3, an $\mathcal{F}$-open subset means an open subset with respect to the plurifine topology.

Proposition 2.2.2. Let $\varphi _1,\ldots ,\varphi _ p\in \mathrm{PSH}(X)$.

  1. The product $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p$ is local with respect to the plurifine topology in the following sense: Let $O\subseteq X$ be an $\mathcal{F}$-open subset and $\psi _1,\ldots ,\psi _ p\in \mathrm{PSH}(X)$. Assume that

    \[ \varphi _ j|_{O}=\psi _ j|_{O},\quad j=1,\ldots ,p, \]

    and that

    \[ \bigwedge _{j=1}^ p \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ j\textup{ and } \bigwedge _{j=1}^ p \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi _ j \]

    are both well-defined, then

    2.5
    \begin{equation} \left.\left(\bigwedge _{j=1}^ p \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ j\right)\right|_{O}=\left.\left(\bigwedge _{j=1}^ p \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi _ j\right)\right|_{O}. \end{equation}

    If furthermore $O$ is open in the usual topology, then the product

    \[ \bigwedge _{j=1}^ p \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ j|_{O} \]

    on $O$ is well-defined and

    2.6
    \begin{equation} \left. \left(\bigwedge _{j=1}^ p \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ j\right)\right|_{O}=\bigwedge _{j=1}^ p \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ j|_{O}. \end{equation}

    Let $\mathcal{U}$ be an open covering of $X$. Then $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p$ is well-defined if and only if each product

    \[ \bigwedge _{j=1}^ p \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ j|_{U},\quad U\in \mathcal{U}. \]

  2. The current $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p$ and the fact that it is well-defined depend only on the currents $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ j$, not on the choice of the $\varphi _ j$'s nor on the ordering of the $\varphi _ j$'s.

  3. When $\varphi _1,\ldots ,\varphi _ p\in L^{\infty }_{\mathrm{loc}}(X)$, the non-pluripolar product $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p$ is well-defined and is equal to the Bedford–Taylor product. More generally, if $q\leq p$, and we only assume that $\varphi _1,\ldots ,\varphi _ q\in L^{\infty }_{\mathrm{loc}}(X)$, and that the non-pluripolar product $T\coloneqq \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _{q+1}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p$ is well-defined, then the non-pluripolar product $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p$ is also well-defined, and is equal to the Bedford–Taylor product $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ q\wedge T$ as in 2.1.2.

  4. Assume that $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p$ is well-defined. Then $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p$ puts no mass on pluripolar sets.

  5. Assume that $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p$ is well-defined. Then $\bigwedge _{j=1}^ p \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ j$ is a closed positive $(p,p)$-current on $X$.
    Note. This current is in fact strongly positive, as shown in [Xiabdiv2].

  6. The product is multilinear: Let $\psi _1\in \mathrm{PSH}(X)$, $a,b>0$ then

    2.7
    \begin{equation} \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(a\varphi _1+b\psi _1)\wedge \bigwedge _{j=2}^ p \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ j =a\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1\wedge \bigwedge _{j=2}^ p \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ j + b\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi _1\wedge \bigwedge _{j=2}^ p \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ j \end{equation}

    in the sense that the left-hand side is well-defined if and only if both terms on the right-hand side are well-defined, and the equality holds in that case.

Proof. For bounded potentials, Bedford--Taylor plurifine locality identifies their products on every plurifine open set on which the potentials agree. Apply it to the bounded truncations $\max\{\varphi_j,-k\}$ and $\max\{\psi_j,-k\}$, on the intersection of $O$ with the finite-level loci of all the potentials. The truncated products agree there. Let $k\to\infty$ in the increasing restriction measures defining the non-pluripolar product. The omitted pole sets have zero mass by construction, so equality holds on all of $O$. This limiting argument, including invariance under changes of local potentials, is established in the local calculus of non-pluripolar products.

In view of 3, we do not need to specify whether our product $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p$ is the Bedford–Taylor product or the non-pluripolar product when the $\varphi _ i$'s are all locally bounded.

Example 2.2.3. Let $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X)$, then

2.8
\begin{equation} (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^1=\mathds {1}_{\{ \varphi >-\infty \} }\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi . \end{equation}

Here $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^1$ on the left-hand side denotes the non-pluripolar product, while $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $ on the right-hand side denotes the usual derivative in the sense of current.

The problem is local, so we may assume that $X=\Delta ^ n$, the unit polydisk in $\mathbb {C}^ n$. Take $f\in C^{\infty }_ c(\Delta ^ n)$ with $f\geq 0$. We need to show that

\[ \lim _{k\to \infty } \int _{\Delta ^ n} \mathds {1}_{\{ \varphi >-k\} }f\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\bigl (\varphi \lor (-k)\bigr )\wedge \omega ^{n-1}=\int _{\Delta ^ n} \mathds {1}_{\{ \varphi >-\infty \} }f\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \wedge \omega ^{n-1}, \]

where $\omega $ is the standard Kähler form on $\mathbb {C}^ n$. By the dominated convergence theorem, this reduces immediately to the $1$-dimensional case:

\[ \lim _{k\to \infty } \int _{\Delta ^1} \mathds {1}_{\{ \varphi >-k\} }f\, \Delta \bigl (\varphi \lor (-k)\bigr )=\int _{\Delta ^1} \mathds {1}_{\{ \varphi >-\infty \} }f\, \Delta \varphi , \]

for any subharmonic function $\varphi $ on the unit disk $\Delta ^1$, which is a special case of [Proposition 3.3, EK24].

Definition 2.2.4. Let $T_1,\ldots ,T_ p$ be closed positive $(1,1)$-currents on $X$. We say that $T_1\wedge \cdots \wedge T_ p$ is well-defined if there exists an open covering $\mathcal{U}$ of $X$ such that on each $U\in \mathcal{U}$, we can find $\varphi _ j^ U\in \mathrm{PSH}(U)$ ($j=1,\ldots ,p$) such that

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ j^ U=T_ j,\quad j=1,\ldots ,p \]

and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1^ U\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p^ U$ is well-defined. In this case, we define the non-pluripolar product $T_1\wedge \cdots \wedge T_ p$ as the closed positive $(p,p)$-current on $X$ defined by

2.9
\begin{equation} \left(T_1\wedge \cdots \wedge T_ p\right)|_ U=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _1^ U\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ p^ U,\quad U\in \mathcal{U}. \end{equation}
The product $T_1\wedge \cdots \wedge T_ p$ is independent of the choices we made thanks to proposition 2.2.2 1 and 2.

proposition 2.2.2 can be formulated in terms of currents without any difficulty.

Remark 2.2.5. Similar to 2.1.2, there is also an extension of the non-pluripolar theory allowing us to define

\[ T_1\wedge \cdots \wedge T_ p\cap T \]

for any closed positive current $T$. This is the relative non-pluripolar product introduced by Vu [Vu20]. Unlike the relative Bedford–Taylor products, the relative non-pluripolar products present some pathological behaviors. For example, they are not linear in general.

Remark 2.2.6. Another possible generalization of the non-pluripolar products is motivated by proposition 2.2.2 1. One could begin by defining a generalized notion of plurisubharmonic functions on $\mathcal{F}$-open sets, called $\mathcal{F}$-plurisubharmonic functions and define their non-pluripolar products. See [EKFW11, EKW14].

Proposition 2.2.7. Let $X$ be a compact Kähler manifold and let $T_1,\ldots ,T_ p$ be closed positive $(1,1)$-currents on $X$. Then $T_1\wedge \cdots \wedge T_ p$ is well-defined.

Proof. Write $T_j=\theta_j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm c}\varphi_j$ by the global potential lemma, and fix a Kähler form $\omega$. Choose $C$ so large that every $\tau_j=\theta_j+C\omega$ is semipositive. The compact mass estimate proves \[\int_X\langle T_1\wedge\cdots\wedge T_p\rangle\wedge\omega^{n-p}\leq\int_X\tau_1\wedge\cdots\wedge\tau_p\wedge\omega^{n-p}<\infty.\] Thus the increasing restricted truncation measures have locally finite coefficient measures. The construction therefore defines a current everywhere; closedness of the truncation limit and the local-calculus theorem show that it is independent of the chosen potentials and patches between charts. Compactness and the Kähler form are used in the displayed uniform mass bound.

This proposition explains why we usually work in the setting of compact Kähler manifolds.

Corollary 2.2.8. Let $X$ and $Z$ be connected compact Kähler manifolds of dimensions $n$ and $m$, respectively, and let $p_1\colon X\times Z\to X$ and $p_2\colon X\times Z\to Z$ be the projections. Let $\theta $ be a closed real smooth $(1,1)$-form on $X$, let $\phi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, and let $\gamma $ be a closed smooth positive $(1,1)$-form on $Z$. Put

\[ Y=X\times Z,\qquad \Theta =p_1^*\theta +p_2^*\gamma ,\qquad \Phi =p_1^*\phi . \]

Suppose that $W\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\Theta )$ and $\Phi -C\leq W\leq \Phi +C$ for some $C>0$. Set

\[ T=\Theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}W,\qquad S=\Theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Phi , \]

and write $W_ z(x)=W(x,z)$. Then $W_ z\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ for every $z\in Z$. For $0\leq j\leq n$ and every smooth real $(m,m)$-form $\eta $ on $Z$,

2.10
\begin{equation} \begin{aligned} & \int _ Y(W-\Phi )p_2^*\eta \wedge T^ j\wedge S^{n-j}\\ & \qquad =\int _ Z\left(\int _ X(W_ z-\phi )\, \theta _{W_ z}^ j\wedge \theta _\phi ^{n-j}\right)\eta (z), \end{aligned} \end{equation}

and

2.13
\begin{equation} \int _ Y p_2^*\eta \wedge T^ n =\int _ Z\left(\int _ X\theta _{W_ z}^ n\right)\eta (z). \end{equation}

Proof. By linearity, it suffices to consider $\eta \geq 0$. All the non-pluripolar products that occur below are well-defined by proposition 2.2.7. We work on a relatively compact product chart $Q\times D\subseteq X\times Z$ and let $a$ and $b$ be smooth local potentials of $p_1^*\theta $ and $p_2^*\gamma $. After subtracting constants, put $w=W+a+b\leq 0$ and $f=\Phi +a+b\leq 0$. For $k\geq 1$, set

\[ \begin{aligned} w_ k& =w\lor (-k),& f_ k& =f\lor (-k),\\ O_ k& =\{ w>-k\} \cap \{ f>-k\} ,& \mu _{j,k}& =\mathds {1}_{O_ k}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_ k)^ j\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f_ k)^{n-j}. \end{aligned} \]

For $z\in D$, write $w_ z(x)=w(x,z)$ and $f_ z(x)=f(x,z)$, and set

\[ \mu _{j,k,z} =\mathds {1}_{\{ w_ z>-k\} \cap \{ f_ z>-k\} } \bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}_ x(w_ z\lor (-k))\bigr )^ j\wedge \bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}_ x(f_ z\lor (-k))\bigr )^{n-j}. \]

Apply 2.1.5 with $u_1=\cdots =u_ j=w_ k$, $u_{j+1}=\cdots =u_ n=f_ k$, and

\[ h=\rho G\mathds {1}_{O_ k},\qquad \rho \in \mathcal{C}^{\infty }_ c(Q\times D),\quad \rho \geq 0, \]

where $G$ is any bounded non-negative Borel function. This gives

2.16
\begin{equation} \int _{Q\times D}\rho G\, p_2^*\eta \wedge \mu _{j,k} =\int _ D\left(\int _ Q\rho _ zG_ z\, \mu _{j,k,z}\right)\eta (z). \end{equation}

By plurifine locality, $\mu _{j,k}$ increases to the local expression of $T^ j\wedge S^{n-j}$, while, for every $z\in D$, $\mu _{j,k,z}$ increases to the local expression of $\theta _{W_ z}^ j\wedge \theta _\phi ^{n-j}$. Taking $G=\delta +C\geq 0$ in (2.16), applying monotone convergence, subtracting the corresponding constant-weight identity, and summing over a non-negative partition of unity proves (2.10). The same argument with constant weight and degree $n$ proves (2.13). $\square$

2.3 Quasi-continuous functions

Let $X$ be a compact Kähler manifold of dimension $n$ and $\theta $ be a closed real smooth $(1,1)$-form on $X$ representing a big cohomology class.

Definition 2.3.1. Let $A\subseteq X$ be a Borel subset. The $\theta $-capacity $\operatorname{Cap}_{\theta }(A)$ of $A$ is defined as

\[ \operatorname{Cap}_{\theta }(A)\coloneqq \sup \left\{ \int _ A\theta _{\varphi }^ n : \varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ),V_{\theta }-1\leq \varphi \leq V_{\theta } \right\} . \]
Here $V_{\theta }$ is defined in (2.20) below. As we will recall below, the non-pluripolar product $\theta _{\varphi }^ n$ is well-defined. The capacity is not very sensitive to the choice of $\theta $:

Theorem 2.3.2. Let $\theta '$ be another closed real smooth $(1,1)$-form on $X$ representing a big cohomology class. Then there are continuous functions $f,g\colon [0,\infty )\rightarrow [0,\infty )$, with $f(0)=g(0)=0$, such that for any Borel subset $E\subseteq X$, we have

\[ \operatorname{Cap}_{\theta }(E)\leq f\left( \operatorname{Cap}_{\theta '}(E)\right),\quad \operatorname{Cap}_{\theta '}(E)\leq g\left( \operatorname{Cap}_{\theta }(E)\right). \]

Proof. Use the comparison with Kähler capacity with the reference potentials defining $\operatorname{Cap}_\theta$ and $\operatorname{Cap}_{\theta'}$. It gives continuous nondecreasing functions $F,G,F',G'$, all zero at zero, with \[\operatorname{Cap}_\theta\leq F\circ\operatorname{Cap}_\omega,\quad\operatorname{Cap}_\omega\leq G\circ\operatorname{Cap}_\theta,\quad\operatorname{Cap}_{\theta'}\leq F'\circ\operatorname{Cap}_\omega,\quad\operatorname{Cap}_\omega\leq G'\circ\operatorname{Cap}_{\theta'}.\] The reference potentials have positive mass because the classes are big. Set $f=F\circ G'$ and $g=F'\circ G$. Composing the respective inequalities proves the two assertions for every Borel set. The compositions are continuous and vanish at zero, as required.

A more general result is proved in [Lu21]. Similar comparison results hold between $\theta $-capacity and the classical Bedford–Taylor capacity, see [Section 9.2, GZ17] for the proof. As a consequence, we can freely apply the results in [Section 4.2, GZ17], even though capacity has a different meaning there.

Definition 2.3.3. Let $U$ be an open subset of $X$. A function $f\colon U\rightarrow [-\infty ,\infty ]$ is quasi-continuous if it is Borel measurable and for any $\epsilon >0$, there is an open subset $G\subseteq U$ such that

  1. $\operatorname{Cap}_{\theta }(G)\leq \epsilon $;

  2. $f|_{U\setminus G}$ is real-valued and continuous.

Thanks to theorem 2.3.2, the notion of quasi-continuous functions is independent of the choice of $\theta $. Note that if $f,g\colon U\rightarrow [-\infty ,\infty ]$ are two Borel measurable functions equal quasi-everywhere (see definition 1.1.15), then $f$ is quasi-continuous if and only if $g$ is.

Theorem 2.3.4. Let $A\subseteq X$ be a Borel set. Then the following are equivalent:

  1. $A$ is pluripolar;

  2. $\operatorname{Cap}_{\theta }(A)=0$.

Proof. By capacity comparison, zero $\theta$-capacity is equivalent to zero Kähler capacity. The comparison with coordinate-ball capacities makes this equivalent to zero local Bedford--Taylor capacity in every member of a finite coordinate cover. The local zero-capacity theorem identifies these zero sets with the pluripolar sets. This proves both implications. For a Borel set, one may first apply the local statement to compact subsets and then use the capacitability and zero-capacity characterization proved with that theorem; a mere measure-zero argument would not suffice.

See [Theorem 4.40, GZ17] for the proof.

Example 2.3.5. A quasi-plurisubharmonic function on an open subset $U\subseteq X$ is always quasi-continuous. See [Theorem 4.20, GZ17] for the proof.

More generally, if $\varphi ,\psi $ are two quasi-plurisubharmonic functions on $U$, then the following function

\[ f(x)\coloneqq \begin{cases} \varphi (x)-\psi (x),& \textup{if }(\varphi \lor \psi )(x)\neq -\infty ;\\ \infty ,& \textup{otherwise} \end{cases} \]
is quasi-continuous.
Note. In the literature, people usually say carelessly that $\varphi -\psi $ is quasi-continuous.

Definition 2.3.6. Let $U$ be an open subset of $X$. Let $(f_ i)_{i\in I}$ be a net of Borel measurable functions $f_ i\colon U\rightarrow [-\infty ,\infty ]$, and $f\colon U\rightarrow [-\infty ,\infty ]$ be a Borel measurable function. We say $(f_ i)_{i\in I}$ converges to $f$ in capacity if for any $\delta >0$, we have

\[ \lim _{i\in I} \operatorname{Cap}_{\theta }\Bigl ( \{ f_ i>f+\delta \} \Bigr )=0,\quad \lim _{i\in I} \operatorname{Cap}_{\theta }\Bigl ( \{ f_ i<f-\delta \} \Bigr )=0\footnotemark . \]
Note. It is very tempting to write $\lim _{i\in I} \operatorname{Cap}_{\theta }\left( \{ |f_ i-f|>\delta \} \right)=0$, as in [Definition 4.23, GZ17] for example. But the set where $f_ i-f$ is not defined is not a pluripolar set in general. Hence this abuse of notation is not acceptable.
We sometimes write $f_ i\xrightarrow {\mathrm{C}} f$.
Note that $f$ is not uniquely determined by the net $(f_ i)_{i\in I}$. Thanks to theorem 2.3.2, the notion of convergence in capacity is independent of the choice of $\theta $.

Proposition 2.3.7. Let $U$ be an open subset of $X$. Let $(\varphi _ i)_{i\in I}$ be a net in $\mathrm{PSH}(U,\theta )$ and $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(U,\theta )$. Assume one of the following conditions holds:

  1. $(\varphi _ i)_{i\in I}$ is decreasing and $\varphi $ is the limit of the net;

  2. $(\varphi _ i)_{i\in I}$ is increasing and converges almost everywhere to $\varphi $.

Then $(\varphi _ i)_{i\in I}$ converges to $\varphi $ in capacity.

See [Proposition 4.25, GZ17] for the proof. The reference concerns only the sequence case, but the proof works for nets as well.

Theorem 2.3.8. Let $U$ be an open subset of $X$. Let $(\chi _ i\colon U\rightarrow [0,\infty ))_ i$ be a sequence of quasi-continuous functions. Assume that the sequence is uniformly bounded and converges in capacity to $\chi \colon U\rightarrow [0,\infty )$. For each $j=1,\ldots ,p$, let $(\varphi _ j^ i)_ i$ be uniformly bounded sequences in $\mathrm{PSH}(U,\theta _ j)$ converging in capacity to bounded $\varphi _ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(U,\theta _ j)$. Then

\[ \chi _ i\, \bigwedge _{j=1}^ p \theta _{j,\varphi _ j^ i}\rightharpoonup \chi \, \bigwedge _{j=1}^ p \theta _{j,\varphi _ j} \]

weakly as currents on $U$.

See [Theorem 4.26, GZ17]
Note. In the displayed formula in the statement of [Theorem 4.26, GZ17], $1\leq j\leq p$ should be $1\leq i\leq p$ on both sides.
.

2.4 Properties of non-pluripolar products

Let $X$ be a connected compact Kähler manifold of dimension $n$ and $\theta ,\theta _1,\ldots ,\theta _ n$ be closed real smooth $(1,1)$-forms on $X$.

We write

2.19
\begin{equation} \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )_{>0}=\left\{ \varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ): \int _ X\theta _{\varphi }^ n>0 \right\} . \end{equation}

The non-pluripolar product $\theta _{\varphi }^ n$ is well-defined thanks to proposition 2.2.7.

Remark 2.4.1. Suppose that $X$ is a connected complex manifold of dimension $0$, namely, $X$ is a single point. In this case, by definition, the non-pluripolar product $\theta _{\varphi }^ n$ is given by the current of integration at the unique point. So $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )_{>0}=\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )\cong \mathbb {R}$ in this case and $\int _ X \theta _{\varphi }^ n=1$ for all $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$.

Recall the following basic result:

Proposition 2.4.2. Assume that $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )_{>0}$ is non-empty. Then the cohomology class $\{ \theta \} $ is big.

See [Proposition 1.22, BEGZ10]. We recall a few basic facts about the non-pluripolar masses.

Proposition 2.4.3. Let $\pi \colon Y\rightarrow X$ be a proper bimeromorphic morphism from a Kähler manifold $Y$ and $T_1,\ldots ,T_ n$ be closed positive $(1,1)$-currents on $X$. Then

\[ \int _ Y (\pi ^*T_1)\wedge \cdots \wedge (\pi ^*T_ n)=\int _ X T_1\wedge \cdots \wedge T_ n. \]

Similarly, if $S_1,\ldots ,S_ n$ are closed positive $(1,1)$-currents on $Y$, then

\[ \int _ Y S_1\wedge \cdots \wedge S_ n=\int _ X \left(\pi _*S_1\right)\wedge \cdots \wedge \left(\pi _*S_ n\right). \]
Here $\pi ^*T_ i$ is defined as follows: Write $T_ i=\theta _ i+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ i$ for some closed smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\theta _ i$ on $X$ and $\varphi _ i\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta _ i)$, then $\pi ^*T_ i=\pi ^*\theta _ i+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\pi ^*\varphi _ i$. The pull-back $\pi ^*T_ i$ is clearly independent of the choices of $\theta _ i$ and $\varphi _ i$.

Proof. The assertions follow from proposition 2.2.2 1 on the Zariski open set where $\pi $ is an isomorphism. The exceptional locus is pluripolar, and the relevant non-pluripolar products put no mass on pluripolar sets by proposition 2.2.2 4. $\square$

Theorem 2.4.4. Let $\varphi _0,\varphi _1\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. Then the map

\[ [0,1] \ni t\mapsto \log \int _ X \theta _{t\varphi _1+(1-t)\varphi _0}^ n \]

is concave.

More generally, given closed positive $(1,1)$-currents $T_1,\ldots ,T_ n$ on $X$, we have

\[ \int _ X T_1\wedge \cdots \wedge T_ n\geq \prod _{i=1}^ n \left( \int _ X T_ i^ n \right)^{1/n}. \]
See [DDNL19log] for the proof.

Remark 2.4.5. Here and in the sequel, when we write expressions like $t\varphi +(1-t)\psi $ for $\varphi ,\psi \in \mathrm{QPSH}(X)$, we will follow the convention that when $t=0$, the value is $\psi $ and when $t=1$, the value is $\varphi $.

We shall write

2.20
\begin{equation} V_{\theta }=\sup \left\{ \varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ):\varphi \leq 0 \right\} . \end{equation}

Note that the upper semicontinuous regularization $V_{\theta }^*$ of $V_{\theta }$ is itself a candidate of (2.20) if $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )\neq \varnothing $ as follows from proposition 1.2.2, therefore, $V_{\theta }=V_{\theta }^*\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ if $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )\neq \varnothing $.

The function $V_{\theta }$ should be regarded as a canonical representative of the least singular potentials in $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. We recall the following result:

Lemma 2.4.6. Suppose that $\{ \theta \} $ is big. The potential $V_{\theta }$ is locally bounded on $X\setminus \operatorname{nK}(\{ \theta \} )$.

Recall that $\operatorname{nK}(\{ \theta \} )$ is the non-Kähler locus introduced in definition 1.7.8.

Proof. By definition of $\operatorname{nK}(\{ \theta \} )$, it suffices to show that if $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ has analytic singularities and $\theta _{\varphi }$ is a Kähler current, then $V_{\theta }$ is locally bounded outside $\{ \varphi =-\infty \} $. After replacing $\varphi $ by $\varphi -\sup _ X\varphi $, the function $\varphi $ is a candidate in (2.20); hence $V_{\theta }\geq \varphi $ and $V_{\theta }\leq 0$. Since $\varphi $ is locally bounded outside its pole set, so is $V_{\theta }$. $\square$

The non-pluripolar product has a lower semicontinuity property.

Theorem 2.4.7 (Semicontinuity theorem). Let $\varphi _ j, \varphi _ j^ k\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta _ j)$ ($k\in \mathbb {N}$, $j=1,\ldots ,n$). Let $\chi _ k,\chi $ ($k\in \mathbb {N}$) be non-negative uniformly bounded quasi-continuous functions on $X$ such that $(\chi _ k)_ k$ converges to $\chi $ in capacity. Assume that for any $j=1,\ldots ,n$, as $k\to \infty $, either $\varphi _ j^ k$ decreases to $\varphi _ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta _ j)$ or increases to $\varphi _ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta _ j)$ almost everywhere. Then

2.21
\begin{equation} \varliminf _{k\to \infty }\int _ X\chi _ k \, \theta _{1,\varphi _1^ k}\wedge \cdots \wedge \theta _{n,\varphi _ n^ k} \geq \int _ X \chi \, \theta _{1,\varphi _1}\wedge \cdots \wedge \theta _{n,\varphi _ n}. \end{equation}

If in addition,

\[ \lim _{k\to \infty }\int _ X\theta _{1,\varphi _1^ k}\wedge \cdots \wedge \theta _{n,\varphi _ n^ k} =\int _ X \theta _{1,\varphi _1}\wedge \cdots \wedge \theta _{n,\varphi _ n}, \]

then

\[ \chi _ k \, \theta _{1,\varphi _1^ k}\wedge \cdots \wedge \theta _{n,\varphi _ n^ k}\rightharpoonup \chi \, \theta _{1,\varphi _1}\wedge \cdots \wedge \theta _{n,\varphi _ n} \]
weakly as measures
Note. Recall that the weak convergence of Radon measures is stronger than the weak convergence as currents in general. When the Radon measures have uniformly bounded total variation, they are equivalent.
. In particular, the limit in (2.21) exists and equality holds in (2.21).
See [Theorem 2.6, DDNLsurv] for the proof. The non-pluripolar mass is a monotone quantity with respect to the singularity type.
Theorem 2.4.8 (Monotonicity theorem). Let $\varphi _ j,\psi _ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta _ j)$ for $j=1,\ldots ,n$. Assume that $\varphi _ j \succeq \psi _ j$
Note. See definition 1.5.2 for the notation.
for every $j$, then
\[ \int _{X} \theta _{1,\varphi _1}\wedge \cdots \wedge \theta _{n,\varphi _ n} \geq \int _{X} \theta _{1,\psi _1}\wedge \cdots \wedge \theta _{n,\psi _ n}. \]

In particular, if $\varphi ,\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $\varphi \succeq \psi $, then

\[ \int _ X \theta _{\varphi }^ n\geq \int _ X \theta _{\psi }^ n. \]

In other words, the non-pluripolar mass increases as the potentials become less singular.

See [Theorem 1.1, DDNL18mono]. We will prove a vast extension of this theorem in proposition 6.1.16.

By this theorem, the non-pluripolar mass $\int _ X \theta _{\varphi }^ n$ can be used as a rough measure of the singularities of $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. In section 3.1, we shall refine this measure by defining the notion of $P$-envelope.

As a corollary, we obtain that

Corollary 2.4.9. Fix a directed set $I$. For each $j=1,\ldots ,n$, take an increasing net $(\varphi _ j^ i)_{i\in I}$ in $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta _ j)$, uniformly bounded from above. Set

\[ \varphi _ j\coloneqq \operatorname *{sup}_{i\in I}{}\! {}^{*} \varphi _ j^ i,\quad j=1,\ldots ,n. \]

Then

2.22
\begin{equation} \lim _{i\in I}\int _ X \theta _{1,\varphi _1^ i}\wedge \cdots \wedge \theta _{n,\varphi _ n^ i} = \int _ X \theta _{1,\varphi _1}\wedge \cdots \wedge \theta _{n,\varphi _ n}. \end{equation}

In particular, if the nets are indeed sequences, we have

\[ \theta _{1,\varphi _1^ i}\wedge \cdots \wedge \theta _{n,\varphi _ n^ i} \rightharpoonup \theta _{1,\varphi _1}\wedge \cdots \wedge \theta _{n,\varphi _ n}. \]

Proof. The final assertion follows from (2.22) together with theorem 2.4.7. It remains to establish (2.22).

We may assume that $I$ is infinite as there is nothing to prove otherwise. Thanks to theorem 2.4.8, we already know the $\leq $ inequality in (2.22). We prove the reverse inequality. When $I\cong \mathbb {Z}_{>0}$ as directed sets, the reverse inequality follows from theorem 2.4.7. In general, by Choquet's lemma proposition 1.2.3, we can find a countable infinite subset $R\subseteq I$ such that

\[ \operatorname *{sup}_{r\in R}{}\! {}^{*}\varphi ^ r_ j=\operatorname *{sup}_{i\in I}{}\! {}^{*}\varphi ^ i_ j \]

for all $j=1,\ldots ,n$. We fix a bijection $R\cong \mathbb {Z}_{>0}$. For any $j=1,\ldots ,n$, we will then denote elements $\varphi _ j^ r$ ($r\in R$) by $\varphi _ j^1,\varphi _ j^2,\dots $. We shall write

\[ \psi _ j^ a=\varphi _ j^1\lor \cdots \lor \varphi _ j^ a \]

for each $a\in \mathbb {Z}_{>0}$.

For every $a$ we may choose $i_ a\in I$ dominating the finitely many indices corresponding to $\varphi _ j^1,\ldots ,\varphi _ j^ a$ for all $j$. It then follows from the monotonicity theorem that

\[ \lim _{i\in I}\int _ X \theta _{1,\varphi _1^ i}\wedge \cdots \wedge \theta _{n,\varphi _ n^ i}\geq \lim _{a\to \infty }\int _ X \theta _{1,\psi _1^ a}\wedge \cdots \wedge \theta _{n,\psi _ n^ a}. \]

From the special case mentioned above, we know that the right-hand side is exactly the right-hand side of (2.22), so we conclude. $\square$

We prove an interesting inequality about the Monge–Ampère measure of the maximum of two potentials.

Lemma 2.4.10. Let $\varphi ,\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. Then

2.23
\begin{equation} \theta _{\varphi \lor \psi }^ n\geq \mathds {1}_{\{ \varphi \geq \psi \} }\, \theta _{\varphi }^ n+\mathds {1}_{\{ \varphi <\psi \} }\, \theta _{\psi }^ n. \end{equation}

In particular, if $\varphi \leq \psi $, then

\[ \mathds {1}_{\{ \varphi =\psi \} }\, \theta _{\varphi }^ n\leq \mathds {1}_{\{ \varphi =\psi \} }\, \theta _{\psi }^ n. \]
At a first sight, (2.23) might seem trivial, and it is if we replace $\{ \varphi \geq \psi \} $ by $\{ \varphi >\psi \} $. The difficulty really lies on understanding the contact set $\{ \varphi =\psi \} $.

Proof. For each $k\in \mathbb {N}$, we set

\[ \psi _ k=\psi \lor (V_{\theta }-k),\quad \varphi _ k=\varphi \lor (V_{\theta }-k). \]

For each $t>0$ and each $k\in \mathbb {N}$, we have

\[ \begin{split} \theta _{\psi _ k\lor (\varphi _ k+t)}^ n\geq & \mathds {1}_{\{ \psi _ k>\varphi _ k+t\} }\, \theta _{\psi _ k\lor (\varphi _ k+t)}^ n+\mathds {1}_{\{ \psi _ k<\varphi _ k+t\} }\, \theta _{\psi _ k\lor (\varphi _ k+t)}^ n\\ =& \mathds {1}_{\{ \psi _ k>\varphi _ k+t\} }\, \theta _{\psi _ k}^ n+\mathds {1}_{\{ \psi _ k<\varphi _ k+t\} }\, \theta _{\varphi _ k}^ n, \end{split} \]

where the equality follows from the plurifine locality of non-pluripolar products proposition 2.2.2 1. We observe that for any $t>0$, $\psi _ k\lor (\varphi _ k+t)\sim \psi _ k\lor \varphi _ k$. Therefore, the monotonicity theorem theorem 2.4.8 guarantees that

\[ \int _ X \theta _{\psi _ k\lor (\varphi _ k+t)}^ n=\int _ X\theta _{\psi _ k\lor \varphi _ k}^ n. \]

As $t\to 0+$,

\[ \theta _{\psi _ k\lor (\varphi _ k+t)}^ n\rightharpoonup \theta _{\psi _ k\lor \varphi _ k}^ n \]

by theorem 2.4.7.

Letting $t\to 0+$, we conclude that

\[ \theta _{\psi _ k\lor \varphi _ k}^ n\geq \mathds {1}_{\{ \psi _ k>\varphi _ k\} }\, \theta _{\psi _ k}^ n+\mathds {1}_{\{ \psi _ k\leq \varphi _ k\} }\, \theta _{\varphi _ k}^ n. \]

In particular, multiplying both sides by $\mathds {1}_{\{ \min \{ \varphi ,\psi \} >V_{\theta }-k\} }$ and applying proposition 2.2.2 1 again, we find that

\[ \begin{split} \mathds {1}_{\{ \min \{ \varphi ,\psi \} >V_{\theta }-k\} }\, \theta _{\psi \lor \varphi }^ n\geq & \mathds {1}_{\{ \min \{ \varphi ,\psi \} >V_{\theta }-k\} \cap \{ \psi \leq \varphi \} }\, \theta _{\varphi }^ n \\ & +\mathds {1}_{\{ \min \{ \varphi ,\psi \} >V_{\theta }-k\} \cap \{ \psi >\varphi \} }\, \theta _{\psi }^ n. \end{split} \]

Letting $k\to \infty $, we conclude (2.23). $\square$

Corollary 2.4.11. Let $(\varphi _ i)_{i\in I}$ be a net in $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, and $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ so that $\varphi _ i\to \varphi $ in $L^1$. Assume that $\varphi _ i\leq 0$ for all $i\in I$. Then

2.28
\begin{equation} \varlimsup _{i\in I} \int _{\{ \varphi _ i=0\} }\theta _{\varphi _ i}^ n\leq \int _{\{ \varphi =0\} } \theta _{\varphi }^ n. \end{equation}

Proof. Observe that $\varphi \leq 0$ by proposition 1.5.4.

Step 1. We first assume that $I\cong \mathbb {Z}_{>0}$ as directed sets. Namely, $(\varphi _ i)_{i>0}$ is a sequence.

For each $k>0$, let

\[ \psi _ k=\operatorname *{sup}_{j\geq k}{}\! {}^{*} \varphi _ j. \]

Then $(\psi _ k)_{k}$ is a decreasing sequence in $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with limit $\varphi $, see corollary 1.2.8 for example.

Thanks to 2.4.10, for each $k>0$, we have

\[ \mathds {1}_{\{ \varphi _ k=\psi _ k\} }\, \theta _{\varphi _ k}^ n\leq \mathds {1}_{\{ \varphi _ k=\psi _ k\} }\, \theta _{\psi _ k}^ n. \]

Multiplying both sides with $\mathds {1}_{\{ \varphi _ k=0\} }$, we find that

\[ \mathds {1}_{\{ \varphi _ k=0\} }\, \theta _{\varphi _ k}^ n\leq \mathds {1}_{\{ \varphi _ k=0\} }\, \theta _{\psi _ k}^ n. \]

Take $b,C>0$. For each $k>0$, we have

\[ \begin{split} \int _{\{ \varphi _ k=0\} } \theta _{\varphi _ k}^ n\leq \int _{\{ \varphi _ k=0\} } \theta _{\psi _ k}^ n\leq \int _{\{ \psi _ k=0\} } \theta _{\psi _ k}^ n\\ =\int _{\{ \psi _ k=0\} } \theta _{\psi _ k\lor (V_{\theta }-C)}^ n\leq \int _ X \mathrm{e}^{b\psi _ k}\, \theta _{\psi _ k\lor (V_{\theta }-C)}^ n, \end{split} \]

where the equality part follows from proposition 2.2.2 1.

Letting $k\to \infty $ with the help of theorem 2.4.7, we conclude that

\[ \varlimsup _{k\to \infty }\int _{\{ \varphi _ k=0\} } \theta _{\varphi _ k}^ n\leq \int _ X \mathrm{e}^{b\varphi }\, \theta _{\varphi \lor (V_{\theta }-C)}^ n. \]

Letting $b\to \infty $ and then $C\to \infty $, we conclude (2.28).

Step 2. We now handle the general net case.

Fix a volume form on $X$, so that we can talk about the $L^1$-norm on $X$ in the sequel.

Suppose that (2.28) fails. Then there is $\delta >0$ such that, for every $i_0\in I$, one can find $i\geq i_0$ satisfying

\[ \int _{\{ \varphi _ i=0\} }\theta _{\varphi _ i}^ n>\int _{\{ \varphi =0\} }\theta _{\varphi }^ n+\delta . \]

Since $\varphi _ i\to \varphi $ in $L^1$, for every $k>0$ there is $a_ k\in I$ such that

\[ \| \varphi _ i-\varphi \| _{L^1}<k^{-1},\quad i\geq a_ k. \]

We can then recursively choose an increasing sequence $(i_ k)_{k>0}$ in $I$ with $i_ k\geq a_ k$ and

\[ \int _{\{ \varphi _{i_ k}=0\} }\theta _{\varphi _{i_ k}}^ n>\int _{\{ \varphi =0\} }\theta _{\varphi }^ n+\delta . \]

This sequence converges to $\varphi $ in $L^1$, contradicting Step 1. $\square$

Next we introduce an envelope construction, which will be repeatedly used in the sequel.

Definition 2.4.12. Given a function $f\colon X\rightarrow [-\infty ,\infty ]$, we define $P_{\theta }(f)$ as follows:

2.31
\begin{equation} P_{\theta }(f)\coloneqq \operatorname *{sup}_{}{}\! {}^{*}\left\{ \varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ): \varphi \leq f\textup{ quasi-everywhere} \right\} \footnotemark . \end{equation}
Note. In the original article [DDNLsurv], the authors required that $\varphi \leq f$ everywhere. But in the proof of their Theorem 2.7 (theorem 2.4.14 below), they actually relied on the current definition. In the most relevant case, if $f$ is $\mathcal{F}$-upper semicontinuous (for example, if $f$ is upper semicontinuous), then these two definitions coincide. This is due to 1.3.17 and proposition 1.3.15.
The function $P_{\theta }(f)$ is either constantly $\pm \infty $ or lies in $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. Moreover, given another function $g\colon X\rightarrow [-\infty ,\infty ]$, equal to $f$ quasi-everywhere, we have $P_{\theta }(f)=P_{\theta }(g)$. In particular, it makes sense to talk about $P_{\theta }(f)$ even if $f$ is only defined outside a pluripolar set.

We also observe that

2.32
\begin{equation} P_{\theta }(f)\leq f\quad \textup{quasi-everywhere}. \end{equation}

This is a consequence of proposition 1.2.3 and proposition 1.2.5.

Lemma 2.4.13. Fix a closed smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\theta $ on $X$ representing a big cohomology class. Let $f\colon X\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$ be a lower semicontinuous function. Assume that $P_{\theta }(f)\not\equiv \infty $. Then $P_{\theta }(f)$ is locally bounded outside $\operatorname{nK}(\{ \theta \} )$. Set $U\coloneqq \{ P_{\theta }(f)<f\} \setminus \operatorname{nK}(\{ \theta \} )$, then

2.33
\begin{equation} \Bigl (\theta |_ U+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}P_{\theta }(f)|_ U\Bigr )^ n=0. \end{equation}

Proof. First observe that $V_{\theta }+\inf _ X f$ is a candidate in the definition (2.31). So

\[ P_{\theta }(f)\geq V_{\theta }+\inf _ X f, \]

and in particular $P_{\theta }(f)\not\equiv -\infty $.

In particular, $P_{\theta }(f)\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. It is therefore bounded from above. By 2.4.6, it is locally bounded away from $\operatorname{nK}(\{ \theta \} )$.

Now fix $x\in U$, and $\epsilon >0$ so that

\[ P_{\theta }(f)(x)<f(x)-\epsilon . \]

Take holomorphic balls $B\Subset B'\Subset U$ containing $x$. After shrinking $B$, we can guarantee that

2.34
\begin{equation} \left.P_{\theta }(f)\right|_{B}<f(x)-\epsilon ,\quad f|_ B> f(x)-\epsilon . \end{equation}

Let $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ be the balayage of $P_{\theta }(f)$ on $B$, as constructed in proposition 1.5.9. By the domination principle proposition 1.2.15, we find that

\[ \varphi |_{B}\leq f(x)-\epsilon <f|_{B}. \]

Since $\varphi =P_{\theta }(f)$ outside $B$, we conclude that

\[ \varphi \leq f\quad \textup{quasi-everywhere}. \]

From this it follows that $\varphi \leq P_{\theta }(f)$, and hence equality holds. But then (2.33) holds automatically on $B$ since $\varphi $ is constructed by balayage. Since $x$ and $B$ are arbitrary, we conclude (2.33). $\square$

Theorem 2.4.14. Let $f\colon X\rightarrow [-\infty ,\infty ]$ be a quasi-continuous function.

Assume that $P_{\theta }(f)\not\equiv \pm \infty $. Then $P_{\theta }(f)\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and

2.35
\begin{equation} \int _{\{ P_{\theta }(f)<f\} } \theta _{P_{\theta }(f)}^ n=0. \end{equation}

Thanks to (2.32), we can rewrite (2.35) as

\[ \int _{\{ P_{\theta }(f)\neq f\} } \theta _{P_{\theta }(f)}^ n=0. \]
We shall say that $\theta _{P_{\theta }(f)}^ n$ is carried by the contact set $\{ P_{\theta }(f)=f\} $.
Note. It is tempting to say that $\theta _{P_{\theta }(f)}^ n$ is supported on the contact set $\{ P_{\theta }(f)=f\} $, as people are already doing in the literature. It should be mentioned that this is an abuse of the language, since the support of $\theta _{P_{\theta }(f)}^ n$ (as a closed subset of $X$) could be much larger in general. One could probably introduce a notion of plurifine support, similar to what Fuglede did in the classical potential theory [Fug72].

Proof. Step 1. We first reduce to the case where $f$ is bounded.

Step 1.1. Reduce to the case where $f\leq 0$.

Take $C\in \mathbb {R}$ so that $P_{\theta }(f)\leq C$. Then

\[ P_{\theta }(f)=P_{\theta }\Bigl ( \min \{ f,C\} \Bigr ). \]

Thus we can replace $f$ by $\min \{ f,C\} $. Replacing $f$ by $f-\sup _ X f$, we reduce easily to the case where $f\leq 0$.

Step 1.2. Reduce to the case where $f$ is bounded. Assume that in this case, the theorem is known. We prove (2.35) for $f\leq 0$.

For each $j>0$, we have

2.36
\begin{equation} \int _{\{ P_{\theta }(f_ j)<f_ j\} } \theta _{P_{\theta }(f_ j)}^ n=0, \end{equation}

where $f_ j=f\lor (-j)$.

Now fix $C>0$ and two open sets $G'\Subset G\Subset X\setminus \operatorname{nK}(\{ \theta \} )$. Fix a smooth function $\chi \colon X\rightarrow [0,1]$ so that $\chi |_{G'}\equiv 1$ and $\chi $ is supported in $G$.

Define

\[ U_ C\coloneqq G \cap \left\{ P_{\theta }(f)>V_{\theta }-C \right\} ,\quad U'_ C\coloneqq G' \cap \left\{ P_{\theta }(f)>V_{\theta }-C \right\} . \]

Then $U_ C$ is $\mathcal{F}$-open. It follows from proposition 2.2.2 1 that for any $j>0$,

\[ \mathds {1}_{U_ C}\, \theta _{P_{\theta }(f_ j)\lor (V_{\theta }-C)}^ n=\mathds {1}_{U_ C}\, \theta _{P_{\theta }(f_ j)}^ n. \]

In particular, thanks to (2.36),

2.37
\begin{equation} \int _{U_ C} \left( 1-\mathrm{e}^{P_{\theta }(f_ j)-f_ j} \right)\, \theta _{P_{\theta }(f_ j)\lor (V_{\theta }-C)}^ n=0. \end{equation}

Note that the $P_{\theta }(f_ j)\lor (V_{\theta }-C)$'s for various $j$ are uniformly bounded from below on $U_ C$, thanks to 2.4.6.

Next we claim that $(P_{\theta }(f_ j))_ j$ is decreasing and converges to $P_{\theta }(f)$.

The sequence is decreasing; let us compute its limit. In fact, $P_{\theta }(f_ j)\leq f_ j$ quasi-everywhere. It follows that $\inf _ j P_{\theta }(f_ j)\leq f$ quasi-everywhere and hence

\[ \inf _{j>0} P_{\theta }(f_ j)\leq P_{\theta }(f). \]

The reverse inequality is trivial, and our assertion follows.

Since $P_{\theta }(f)\not\equiv -\infty $, we know that the set $\{ f=-\infty \} $ is pluripolar. It follows that $P_{\theta }(f_ j)-f_ j$ converges to the following function $g\colon X\rightarrow [-\infty ,\infty )$ in capacity:

\[ g(x)=\begin{cases} P_{\theta }(f)(x)-f(x),& \textup{if } f(x)\neq -\infty ;\\ -\infty ,& \textup{otherwise}. \end{cases} \]

Therefore,

\[ 1-\mathrm{e}^{P_{\theta }(f_ j)-f_ j}\xlongrightarrow {\mathrm{C}} 1-\mathrm{e}^{g}. \]

Next we claim that

2.40
\begin{equation} \int _{U'_ C} \left( 1-\mathrm{e}^{g} \right)\, \theta _{P_{\theta }(f)}^ n=0. \end{equation}

Since $g\leq 0$ quasi-everywhere, the left-hand side of (2.40) is non-negative. It suffices to prove the $\leq $ direction.

We wish to let $j\to \infty $ directly in (2.37), but since $U_ C$ is not open in general, this cannot be done directly. In the sequel, we shall slightly enlarge $U_ C$ to get an open set and then take the limit.

Fix $\epsilon >0$. We can find an open subset $W\Subset X\setminus \operatorname{nK}(\{ \theta \} )$ so that

2.41
\begin{equation} \operatorname{Cap}_{C^{-1}\theta }(W\setminus U_ C)<\epsilon . \end{equation}

For example, we can construct $W$ as follows. By example 2.3.5, we can find an open set $A\subseteq G$ so that $\operatorname{Cap}_{C^{-1}\theta }(A)<\epsilon $ and $P_{\theta }(f)-V_{\theta }$ is continuous on $G\setminus A$. Then $U_ C\cap (G\setminus A)$ is relatively open in $G\setminus A$, so we may choose an open set $W_0\subseteq G$ such that

\[ W_0\cap (G\setminus A)=U_ C\cap (G\setminus A). \]

Taking $W=W_0\cup A$, we get $U_ C\subseteq W$ and $W\setminus U_ C\subseteq A$, hence (2.41).

Thanks to (2.41) and (2.37), we have

\[ \int _ W \chi \left( 1-\mathrm{e}^{P_{\theta }(f_ j)-f_ j} \right)\, \theta _{P_{\theta }(f_ j)\lor (V_{\theta }-C)}^ n\leq C^ n\epsilon . \]

Letting $j\to \infty $ and applying theorem 2.3.8, we find that

\[ \int _{U'_ C} \left( 1-\mathrm{e}^{g} \right)\, \theta _{P_{\theta }(f)\lor (V_{\theta }-C)}^ n\leq \int _ W \chi \left( 1-\mathrm{e}^{g} \right)\, \theta _{P_{\theta }(f)\lor (V_{\theta }-C)}^ n\leq C^ n\epsilon . \]

Since $\epsilon >0$ is arbitrary, (2.40) follows.

Now letting $C\to \infty $ in (2.40), we find

\[ \int _{G'}\left( 1-\mathrm{e}^{g} \right)\, \theta _{P_{\theta }(f)}^ n=0. \]

Since $G'\Subset X\setminus \operatorname{nK}(\{ \theta \} )$ is arbitrary, and $\operatorname{nK}(\{ \theta \} )\subseteq X$ is a proper analytic subset, we finally conclude

\[ \int _ X \left( 1-\mathrm{e}^{g} \right)\, \theta _{P_{\theta }(f)}^ n=0. \]

In other words, $P_{\theta }(f)=f$ almost everywhere with respect to $\theta _{P_{\theta }(f)}^ n$. This proves (2.35).

Step 2. We now assume that $-C'\leq f\leq 0$ for some $C'>0$.

For each $j>0$, take an open subset $V_ j\subseteq X$ so that

  1. $\operatorname{Cap}_{C'^{-1}\theta }(V_ j)\leq 2^{-j-1}$, and

  2. $f|_{X\setminus V_ j}$ is continuous.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that the $V_ j$'s are decreasing. Take a continuous function $f_ j\colon X\rightarrow [-C',0]$ extending $f|_{X\setminus V_ j}$. This is always possible thanks to Tietze's extension theorem. For each $j>0$, we let

\[ h_ j\coloneqq \sup _{k\geq j} f_ k. \]

Then $h_ j$ is lower semicontinuous and $h_ j$ agrees with $f$ outside $V_ j$. Set

\[ h=\inf _{j>0} h_ j. \]

Then the set $\{ h\neq f\} $ is contained in the intersection of the $V_ j$'s and hence is a pluripolar set, thanks to theorem 2.3.4. In particular, $P_{\theta }(f)=P_{\theta }(h)$.

Now we can apply 2.4.13 to conclude that

\[ \int _{X\setminus \operatorname{nK}(\{ \theta \} )} \left(1-\mathrm{e}^{P_{\theta }(h_ j)-h_ j} \right) \theta _{P_{\theta }(h_ j)}^ n=0 \]

for each $j>0$.

Fix two open subsets $G'\Subset G\Subset X\setminus \operatorname{nK}(\{ \theta \} )$. Note that $-C'\leq h_ j\leq 0$. In particular,

\[ V_{\theta }-C'\leq P_{\theta }(h_ j)\leq V_{\theta }. \]

Hence,

\[ \begin{aligned} & \int _ G \left| 1-\mathrm{e}^{P_{\theta }(h_ j)-f}\right|\, \theta _{P_{\theta }(h_ j)}^ n\\ =& \int _{G\cap V_ j} \left|1-\mathrm{e}^{P_{\theta }(h_ j)-f}\right|\, \theta _{P_{\theta }(h_ j)}^ n+\int _{G\setminus V_ j} \left|1-\mathrm{e}^{P_{\theta }(h_ j)-h_ j}\right|\, \theta _{P_{\theta }(h_ j)}^ n\\ =& \int _{G\cap V_ j} \left|1-\mathrm{e}^{P_{\theta }(h_ j)-f}\right|\, \theta _{P_{\theta }(h_ j)}^ n\\ \leq & \int _{G\cap V_ j} \left( 1+\mathrm{e}^{C'}\right)\, \theta _{P_{\theta }(h_ j)}^ n\\ \leq & \left( 1+\mathrm{e}^{C'}\right) \cdot 2^{-j-1} C'^ n. \end{aligned} \]

Now we can apply the same arguments as in Step 1.2 to conclude that

\[ \int _{G'}\left(1-\mathrm{e}^{P_{\theta }(f)-f}\right)\, \theta _{P_{\theta }(f)}^ n=0. \]

Since $G'\Subset X\setminus \operatorname{nK}(\{ \theta \} )$, (2.35) follows. $\square$

The following lemma is striking in that we begin only with an upper bound of $\varphi $, but at the end of the day, we get a lower bound almost for free. This powerful method will be employed again and again in the whole book. A more general version will be proved in theorem 6.1.17, after introducing the terminology of $P$-preorder in chapter 6.

Lemma 2.4.15. Let $\varphi ,\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, $\varphi \preceq \psi $ and $\int _ X \theta _{\varphi }^ n>0$. Then for any

2.47
\begin{equation} a\in \left( 1, \left(\frac{\int _ X \theta _{\psi }^ n}{\int _ X \theta _{\psi }^ n-\int _ X \theta _{\varphi }^ n}\right)^{1/n} \right)\footnotemark , \end{equation}

there is $\eta \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )_{>0}$ such that

2.48
\begin{equation} a^{-1}\eta +\left(1-a^{-1}\right)\psi \leq \varphi . \end{equation}
Note. The fraction in (2.47) is understood as $\infty $ if either $\int _ X \theta _{\psi }^ n=\int _ X \theta _{\varphi }^ n$ or $n=0$. Thanks to the monotonicity theorem theorem 2.4.8, the interval (2.47) is non-empty.

We write

2.49
\begin{equation} \begin{aligned} P_{\theta }\Bigl (a\varphi +(1-a)\psi \Bigr )\coloneqq & \operatorname *{sup}_{}{}\! {}^{*}\left\{ \eta \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ): a^{-1}\eta +\left(1-a^{-1}\right)\psi \leq \varphi \right\} \\ \in & \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ). \end{aligned} \end{equation}

Note that if we regard $a\varphi +(1-a)\psi $ as a function defined outside the pluripolar set $\{ \varphi =\psi =-\infty \} $ on $X$, then (2.49) coincides with the envelope in the sense of (2.31).

Observe that

2.52
\begin{equation} a^{-1}P_{\theta }\Bigl (a\varphi +(1-a)\psi \Bigr )+(1-a^{-1})\psi \leq \varphi . \end{equation}

In fact, this equation holds outside a pluripolar set by proposition 1.2.5, hence it holds everywhere by proposition 1.2.6.

Finally, observe that

\[ P_{\theta }\Bigl (a\varphi +(1-a)\psi \Bigr )\preceq P_{\theta }\Bigl (a\varphi +(1-a)\varphi \Bigr )=\varphi . \]

If $\varphi \leq \psi $, the same argument gives

\[ P_{\theta }\Bigl (a\varphi +(1-a)\psi \Bigr )\leq \varphi . \]

Proof. Without loss of generality, we may assume that $\varphi \leq \psi \leq 0$.

Step 1. We show the existence of $\eta \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfying (2.48).

Step 1.1. We first consider the case where $\psi \sim \varphi $. In this case, we can take $\eta =\varphi -C$ enough $C>0$. But since by (2.52), we also have $P_{\theta }(a\varphi +(1-a)\psi )\leq \psi $, we find that

\[ P_{\theta }\Bigl (a\varphi +(1-a)\psi \Bigr )\sim \varphi \sim \psi \]

in this case.

Step 1.2. We make a first reduction of the problem.

Now for each $\gamma \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, we let

\[ P_{\theta }[\gamma ]\coloneqq \operatorname *{sup}_{}{}\! {}^{*}\Bigl \{ \eta \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ): \eta \leq \min \{ \gamma +C,0\} \textup{ for some }C>0 \Bigr \} \footnotemark . \]
Note. This is precisely the $P$-envelope to be introduced later in definition 3.1.4.

Observe that due to corollary 2.4.9 and theorem 2.4.8, we have

2.53
\begin{equation} \int _ X \theta _{P_{\theta }[\gamma ]}^ n=\int _ X \theta _{\gamma }^ n. \end{equation}

Further, $0\geq P_{\theta }[\gamma ]\geq \gamma $ if $\gamma \leq 0$.

We now define an increasing transfinite sequence $(\psi _{\alpha })_{\alpha }$ consisting of non-positive functions in $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ as follows:

  1. $\psi _0=\psi $;

  2. $\psi _{\alpha +1}=P_{\theta }[\psi _{\alpha }]$;

  3. if $\alpha $ is a limit ordinal, we define

    \[ \psi _{\alpha }=\operatorname *{sup}_{\beta <\alpha }{}\! {}^{*} \psi _{\beta }. \]

Observe that

\[ \int _ X \theta _{\psi _{\alpha }}^ n=\int _ X \theta _{\psi }^ n \]

by corollary 2.4.9 and (2.53). Since $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ is indeed a set, the transfinite sequence must stabilize, say at some $\phi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. Note that $0 \geq \phi \geq \psi $, and $P_{\theta }[\phi ]=\phi $.

Note that the interval (2.47) remains the same if we replace $\psi $ by $\phi $. Take $a$ in this interval, it suffices to prove the existence of $\eta \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ so that

2.54
\begin{equation} a^{-1}\eta +\left(1-a^{-1}\right)\phi \leq \varphi . \end{equation}

Let us record the following observation for later use. Suppose that $\tau \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and $\tau \preceq \phi $. Then

2.55
\begin{equation} \sup _{\{ \phi \neq -\infty \} } (\tau -\phi )=\sup _ X \tau . \end{equation}

Observe that $\phi \leq 0$, so on the set $\{ \phi \neq -\infty \} $, we have

\[ \tau -\phi \geq \tau . \]

So the $\geq $ direction in (2.55) follows from corollary 1.3.18. Conversely, by assumption, we can find a constant $C>0$ so that

\[ \tau -\sup _ X \tau \leq \min \{ \phi +C,0 \} . \]

It follows that $\tau -\sup _ X \tau \leq P_{\theta }[\phi ]=\phi $. Therefore, the reverse inequality follows, and (2.55) follows.

For each $k>0$, we introduce

\[ \varphi _ k=\varphi \lor \left(\phi -k\right),\quad \eta _ k\coloneqq P_{\theta }\Bigl ( a \varphi _ k+(1-a)\phi \Bigr ). \]

Since $\varphi _ k\sim \phi $, using Step 1.1, we have $\eta _ k\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and $\eta _ k\sim \phi $ as well.

Note that $(\eta _ k)_ k$ is decreasing in $k$. Let

\[ \eta \coloneqq \inf _{k\in \mathbb {N}} \eta _ k. \]

Note that $\eta $ automatically satisfies (2.54). It remains to show that $\eta \not\equiv -\infty $.

Step 1.3. We prove that $\eta \not\equiv -\infty $.

Suppose, for contradiction, that $\sup _ X \eta _ k\to -\infty $. For each $k>0$, let

\[ \gamma _ k\coloneqq a^{-1}\eta _ k+\left(1-a^{-1}\right)\phi ,\quad D_ k\coloneqq \left\{ \gamma _ k=\varphi _ k\right\} . \]

We claim that

2.56
\begin{equation} \theta _{\eta _ k}^ n\leq a^ n\mathds {1}_{D_ k}\, \theta _{\varphi _ k}^ n. \end{equation}

Since $\gamma _ k\leq \varphi _ k$ with equality on $D_ k$, 2.4.10 gives

\[ \mathds {1}_{D_ k}\, \theta _{\gamma _ k}^ n\leq \mathds {1}_{D_ k}\, \theta _{\varphi _ k}^ n. \]

On the other hand,

\[ \theta _{\gamma _ k}=a^{-1}\theta _{\eta _ k}+\left(1-a^{-1}\right)\theta _{\phi }, \]

so positivity and multilinearity of the non-pluripolar product imply

\[ a^{-n}\theta _{\eta _ k}^ n\leq \theta _{\gamma _ k}^ n. \]

Finally, it follows from theorem 2.4.14 that

\[ \mathds {1}_{D_ k}\, \theta _{\eta _ k}^ n=\theta _{\eta _ k}^ n. \]

Putting these results together, we conclude (2.56).

Fix $j>k>0$. Note that

2.57
\begin{equation} \int _{\{ \varphi \leq \phi -k \} } \theta _{\varphi _ j}^ n=\int _ X \theta _{\varphi _ j}^ n-\int _{\{ \varphi >\phi -k \} } \theta _{\varphi _ j}^ n=\int _ X \theta _{\phi }^ n-\int _{\{ \varphi >\phi -k \} } \theta _{\varphi }^ n, \end{equation}

where we applied theorem 2.4.8 and proposition 2.2.2 1 in the second equality.

Next, we compute

\[ \begin{aligned} \int _{\left\{ \eta _ j\leq \phi -ak \right\} } \theta _{\eta _ j}^ n\leq & a^ n \int _{\left\{ \eta _ j\leq \phi -ak \right\} } \mathds {1}_{D_ j}\, \theta _{\varphi _ j}^ n & \textup{by (\href{../../tag/87D0AE27AE70/}{2.56})}\\ \leq & a^ n \int _{\left\{ a\varphi _ j+(1-a)\phi \leq \phi -ak \right\} \cap \left\{ \phi \neq -\infty \right\} } \theta _{\varphi _ j}^ n\\ = & a^ n \int _{\left\{ \varphi _ j\leq \phi -k \right\} } \theta _{\varphi _ j}^ n\\ = & a^ n \int _{\left\{ \varphi \leq \phi -k \right\} } \theta _{\varphi _ j}^ n\\ \leq & a^ n\left( \int _ X \theta _{\phi }^ n- \int _{\left\{ \varphi >\phi -k \right\} } \theta _{\varphi }^ n \right)& \textup{ by (\href{../../tag/2263D1F9CA2F/}{2.57})}. \end{aligned} \]
